Geometric Constructions of Mod p p Cohomology Operations
Herng Yi Cheng

Abstract
The Brown Representability Theorem implies that cohomology operations can be represented by continuous maps between Eilenberg-Maclane spaces. These Eilenberg-Maclane spaces have explicit geometric models as spaces of cycles on round spheres and spaces of relative cycles on unit disks, due to the Almgren Isomorphism Theorem. A. Nabutovsky asked what maps between spaces of cycles represent the Steenrod squares. In this work we answer this question by constructing maps with explicit formulas from spaces of cycles on spheres to spaces of relative cycles on disks that represent all Steenrod squares, as well as all Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms on mod p p cohomology, for all primes p p .



1
Introduction

In this work we will give explicit geometric constructions that represent mod
p
cohomology operations, chief among them the Steenrod powers and the Bockstein homomorphisms.

For the rest of this article, let
p
be a given prime, and regard all homology and cohomology to be in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
coefficients unless otherwise indicated. By a
mod
p
cohomology operation
we mean, for some fixed
m,n\geq 0
, a family of homomorphisms
\theta_{X}:H^{m}(X)\to H^{n}(X)
for each space
X
which are natural with respect to pullbacks along continuous maps between spaces. The cup powers
\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{p}
are immediate examples of mod
p
cohomology operations. Other examples include the
Bockstein homomorphisms

\beta:H^{n}(X)\to H^{n+1}(X)
for each
n\geq 1
, which are defined as the connecting homomorphisms in the long exact sequence of cohomology groups that arises from the short exact sequence of coefficient groups
0\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to 0
. Basically, the Bockstein homomorphisms answers the following question about each mod
p
cocycle: if we treat it as a mod
p^{2}
cochain, how many times is its coboundary divisible by
p
?

One final important family of mod
p
cohomology operations are the
Steenrod powers

P^{i}:H^{n}(X)\to H^{n+2i(p-1)}(X)
for
n,i\geq 0
, which were introduced by N. E. Steenrod
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
. Note that
P^{0}=\id
. When
p=2
,
P^{i}
and
\beta\circ P^{i}
are called
Steenrod squares
and are written using an alternate notation as
\mathit{Sq}^{2i}
and
\mathit{Sq}^{2i+1}
respectively. As a result,
\mathit{Sq}^{1}=\beta
. The Steenrod powers are characterized by a list of algebraic axioms as explained in
[
22
, Section 4.L]
, and can be constructed using the equivariant cohomology of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
group actions, which we explain in
section

7.1
. On the other hand, it has been challenging to provide geometric intuition for them. When
X
is a closed manifold and
\alpha\in H^{n}(X)
has its Poincaré dual represented by an embedded submanifold
\Sigma\subset X
, in some sense
P^{i}(\alpha)
detects twisting in the normal bundle of
\Sigma
.

The Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms generate the mod
p
cohomology operations, in the sense that every such operation is the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear combination of cup products of compositions of Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms. Thus we may restrict our study to the Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms.

1.1
Brown representability and the main theorems

To find geometric representations of the Bockstein homomorphisms and Steenrod powers, our starting point is the
Brown Representability Theorem
, which gives isomorphisms
H^{n}(X)\cong[X,K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)]
, where
X
is a cell complex,
[X,Y]
consists of the homotopy classes of pointed continuous maps
X\to Y
for some choice of basepoints on
X
and
Y
, and
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
denotes an Eilenberg-Maclane space. Under this isomorphism, each pointed continuous map
a:X\to K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
corresponds to
a^{*}(\iota_{n})
, where
\iota_{n}\in H^{n}(K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n))
is the
fundamental cohomology class
. Inspired by this, we will geometrically represent
\alpha\in H^{n}(X)
by choosing a space
Y\sim_{w}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
, where
\sim_{w}
denotes weak homotopy equivalence, so that
Y
has an explicit geometry. (In contrast, even though
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
can always be represented by a cell complex, the number of cells it has in each dimension is not even precisely known.) Then we will construct a “geometrically nice” continuous map
a:X\to Y
such that
a^{*}(\iota_{n})=\alpha
. (Since
Y\sim_{w}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
,
\iota_{n}
may also be considered as the fundamental cohomology class of
Y
.) Such a map
a:X\to Y
will be called a
Brown representative
for
\alpha
.

Our choice for
Y
comes from the field of Geometric Measure Theory: when
M
is a compact Riemannian manifold with a submanifold
N
, let
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
denote the space of
mod
p
integral (relative)
k
-cycles in
M
.
Intuitively, the elements of this space can be thought of as “limits” of mod
p
singular chains in
M
whose singular simplices are Lipschitz, and whose boundaries are supported in
N
. We will write
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
to mean
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,\emptyset)
. This set can be given several natural topologies. A common one is the
flat topology
which is induced by the
flat metric

\mathcal{F}
, where
\mathcal{F}(S,T)
is roughly the area of the smallest “filling” of
S-T
. (By a filling of
S-T
, we mean a chain whose boundary is
S-T
.) However, the maps between spaces of cycles that we will construct, including the Cartesian product map
T\mapsto T\times T
, will not be continuous in the flat topology. For this reason, we will instead use the
inductive limit topology

[
5
, (1.9)]
, which is a refinement of the flat topology.

These spaces of cycles have a group structure akin to that of the groups of singular cycles, and as a result they are weakly homotopy equivalent to products of Eilenberg-Maclane spaces
[
22
, Corollary 4K.7]
. In fact, we will prove the weak homotopy equivalences
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)\sim_{w}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})\sim_{w}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+k},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+k})
for all
k\geq 0
. More generally, the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,\partial M))\cong H_{i+k}(M,\partial M)

\displaystyle\text{for all }k\geq 0\text{ and }i\geq 1.

(1.1)

This is an analogue of the
Almgren Isomorphism Theorem
, the same statement for similar spaces of cycles
[
5
,
20
]
. It can be viewed as a generalization of the Dold-Thom Theorem. Thus we may find Brown representatives of cohomology classes which are nice maps to spaces of cycles, where the degree of the class is equal to the codimension of the cycle in the ambient sphere or disk.

Remark 1.1
.

Equation

1.1
follows from the Almgren Isomorphism Theorem for spaces of cycles with the flat topology and the fact that on such a space of cycles, the inductive limit topology is weakly homotopy equivalent to the flat topology. This was stated in
[
5
]
for spaces of cycles with
\mathbb{Z}
coefficients, but we have not been able to find a proof in the literature. Nevertheless, these weak homotopy equivalences follow immediately from the approximation theorems in
[
20
]
, which allow continuous families of cycles parametrized by compact sets to be homotoped to families of cycles that are bounded in mass.

Consider the following examples of Brown representatives for the generators
H^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and
H^{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{2})
, for
p=2
. The former is a map
a:\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
whose formula, if we parametrize
\mathbb{S}^{1}
as
[-1,1]
, is
a(t)=\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
. (It is well-defined because
a(-1)
and
a(1)
are both points, which are equal to zero as 1-cycles.) It gives a 1-parameter family of circles that “sweep out”
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, starting from 0 and ending at 0 (see
fig.

1
(a)). The latter is a map
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{3},\partial\mathbb{D}^{3})
. If
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
is considered as the space of lines through the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then the map has formula
\ell\mapsto\ell^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{D}^{3}
(see
fig.

1
(b)–(c)).

(a)

(b)               (c)

Figure 1:
(Suppose that
p=2
.) (a) A Brown representative
a:\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
of the generator of
H^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, where
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is parametrized as
[-1,1]
. (b)
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
can be visualized as the upper hemisphere of
S^{2}
, with antipodal points on the equator identified. Each point on the upper hemisphere corresponds to a line
\ell
through the origin. (c) A Brown representative of the generator of
H^{1}(\mathbb{RP}2)
sends
\ell\in\mathbb{RP}^{2}
to
\ell^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{D}^{3}\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{3},\partial\mathbb{D}^{3})
.

Going a step further, the Brown representability theorem also implies that the set of mod
p
cohomology operations
H^{m}(-)\to H^{n}(-)
bijects with
[K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m),K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)]
. A map
f:K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)\to K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
corresponds to the cohomology operation
\phi
so that applying
\phi
corresponds to composing with
f
: if a class
\alpha\in H^{m}(X)
has Brown representative
a:X\to K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)
, then
\phi(\alpha)\in H^{n}(X)
has Brown representative
f\circ a
. This is equivalent to the identity
f^{*}(\iota_{n})=\phi(\iota_{m})
. In this work, we have geometrically represented the cohomology operation
\phi:H^{m}(-)\to H^{n}(-)
, where
\phi
could be a Bockstein homomorphism or a Steenrod power, by choosing spaces of cycles
Y\sim_{w}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)
and
Y^{\prime}\sim_{w}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
and constructing geometrically nice maps
f:Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
f^{*}(\iota_{n})=\phi(\iota_{m})
. We call
f
a
Brown representative for
\phi
.

We have constructed Brown representatives for the Bockstein homomorphisms as follows. The domains of these maps are
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, which consists of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear combinations of points in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
whose coefficients should sum to zero. (This condition makes it a connected space.) The codomains are
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
, which consist of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear combinations of points in
\mathbb{D}^{n}
, but with no further restrictions on their coefficients. As these are cycles relative to the boundary, any points on the boundary are considered to vanish. For every set
X
,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts on the Cartesian power
X^{p}
by cyclic permutations which are generated by
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})\mapsto(x_{2},\dotsc,x_{p},x_{1})
. Let
X^{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
denote the quotient by this action.

Theorem 1.2
.

For each
n\geq 1
, the Bockstein homomorphism
\beta:H^{n}(-)\to H^{n+1}(-)
has a Brown representative

\displaystyle b:\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})

(1.2)

\displaystyle b(x_{1}+\dotsb+x_{k})=\sum_{[(i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p})]\in\{1,\dotsc,k\}^{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\frac{x_{i_{1}}+\dotsb+x_{i_{p}}}{p},

where each
x_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
and the fraction denotes the barycenter of
x_{i_{1}},\dotsc,x_{i_{p}}
, considered as points in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

In particular, when
p=2
,
b(x_{1}+\dotsb+x_{k})
is the sum of the midpoints of every unordered pair
\{x_{i},x_{j}\}
.

An example for
b
when
n=1
are illustrated for
p=2
in
fig.

2
(a)–(b), which shows how it computes a sum of midpoints. A similar example for
n=1
and
p=3
in
fig.

2
(c)–(d) requires more explanation. When the input to
b
is a mod 3 cycle
x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}
(see
fig.

2
(c)), the sum in
eq.

1.2
is indexed by the elements of
\{1,2,3\}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, which are 3-tuples of indices with cyclic permutations identified. One representative from each equivalence class is listed below:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}(1,1,1)&(1,1,2)&(1,2,2)&(1,2,3)\\
(2,2,2)&(2,2,3)&(2,3,3)&(3,2,1)\\
(3,3,3)&(3,3,1)&(3,1,1)\end{array}

For each
(i,j,k)
listed above, the output of
b
contains the point
\frac{1}{3}(x_{i}+x_{j}+x_{k})
. The points corresponding to
(i,i,i)
lie on the boundary and vanish. Both
(1,2,3)
and
(3,2,1)
correspond to the point
\frac{1}{3}(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3})
, so that point has multiplicity 2. The points in the resulting mod 3 0-cycle trisect the edges of the triangle
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
, and also mark its barycenter (see
fig.

2
(d)).

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 2:
Brown representatives
b:\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2})
of the Bockstein homomorphism
\beta:H^{1}(-)\to H^{2}(-)
for
p=2
in (a)–(b) and
p=3
in (c)–(d). (a) The mod 2 0-cycle
x_{1}+\dotsb+x_{4}
shown as red points. (b)
b(x_{1}+\dotsb+x_{4})
shown as red points, where
(ij)
denotes the midpoint
\frac{1}{2}(x_{1}+x_{j})
. (c) The mod 3 0-cycle
x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}
shown as red points. (b)
b(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3})
shown as red points, where
(ijk)
denotes the barycenter
\frac{1}{3}(x_{1}+x_{j}+x_{k})
. The point
(123)=(321)
has multiplicity 2.

The action of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
by cyclic permutations is fundamental to the construction of the Steenrod powers
[
32
]
, so naturally it also plays a central role in the Brown representatives that we construct for Steenrod powers. Roughly speaking, every Brown representative of a Steenrod power and Bockstein homomorphism whose domain is
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is encapsulated in a single
cyclic product map
, denoted by
\mathit{cyc}
, that takes a cycle
T\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and returns
T^{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Brown representatives for individual Steenrod powers
P^{i}
and the Bockstein homomorphism can be obtained by composing
\mathit{cyc}
with other maps.

To explain
\mathit{cyc}
in more detail, observe that there is an inclusion
(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{p}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}
where
\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}
is viewed as a sphere of radius
\sqrt{p}
. Since
\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})^{p}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts on it by cyclic permutations, with fixed points the diagonal
\Delta=\{(x,\dotsc,x):x\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\}
. Deleting the diagonal from
\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}
and then taking the quotient by the action yields a familiar space: there is a homeomorphism

\displaystyle h:(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}\setminus\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to L_{n}\times\itr\mathbb{D}^{n+1}

\displaystyle\text{where }L_{n}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{RP}^{(p-1)(n+1)-1}&p=2\\
\mathbb{L}_{p}((1,\dotsc,\frac{p-1}{2})^{n+1})&p>2,\end{cases}

(1.3)

and
\itr
denotes the interior.
L_{n}
is a general lens space specified with the notation of
[
22
, p. 144]
. The notation
(1,\dotsc,\frac{p-1}{2})^{n+1}
simply means that the tuple of indices
(1,\dotsc,\frac{p-1}{2})
is repeated
n+1
times.

Theorem 1.3
.

For each prime
p
,
i\geq 0
, and
0\leq k<n
. Let
m=n-k
. Then there exists a cyclic product map,

\displaystyle\mathit{cyc}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathit{cyc}(T)=\overline{h((T^{p}\setminus\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{p})},

from which Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms may be derived. More precisely,
P^{i}:H^{m}(-)\to H^{m+2i(p-1)}(-)
and
\beta\circ P^{i}:H^{m}(-)\to H^{m+2i(p-1)+1}(-)
have Brown representatives

\displaystyle T\mapsto\bigcup_{x\in\mathit{cyc}(T)}g_{pk+m+2i(p-1)}(x)

and

\displaystyle T\mapsto\bigcup_{x\in\mathit{cyc}(T)}g_{pk+m+2i(p-1)+1}(x)

respectively,

where each
g_{q}:L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{a}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{a+q},\partial\mathbb{D}^{a+q})
is a Brown representative for a generator of
H^{q}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
for some
a\geq 0
that may depend on
q
.

(The maps
g_{q}
have explicit formulas, as explained in
remark

5.13
.)

Remark 1.4
.

By a Brown representative of a generator of
H^{q}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
, we mean a Brown representative of the corresponding cohomology generator of the quotient of
L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
by its boundary. This quotient is the Thom space of a trivial real vector bundle over
L_{n}
, and the Brown representative can be chosen in the form of a map from
L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
to a space of cycles, so that the map vanishes over the boundary.

Remark 1.5
.

The map
h
is induced by multiplying with a
p(n+1)\times p(n+1)
matrix which is a real analogue of a Discrete Fourier Transform matrix. This arises from the fact that the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})^{p}
gives a representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
which splits into a direct sum of
n+1
trivial representations and, when
p
is odd,
\frac{p-1}{2}(n+1)
2-dimensional representations. Each 2-dimensional representation is a rotation by a multiple of
2\pi/p
. The trivial representations correspond to the
\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
factor, while the other representations correspond to the lens space factor.

Remark 1.6
.

For simplicity, the formulas in
theorem

1.3
manipulate currents as thoough they were sets, by identifying currents with their supports. Strictly speaking, the operations of unions and closure are not well-defined on currents. This theorem has been stated rigorously in
theorem

7.1
.

Let us illustrate the map
\mathit{cyc}
for
p=2
,
k=0
and
n=1
. In this case, we have
\mathit{cyc}:\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{RP}^{1}\times\mathbb{D}^{2},\mathbb{RP}^{1}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{2})
. Suppose that the input to
\mathit{cyc}
is a 0-cycle in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
made of the vertices of a square (see
fig.

3
(a)). Then if we consider
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
to be the space of lines through the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the output of
\mathit{cyc}
will be
\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}(\ell_{ij},\frac{1}{2}(x_{i}+x_{j}))
, where each
x_{i}
is treated as a point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\ell_{ij}=\vspan\{x_{i}-x_{j}\}
(see
fig.

3
(b)–(c)).

(a)

(b)

(c)

\mathit{cyc}
for the total Steenrod power, when
p=2
,
k=0
, and

. The input 0-cycle
x_{1}+\dotsb+x_{4}
, made of the vertices of a square, is shown as red points in (a). The output
\mathit{cyc}(x_{1}+\dotsb+x_{4})
is a sum of pairs

is a line in

\frac{1}{2}(x_{i}+x_{j})
is a point in
\mathbb{D}^{2}
that is shown in red and labeled as
(ij)
in (c).

When
p=2
, the formulas for the Brown representatives simplify further:

Theorem 1.7
.

When
p=2
, there exists a choice for the
g_{q}
’s in
theorem

1.3
that gives the following Brown representative
\mathit{sq}^{i}
of
\mathit{Sq}^{i}:H^{m}(-)\to H^{m+i}(-)
:

\displaystyle\mathit{sq}^{i}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{2k+n(k+i-1)}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)},\partial\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)})

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathit{sq}^{i}(T)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x,y)\in(T^{2}\setminus\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\ell=\vspan\{x-y\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\end{subarray}}\left((\ell^{\perp})^{k+i-1}\times\left\{\frac{x+y}{2}\right\}\right)\cap\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)}.

In particular, when
T
is a “planar cycle,” i.e.
T=V\cap\mathbb{S}^{n}
for some
(k+1)
-dimensional affine subspace
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, then
eq.

1.5
simplifies to

\mathit{sq}^{i}(V\cap\mathbb{S}^{n})=\bigcup_{\ell=\text{line through origin parallel to }V}\left((\ell^{\perp})^{k+i-1}\times(\ell^{\perp}\cap V)\right)\cap\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)}.

(1.6)

Let us illustrate the map
\mathit{sq}^{i}
for planar cycles when
k=1
,
n=2
, and
i\geq 0
. Suppose that
V
is a plane in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that is parallel to the
xz
-plane and intersects
\mathbb{S}^{2}
in a circle (see
fig.

4
)(a)). Then
\mathit{sq}^{i}(V\cap\mathbb{S}^{2})
is the union of a family of linear subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)(k+i)}
(intersected with
\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)}
). The family is parametrized by lines
\ell
through the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that are parallel to
V
. When
\ell
is the
z
-axis (see
fig.

4
)(a)), the corresponding linear subspace is the Cartesian product of
k+i-1
copies of
\ell^{\perp}
, namely the
xy
-plane, and a copy of
\ell^{\perp}\cap V
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 4:
A Brown representative
\mathit{sq}^{i}
for
\mathit{Sq}^{i}
, when
p=2
,
k=1
,
n=2
, and
i\geq 0
. If the input is a planar cycle
V\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
as shown in (a), then the output is a union, over lines
\ell\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
through the origin and parallel to
V
, of a Cartesian product of copies of
\ell^{\perp}
and a copy of
V
(and then intersected with
\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)}
), as shown in (b).

1.2
Main technical challenges

Actually, to even state
theorems

1.2
,
1.3
and
1.7
rigorously, we must overcome many technical difficulties related to Geometric Measure Theory. Overcoming these obstacles will take a large fraction of this work. The formula in
eq.

1.4
is intuitively written using familiar operations from point-set topology like set difference and closure, but strictly speaking those operations are not defined for flat cycles.
A priori
, the “closure” of the current
h(T^{p}\setminus\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
may not be well-defined as its mass may blow up near the boundary of the ambient space
L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. We will construct the map
\mathit{cyc}
rigorously in
section

6
using the Compactness Theorem for integral chains and the Federer-Fleming Deformation Theorem, and prove that the mass does not blow up.

After constructing
\mathit{cyc}
, we proved that
\mathit{cyc}
is continuous. Our proof is related to a question about an equivariant version of the Isoperimetric Inequality:
Suppose that a group
G
acts on a manifold
X
. If a cycle
T
in
X
is
G
-invariant, does it have a filling (that is, a chain bounded by
T
) that is
G
-invariant and whose mass is bounded in terms of
\mathbf{M}(T)
?
In our situation, suppose that we ignore the homeomorphism
h
in
eq.

1.4
for simplicity. Then the fact that the flat metric is related to areas of fillings implies that to prove that
\mathit{cyc}
is continuous at zero, we need to show that whenever
T\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
has a filling of small mass
\mu
, the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant cycle
T^{p}\in\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1})
must have a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant filling of mass bounded in terms of
\mu
and
\mathbf{M}(T)
.

The Brown representatives for the individual Steenrod powers
P^{i}
and Bockstein homomorphisms from
theorem

1.3
are formulated as maps that send a cycle
T
to the union of a family of cycles
g_{q}(x)\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{a+q},\partial\mathbb{D}^{a+q})
that is parametrized by points
x
in a
pk
-dimensional cycle
\mathit{cyc}(T)
. This is also why unions appear in
theorem

1.7
. Implicitly, this definition treats the union of a
pk
-dimensional family of
a
-cycles as a
(pk+a)
-cycle. Technically, this does not make sense as currents cannot be treated the same as their supports. Furthermore, even if we treated currents the same as their supports, it is not clear
a priori
that when
g_{q}
is an arbitrary continuous family of cycles, the union of the supports of currents in this family is the support of some mod
p
integral cycle. This is because each
\spt g_{q}(x)
may be rectifiable, but
\bigcup_{x}\spt g_{q}(x)
may not be rectifiable. We will define a well-behaved class of families of cycles called
piecewise smooth families
so that this kind of union is rectifiable, and formalize this notion of unions of families of cycles into
gluings of families of cycles that are parametrized by cycles.

Gluings will serve as a key foundational tool to formalize and prove our main theorems. We will prove that every continuous family of cycles is homotopic to an arbitrarily close family that can be “glued” in the aforementioned sense. Our proof adapts the arguments of L. Guth and Y. Liokumovich in their approximation theorems which they used to provide an alternative proof of the Almgren Isomorphism Theorem for spaces of cycles with the flat topology
[
20
]
. Their approximation theorems have been extended by B. Staffa in
[
28
]
. The alternative proof by Guth and Liokumovich of the Almgren Isomorphism Theorem also used a discrete version of gluing that is well-defined only at the level of homology: to glue a family
g(x)
of
k
-cycles parametrized by points
x
in a
l
-cycle
T
, they choose finitely many points
x_{i}
that are finely distributed in
\spt T
, construct a
(k+l)
-cycle that is an “approximate gluing” of the finitely many cycles
g(x_{i})
, and prove that the homology class of the approximate gluing does not depend on the choice of points
x_{i}
. However, as we are constructing maps between spaces of cycles, we needed to develop our more general notion of gluing so that families of cycles are glued into cycles that are well-defined as-is, rather than only at the level of homology.

1.3
Proving
theorem

1.3
using gluing

The Steenrod powers are intimately related to the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
by cyclic permutation on the
p
-fold smash products
X^{\wedge p}
, for cell complexes
X
. Indeed, Steenrod showed how the Steenrod powers arise from
equivariant cohomology
, namely the cohomology groups of a homotopy-theoretic analogue of the quotient
X^{\wedge p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}

[
32
]
. More precisely,
X^{\wedge p}
is homotopy equivalent to
\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times X^{\wedge p}
, on which there is a free action of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let
\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}
denote the quotient by this action. Then for any
\alpha\in H^{m}(X)
, there exists a class
\Gamma(\alpha)\in H^{pm}(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p})
from which
\beta(\alpha)
and every
P^{i}(\alpha)
can be derived; this is explained in detail in
section

7.1
.

To prove
theorem

1.3
, we started by finding Brown representatives for
\Gamma(\iota_{m})
, from which we derived Brown representatives for
\beta(\iota_{m})
and
P^{i}(\iota_{m})
. One of our key ideas is that if
X=\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, where
n-k=m
, then a Brown representative for
\Gamma(\iota_{m})
can be obtained using gluing from a Brown representative for
\Gamma(\alpha)
for a generator
\alpha\in H^{n}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. More precisely,
\Gamma(\alpha)
must have some Brown representative
f:\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pn})
. Then we can define the following Brown representative for
\Gamma(\iota_{m})
:

\displaystyle F:\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pn})

(1.7)

\displaystyle F(t,T_{1},\dotsc,T_{p})=\bigcup_{x\in\{t\}\times(T_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge T_{p})}f(x).

Even though
f
is not known explicitly, the formula in
eq.

1.7
makes
F
compatible with the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
action in a way that allows us to relate
F
to the map
\mathit{cyc}
.

1.4
Proving the Almgren Isomorphism Theorem using gluing

We have also used gluings to provide an alternative proof of
eq.

1.1
, which we call the
Almgren Isomorphism Theorem for mod
p
cycles with the inductive limit topology
. Our strategy was basically to verify the Eilenberg-Steenrod Axioms for a homology theory, in the spirit of standard proofs of the Dold-Thom Theorem. For instance, to verify the Exactness Axiom, we demonstrated the existence of quasifibrations
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\partial M)\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M)\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,\partial M)
by using our technical tool of gluing to prove a weak version of the homotopy lifting property. These quasifibrations yield the necessary long exact sequences:

\dotsb\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\partial M))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,\partial M))\to\dotsb

In this way, our proof introduces a homotopy-theoretic perspective to the Almgren Isomorphism Theorem, in contrast to the more direct proofs of the known cases of the theorem which focused more explicitly on how the geometry of the families of cycles relates to the geometry of homology classes.

1.5
Earlier geometric representations of cohomology classes and operations

Maps from a simplicial complex
X
to
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
(with the flat topology) that represent a nontrivial cohomology class of
X
have been studied in other contexts along with other similar objects under the name of
sweepouts

[
4
,
16
,
14
,
18
]
. The name evokes a family of cycles, parametrized by
X
, that “sweeps through” every point in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
or
\mathbb{D}^{n}
, such as in
fig.

1
(a). In particular, given a particular chain of compositions of Steenrod squares
\mathit{Sq}^{i_{1}}\dotsm\mathit{Sq}^{i_{q}}
that does not vanish, Guth inductively constructed cell complexes
X
and maps from
X
to spaces of cycles that are Brown representatives of a nonzero class
\mathit{Sq}^{i_{1}}\dotsm\mathit{Sq}^{i_{q}}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in H^{*}(X)

[
18
]
. Sweepouts have been applied in the context of Almgren-Pitts min-max theory to prove the existence of minimal submanifolds, study their geometry, and prove several related conjectures.
1
1

1

A survey of some early applications of sweepouts and Almgren-Pitts min-max theory is available in
[
7
]
.

H. B. Lawson had constructed Brown representatives of cohomology classes as maps to spaces of
complex algebraic
k
-cycles
in
\mathbb{CP}^{n}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{2k}^{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})
(the cycles have real dimension
2k
). He proved that
\mathcal{Z}_{2k}^{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})\sim_{w}K(\mathbb{Z},2)\times K(\mathbb{Z},4)\times\dotsb\times K(\mathbb{Z},2n-2k)
. He also represented the total Chern class of the complex Grassmannian
\Gr_{\mathbb{C}}(n-k,n+1)
by the map
f:\Gr_{\mathbb{C}}(n-k,n+1)\to\mathcal{Z}_{2k}^{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})
which sends each complex
(n-k)
-plane to its complex orthogonal complement, considered as an algebraic cycle
[
24
]
. The total Chern class is equal to
f^{*}(\iota_{2}\otimes\iota_{4}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\iota_{2n-2k})
, where
\otimes
denotes the cross product. T. K. Lam defined a similar map that represents the total Stiefel-Whitney class of real Grassmannians
[
23
]
. Our example for
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
(
fig.

1
(b)–(c)) can be derived from that map.

The fact that homology classes are geometrically represented by cycles have led several authors to formulate geometric constructions of the
Steenrod homology operations

\mathit{Sq}_{i}:H_{n+i}(-)\to H_{n}(-)
on mod 2 homology groups. The
\mathit{Sq}_{i}
are dual to the Steenrod squares, in the sense that evaluating
\mathit{Sq}^{i}(\alpha)
on a mod 2 homology class
\beta
is the same as evaluating
\alpha
on
\mathit{Sq}_{i}(\beta)
. M.-L. Michelson considered the infinite symmetric product of an even-dimensional sphere,
\SP(\mathbb{S}^{2n})
, which is homotopy equivalent to
K(\mathbb{Z},2n)
by the Dold-Thom theorem. For each
0\leq k\leq n
they constructed an explicit integral
2(n+k)
-cycle in
\SP(\mathbb{S}^{2n})
whose homology class reduced mod 2, namely
\alpha\in H_{2(n+k)}(\SP(\mathbb{S}^{2n}))
, satisfies
\mathit{Sq}_{2k}(\alpha)\neq 0

[
26
]
. Analogues of
\mathit{Sq}_{i}
have also been constructed for algebraic varieties using intersection theory and its extensions by several authors (see
[
12
, p. 377]
and
[
6
]
). R. M. Hardt and C. G. McCrory represented
\mathit{Sq}_{i}
for a space
X
using the double points of certain maps from cycles to Euclidean space
[
25
,
21
]
. P. F. dos Santos and P. Lima-Filho encoded the Steenrod squares in
RO(C_{2})
-graded equivariant cohomology using a restriction map to the fixed points of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
[
9
]
.

We are not aware of any prior research on geometric representations of Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms for odd primes
p
.

1.6
Potential applications to quantitative homotopy theory

One potential application of our Brown representatives arises from how the Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms serve as the starting ingredients of the mod
p
Adams spectral sequence, which computes the
p
-primary part of the stable homotopy groups of spaces
[
1
]
.
2
2

2

An exposition is available in
[
11
]
.
This connection suggests that our constructions could allow lead to quantitative results about the homotopy groups of spheres. For instance, it is natural to ask whether elements of
\pi_{m}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
can be represented by maps between spheres that are “efficient” or have a “simple” geometry. Let us give each sphere the unit round metric, upon we could quantify the “efficiency” or “complexity” of a map
f:\mathbb{S}^{m}\to\mathbb{S}^{n}
by its
k
-dilation
, which is defined as the infimal value of
C
such that
\vol f(\Sigma)\leq C\vol\Sigma
for all
k
-dimensional submanifolds
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{S}^{m}
. Note that 1-dilation is simply the Lipschitz constant.

L. Guth analyzed the geometry of the Steenrod squares in a different way from us to prove a lower bound on the
k
-dilation of
f
when the cell complex obtained by attaching an
(m+1)
-cell to an
n
-cell via
f
has a nonzero Steenrod square and
k\leq m/2

[
19
]
. Due to results about the Hopf invariant
[
2
]
, the condition in Guth’s result is satisfied only when
m=n+1
, or when
n
is sufficiently large,
m=n+3
or
m=n+7
. This leaves open nearly every other homotopy group of a sphere. Our constructions could potentially help us prove versions of this result for other homotopy groups of spheres and for general Steenrod powers for odd primes.

On the flipside, torsion-free elements of
\pi_{m}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
are known to have representatives with controlled Lipschitz constant, while almost none of the torsion classes have a known representative with an explicitly computed Lipschitz constant. For each
m>n
, a result of Serre implies that
\pi_{m}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is generated by finitely many torsion classes and at most one torsion-free class. The torsion-free generator
\alpha
can be represented by a Hopf fibration or a Whitehead product that is 100-Lipschitz
[
27
, p. 34]
. Evidently, it remains to study the torsion classes.

Let us define
L(m,n)
as the infimal value of a constant
L
such that every torsion class in
\pi_{m}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
has a
L
-Lipschitz representative. How fast does
L(m,n)
grow as a function of
m
and
n
? When
\pi_{m}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\neq 0
,
L(m,n)\geq 1
, as any surjective map between unit spheres must be at least 1-Lipschitz. Another lower bound is suggested by the proof by M. Gromov that the number of classes in
\pi_{m}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
that have
L
-Lipschitz representatives is at most a polynomial in
L

[
13
, p. 305]
. An argument of Gromov suggests an upper bound on
L(m,n)
by a tower of exponentials involving
m
and
n

[
15
]
. However, our geometric construction of the Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms could potentially give rise to a quantitative version of the Adams spectral sequence, which may improve this upper bound on the growth of
L(m,n)
.

1.7
Organization of Content

In
section

2
we define the key objects from Geometric Measure Theory that we will use, such as mod
p
integral currents and cycles, varifolds, and the flat metric.

In
section

3
we will define
piecewise smooth families of cycles
, which are families of cycles for which gluings exist. We will prove in
theorem

3.9
that continuous families of cycles can be approximated by piecewise smooth families. In
section

4
we will prove that piecewise smooth families of cycles have gluings (
theorem

4.3
) and use that to prove the Almgren Isomorphism Theorem for the inductive limit topology (
theorem

4.9
) and the Brown Representability Theorem for spaces of cycles (
theorem

4.13
).

In
section

5
we construct explicit Brown representatives of cohomology generators of projective and lens spaces, and Brown representatives for the cross product (
proposition

5.9
) and cup product (
proposition

5.9
). We also use families of cycles to give geometric representations of a mod
p
analogue of fiber integration (
proposition

5.4
) and the Künneth formula (
corollary

5.5
).

In
section

6
we construct the cyclic product map
\mathit{cyc}
rigorously and prove that it is continuous. We also use that to prove
theorem

1.2
. In
section

7
we give an exposition of Steenrod’s construction of the Steenrod powers via equivariant cohomology, then combine that with gluings to prove
theorems

1.3
and
1.7
.

The appendices contains proofs of results that are technical but straightforward, or proofs that are minor adaptations of ideas from the existing literature. The statements of these results may appear in earlier sections but will be labeled using the Roman alphabet, such as
section

3.1
.
Appendix

A
contains proofs of lemmas in Geometric Measure Theory, and
appendix

B
contains proofs of lemmas about the topology of lens spaces.

2
Definitions

2.1
Mod
p
relative integral currents and the flat metric

Fix a prime
p
and some integers
m\geq k\geq 0
. Let
M
be a connected and compact
m
-dimensional Riemannian manifold, possibly with boundary. Let
\mathcal{H}^{k}
denote the
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on
M
. We say that a mod
p
singular
k
-chain
T=\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, is
Lipschitz
if each
\sigma_{i}:\Delta^{k}\to M
is Lipschitz. We also say that
T
is
non-overlapping
if it is “one-to-one almost everywhere” in the sense that
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\{y\in M:\#\bigcup_{i}\sigma_{i}^{-1}(y)>1\})=0
. We may subdivide
T
using a triangulation of
\Delta^{k}
into
k
-simplices
\tau_{j}
which admit positively oriented bilipschitz homeomorphisms
f_{j}:\Delta^{k}\to\tau_{j}
, and replacing each
\sigma_{i}
with
\sum_{j}(\sigma_{i}\circ f_{j})
. Let
I_{k}(M)
denote the set of non-overlapping mod
p
Lipschitz
k
-chains in
M
, except that two such chains are identified if one of them is a subdivision of the other.

The
mass
of some
T=\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}\in I_{k}(M)
is defined as
\mathbf{M}(T)=\sum_{i}\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\mathcal{H}^{k}(\im\sigma_{i})
, where if
a_{i}
is represented by an integer from 0 to
p-1
, then
\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert=\min\{a_{i},p-a_{i}\}
. Note that we can compute the sums and boundaries of elements of
I_{k}(M)
as singular chains, but the resulting chains may not be non-overlapping.

Let
N
be a compact submanifold of
M
. Consider the equivalence relation
\sim
on
I_{k}(M)
where
S\sim T
if their difference as singular chains can be written as a linear combination of singular simplices with images in
N
. Note that since we have identified each
T\in I_{k}(M)
with its subdivisions, there exists some
T^{\prime}\sim T
so that the simplices of
T^{\prime}
with images in
N
have arbitrarily small total volume. We say that some
\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}\in I_{k}(M)
is
non-overlapping with respect to
N
if
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}\cap\partial(M\setminus N))=0
. Now we define the set of
non-overlapping mod
p
relative
k
-chains

I_{k}(M,N)
to be the subset of chains in
I_{k}(M)
that are non-overlapping with respect to
N
, identified according to
\sim
. For each
T\in I_{k}(M,N)
we define its mass to be
\mathbf{M}(T)=\inf_{\tilde{T}\in T}\mathbf{M}(\tilde{T})
. Given any
S,T\in I_{k}(M,N)
, define

\mathcal{F}(S,T)=\inf\{\mathbf{M}(P)+\mathbf{M}(Q)~:~P\in I_{k+1}(M),Q\in I_{k}(M),\partial P+Q\in S-T\}.

\mathcal{F}
is a pseudometric on
I_{k}(M,N)
. Let
I_{k}(M,N)/\mathcal{F}^{-1}(0)
denote the induced metric space obtained by identifying elements at zero
\mathcal{F}
distance from each other. The completion of this metric space is the
space of mod
p
relative flat
k
-chains

F_{k}(M,N)
. It can be shown that
\mathbf{M}
is well-defined on
I_{k}(M,N)/\mathcal{F}^{-1}(0)
(see
lemma

A.5
), and that it can be extended to
F_{k}(M,N)
using the formula

\mathbf{M}(T)=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\{\mathbf{M}(S):S\in I_{k}(M,N)/\mathcal{F}^{-1}(0),\mathcal{F}(S,T)<\varepsilon\}.

(2.1)

This allows us to define the space of
mod
p
relative integral
k
-currents with the flat metric
,
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})=\{T\in F_{k}(M,N):\mathbf{M}(T)<\infty\}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})
is a group and the boundary map on non-overlapping mod
p
relative chains extends to a continuous map
\partial:\mathcal{I}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})\to\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})
for each
k\geq 0
.

2.2
The inductive limit topology

Henceforth we will write
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
to mean
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})
but with the
inductive limit topology

[
5
, (1.9)]
which is the inductive limit of the mass-bounded subspaces of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})
.

Let us give sufficient conditions for maps between spaces of currents or cycles with the inductive limit topology to be continuous. Let
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
denote the sets
\{T\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N):\mathbf{M}(T)<\mu\}
and
\{T\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N):\mathbf{M}(T)<\mu\}
respectively. We will only consider finite
\mu
. Observe that the inductive limit topology on
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
is exactly the same as the flat topology, and similarly for
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
.

{restatable*}

lemmaLemContinuityIndLimTop

A map
g:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\to Y
is continuous in the inductive limit topology if and only if
g
is continuous in the flat topology when restricted to
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
for every
\mu>0
.

A map
f:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\to\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is continuous with respect to the inductive limit topologies on its domain and codomain if for all
\mu>0
, there exists a
\mu^{\prime}>0
such that
\mathbf{M}(T)<\mu\implies\mathbf{M}(f(T))<\mu^{\prime}
, and
f
restricts to a map
f_{\mu}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})^{\mu^{\prime}}
that is continuous which respect to the flat topologies on the domain and codomain.

The above statements are still true when
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{}}
is replaced by
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{}}
.

2.3
Concentration of mass

When applying local modifications to every cycle in a large family of cycles, we may wish to modify the part of each cycle in some ball of radius
r
that is independent of the particular cycle. Ideally,
r
can be chosen so that the pieces of those cycles inside balls of radius
r
must have mass as small as desired. This is known as the
no concentration of mass condition
, and it does not hold in general. We introduce a
mass concentration profile
to quantify how small
r
must be chosen to guarantee that the masses of the parts of cycles inside
r
-balls must be smaller than some given
\mu>0
. Roughly speaking, we will use this profile in
section

3
to ensure that our local modifications to families of cycles preserve the no concentration of mass condition.

Definition 2.2

(Mass concentration profile)
.

Given any map
F:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
, let its
mass concentration profile
be the function
\chi_{F}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
where
\chi_{F}(r)
is the supremum of all
\mathbf{M}(F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B)
, where
x
ranges over
X
and
B
ranges over all balls of radius
r
in
M
.

We say that a map
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)

has no concentration of mass
if
\lim_{r\to 0}\chi_{f}(r)=0
.

3
Piecewise Smooth Families of Cycles

Suppose that
M
is a closed Riemannian manifold, and let
X
be a
n
-dimensional manifold that is oriented when
p
is odd. Consider a continuous map
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{d}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
. When
X=\mathbb{S}^{n}
, the Almgren isomorphism is defined using a “gluing homomorphism” that “glues” the
m
-parameter family
f
of
d
-cycles into an
(n+d)
-cycle
A
. One difficulty in defining this gluing homomorphism is evident from considering each
f(x)
as an integral current mod
p
, which has an
orientation
: a field
v(x)
of
d
-vectors defined almost everywhere on the support of
f(x)
, which intuitively span the tangent planes. If we view
f(x)
as a “submanifold” of
A
, then
f(x)
should have a “normal bundle” within
A
. Thus we would like to find some
n
-vector field
n(x)
with the goal that so that
v(x)\wedge n(x)
is an orientation of
A
. However, it is not clear
a priori
how to find
n(x)
, especially if the cycles
f(y)
close to
f(x)
approach
f(x)
in an irregular way as
y\to x
. The standard constructions of the gluing homomorphism avoid constructing
n(x)
directly by approximating
f
with a discrete family of cycles, and filling between nearby cycles in this discrete family
[
20
,
17
]
.

Throughout the rest of this section, let
M
be a compact
m
-dimensional manifold, and
N
be either
\partial M
or a compact
m
-dimensional submanifold. We call such a pair
(M,N)
an
m
-dimensional collar pair.

Definition 3.1
.

Consider a map
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
, where
X
is a compact smooth manifold with a smooth polyhedral structure. Then we say that a
piecewise smooth structure for
f
is a triplet of families
(\{\varphi_{\sigma}\},\{Z_{\sigma}\},\{\varphi_{\tau\sigma}\})
, where the first two families are indexed over the cells
\sigma
of
X
and the third is indexed over pairs of cells
\tau\subset\sigma
. Each
\varphi_{\sigma}
is a smooth map
\sigma\times M\to M
called a
chart
and each
Z_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is called a
model relative cycle
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{\sigma}(\sigma\times N)

\displaystyle\subset N

and

\displaystyle\varphi_{\sigma\sharp}(\{x\}\times Z_{\sigma})

\displaystyle=f(x)\text{ for all }x\in\sigma.

(3.1)

We further require that for all
x
in the interior of
\sigma
,
\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)
is a diffeomorphism
M\to M
that restricts to a diffeomorphism
N\to N
.

Each
\varphi_{\tau\sigma}
is a smooth map
\tau\times M\to M
called a
collapse map
that satisfies the following properties:

•

\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,-)_{\sharp}(Z_{\sigma})=Z_{\tau}
for any
x\in\tau
.

•

\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)=\varphi_{\tau}(x,-)\circ\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,-)
for all
x\in\tau
. In other words,

\varphi_{\sigma}|_{\tau\times M}=\varphi_{\tau}\circ\big((x,y)\mapsto(x,\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,y))\big).

(3.2)

•

\varphi_{\sigma\sigma}(x,-)=\id
for all
x\in\sigma
and for all
\kappa\subset\tau\subset\sigma
and
x\in\kappa
,
\varphi_{\kappa\sigma}(x,-)=\varphi_{\kappa\tau}(x,-)\circ\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,-)
.

When a map has a piecewise smooth structure, we say that the map is
piecewise smooth.

Piecewise smooth maps offer a natural way to define families of relative cycles that are continuous in the inductive limit topology, as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

When
X
is a compact Riemannian manifold, every piecewise smooth map
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{d}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is Lipschitz with respect to the geodesic distance in the domain and the inductive limit topology in the codomain.

Proof.

Denote the injectivity radius of
X
by
r
. Fix any
x\in X
. For almost every
y\in X
within distance
r
of
x
, the unique minimizing geodesic
\gamma
from
x
to
y
is transverse to every cell of
X
, except possibly at
x
. Thus
\gamma
can be broken into segments, each of which lies in some top-dimensional cell. To prove the lemma, it suffices to prove that
f
is
(C+L)
-Lipschitz when restricted to each segment, where
L=\max\{1,\max_{\sigma}\Lip(\varphi_{\sigma})\}
and
C=\max_{\sigma}\Lip(\varphi_{\sigma})\mathbf{M}(Z_{\sigma})
. In other words, we may reduce to the case where
\gamma
lies within a single top-dimensional cell
\sigma
.

First we prove that
\mathcal{F}(f(x),f(y))\leq C\length(\gamma)
, by “gluing” the family of cycles
f\circ\gamma
into a filling
Q
of
f(y)-f(x)
. More formally, if we regard
\gamma
as a Lipschitz 1-chain (with coefficients in
G
) in the obvious way, then we may define
Q=(\varphi_{\sigma})_{\sharp}(\gamma\times Z_{\sigma})
, because

\partial Q=(\varphi_{\sigma})_{\sharp}(\partial\gamma\times Z_{\sigma})=(\varphi_{\sigma})_{\sharp}((y-x)\times Z_{\sharp})=\varphi_{\sharp}(\{y\}\times Z_{\sharp})-\varphi_{\sharp}(\{x\}\times Z_{\sharp})=f(y)-f(x).

Therefore
\mathcal{F}(f(x),f(y))\leq\mathbf{M}(Q)\leq C\length(\gamma)
.

Since
\mathbf{M}(f(x))\leq C
for all
x\in X
, this implies that
f
is continuous in the inductive limit topology.
∎

Here are several examples of piecewise smooth families of relative cycles. Any two cycles in these families are ambient isotopic to each other, and this observation allows us to show that the families are piecewise smooth, continuous in the
\mathbf{F}
topology when
k\geq 1
, and continuous in the inductive limit topology by
lemma

3.2
.

Example 3.3
.

Consider an oriented fiber bundle
F\to E\xrightarrow{\xi}B
, where
E
is a closed and oriented Riemannian manifold,
B
is a polyhedral complex, and the fibers are
k
-dimensional submanifolds with boundary inside
\partial E
. Then the map
\xi^{-1}(-):B\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(E)
is piecewise smooth.

Find a triangulation of
B
so that each simplex
\sigma
lies within a trivializing neighbourhood
U
. Let the trivializing homeomorphism be
h:\xi^{-1}(U)\to U\times F
. Define the model relative cycle
Z_{\sigma}=\xi^{-1}(v)
, where
v
is the barycenter of
\sigma
. To define the charts, construct a family of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms,
f:\sigma\times\overline{U}\to\overline{U}
, that fix
\partial U
, so that
f(x,v)=x
. Then define the chart
\varphi_{\sigma}
so that each
\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-):E\to E
is the smooth map that is the identity outside of
\xi^{-1}(U)
, and within
\xi^{-1}(U)
it corresponds, under the trivializing homeomorphism
h
, so the smooth map
(y,z)\mapsto(f(x,y),z)
.

For any
\tau\subset\sigma
, define the corresponding collapse map by the formula
\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,-)=\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)\circ\varphi_{\tau}(x,-)^{-1}
.

Example 3.4
.

Let
p=2
and
n\geq 1
. Then the map
f:\mathbb{RP}^{n}\to\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
that sends
\ell\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to
\ell^{\perp}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
is piecewise smooth.

Consider the fiber bundle
\xi:\SO(n+1)\to\mathbb{RP}^{n}
that sends each matrix to the span of its first column. Choose any smooth simplicial structure
X
on
\mathbb{RP}^{n}
. Let
\Pi=0\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. All of the model currents are
\Pi\cap\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
.

Consider the fiber bundle
\SO(n+1)\to\mathbb{RP}^{n}
that sends each matrix to the span of its first column. Since each simplex
\sigma
is contractible, the inclusion
\sigma\hookrightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{n}
lifts using parallel transport to a smooth map
i_{\sigma}:\sigma\to\SO(n+1)
. Now define the corresponding chart so that
\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)
is the linear transformation
i_{\sigma}(x)
. For any simplex
\tau\subset\sigma
, define the collapse maps so that
\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,-)=\varphi_{\tau}(x,-)^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)
.

Let
\perp_{\mathbb{C}}
denote the orthogonal complement of a complex vector subspace in some
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with respect to the standard Hermitian inner product. Endow each complex vector space with its standard orientation as a complex manifold.

Example 3.5
.

Let
p>2
and
n\geq 1
. Then the map
f:\mathbb{CP}^{n}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2n}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{2n+2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2n+2})
that sends a complex line
\ell\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
to
\ell^{\perp_{\mathbb{C}}}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{D}^{2n+2}
is piecewise smooth. (We have
\mathbb{D}^{2n+2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
by the standard identification of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
with Euclidean space.)

The details of this example follow those of
example

3.4
, except with
\SU(n+1)
instead of
\SO(n+1)
.

Example 3.6
.

For any
n\geq 1
, the map
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2n-1}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{2n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2n})
that sends each equivalence class of points

\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p}\}\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{p}\{z+\lambda x_{j}:z\perp_{\mathbb{C}}x_{1},\lambda\in[0,\infty)\}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{D}^{2n}.

Each summand on the right-hand-side is contained in the direct sum of
\vspan_{\mathbb{C}}\{x_{1}\}^{\perp_{\mathbb{C}}}
(treated as a real vector space) with
\vspan_{\mathbb{R}}\{x_{j}\}
, thus we may give the summand the direct sum orientation. The right-hand-side is a mod
p
relative cycle: its boundary is
p\cdot\vspan_{\mathbb{C}}\{x_{1}\}^{\perp_{\mathbb{C}}}=0
.

The details of this example are a straightforward adaptation of those of
example

3.5
.

3.1
The Piecewise Smooth Approximation Theorem

The main result in this section is that every family of relative cycles that is continuous in the inductive limit topology is homotopic to a piecewise smooth approximation. We will prove this by adapting the proof of similar approximation theorems by Guth and Liokumovich
[
20
]
and Staffa
[
28
]
. Their methods approximate families
\{f(x)\}_{x\in X}
of relative cycles that are continuous in the flat metric by “nicer” families where if
x
and
y
are “close” in
X
, then
f(x)-f(y)
is supported in a small ball. To state our theorem formally, we will need to formalize the above notions, following Guth and Liokumovich.

Like
[
20
]
, we will use covers of
\bar{M}=\overline{M\setminus N}
by
\delta
-admissible sets
which are either open balls in the interior or “thickenings” of open balls in
\bar{M}
. To define those thickenings,
[
20
, Lemma 2.1]
gives a parametrization of a collar neighbourhood of
\bar{M}
by a map
E:\partial\bar{M}\times[0,r_{0}]\bar{M}
that is close to 1-Lipschitz, where
r_{0}
is less than the injectivity radius of
\bar{M}
. When
\partial\bar{M}
is smooth,
E
is defined using the exponential map normal to
\partial\bar{M}
.

Definition 3.7

(
\delta
-admissible sets,
[
20
, Section 2.4]
)
.

For any
0<r<r_{0}
, a
generalized ball of radius
r
in
\bar{M}
is either an open ball of radius
r
in the interior of
\bar{M}
or
E(B_{r}\times[0,r])
where
B_{r}
is an open ball of radius
r
in
\partial\bar{M}
. A collection
\mathcal{U}
of generalized balls in
\bar{M}=\overline{M\setminus N}
is called
\delta
-admissible
for some
\delta>0
if they are all disjoint and their radii add up to less than
\delta
.

Definition 3.8

(Localized maps)
.

Consider a cubical complex
X
, and a map
F:V\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
where
V
is a subcomplex of
X
. We say that
F
is
\varepsilon
-fine
if for any
x,y\in V
that lie in the same cell of
X
, either
\mathbf{F}(F(x),F(y))\leq\varepsilon
(when
k\geq 1
) or
\mathcal{F}(F(x),F(y))<\varepsilon
(when
k=0
).

We say that
F
is
\delta
-localized
if every cell
C
of
X
is associated with a
\delta
-admissible collection
\mathcal{U}_{C}
of sets in
\bar{M}=\overline{M\setminus N}
such that for all
x,y\in C\cap V
,
F(x)-F(y)
is supported inside
\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{C}
. Moreover, we say that the family
\{\mathcal{U}_{C}\}_{C}
is
doubling
if it satisfies the following properties:

•

For every cell
C
, the elements of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
are generalized balls that, even when their radii are doubled, are disjoint from one another.

•

For all cells
C\subset C^{\prime}
and every
B\in\mathcal{U}_{C}
,
2B
is contained inside some
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{C^{\prime}}
.

Now we can state our approximation theorem:

Theorem 3.9

(Piecewise Smooth Approximation Theorem)
.

Let
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
be a continuous from a finite cubical complex. Then for any
\delta>0
,
f
is homotopic to a piecewise smooth map
f^{\prime}
that is also
\delta
-localized.

Moreover, there exist some
\delta_{0},\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if
X
has a subcomplex
Y
such that
f|_{Y}
is piecewise smooth with respect to a refinement of
Y
,
f|_{Y}
is
\delta
-localized for some
\delta<\delta_{0}
, and
f
is
\varepsilon
-fine for some
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, then
f^{\prime}
can be chosen so that
f^{\prime}|_{Y}=f|_{Y}
and the piecewise smooth structure of
f^{\prime}
, restricted to
Y
, is a refinement of that of
f|_{Y}
.

Let us first prove
theorem

3.9
for the case
k=0
.

Proof of
theorem

3.9
when
k=0
.

We will explain the proof for the case where
Y=\emptyset
, as the case of
Y\neq\emptyset
follows similarly. By
[
5
, p. 262]
, the cycles
f(x)
all have mass bounded by some constant
\mu
. Thus by
section

2.2
,
f
is continuous in the flat topology. From the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 2.3(2)]
we know that
f
is homotopic to a map
F
that is
\delta
-localized, where
\delta
can be chosen as small as desired. The homotopy is constructed using only cycles that are all bounded by some constant mass (see
[
20
, Proposition 2.6(3)]
), so in fact
F
and the homotopy are all continuous in the inductive limit topology.
F:\tilde{X}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is defined using a refinement
\tilde{X}
of
X
: first the map
F|_{\tilde{X}^{0}}
is constructed in a way that is
\delta
-localized for some small
\delta<\inj(\bar{M})
, then it is extended into the whole of
\tilde{X}
.

It remains to construct a piecewise smooth structure for
F
. We will construct this inductively by following the inductive extension of
F|_{\tilde{X}^{0}}
over the higher and higher skeleta of
\tilde{X}
, so let us explain how this extension is done. Suppose that
F
has been extended into a
\delta
-localized map on
\tilde{X}^{d}
. Consider any
(d+1)
-cell
C
. Since
F|_{X^{d}}
is
\delta
-localized,
C
corresponds to a
\delta
-admissible family
\mathcal{U}_{C}
such that for all
x,y\in\partial C
,
F(x)-F(y)
is supported in
\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{C}
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{C}
consists of disjoint generalized balls, we may define a 1-parameter family of maps
\psi_{t}:M\to M
so that roughly speaking, each
\psi_{t}
is a radial contraction on each generalized ball
B
and identity outside. More precisely, by induction we can show that
\bigcup_{x\in\partial C}\spt(F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B)
is contained in a generalized ball
B^{\prime}
contained in
B
and with strictly smaller radius. Then define
\psi_{t}
so that it is always the identity on
M\setminus\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{C}
, and over each ball
B
disjoint from
\partial\bar{M}
,
\psi_{0}
is the identity, and
\psi_{1}
collapses each generalized ball to its center and stretches the annulus
\overline{B\setminus B^{\prime}}
onto the entirely of
B
.

For generalized balls in the form of
U=E(B\times[0,r))
, the center is regarded as
(x,0)
where
x
is the center of
B
. Then
\bigcup_{x\in\partial C}\spt(F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}U)
is supported in
U^{\prime}E(B^{\prime}\times[0,r^{\prime}))
where
B^{\prime}
is a ball in
B
of strictly smaller radius. Thus we define
\psi_{t}
over
U
is similarly to the previous case to collapse
U^{\prime}
to a point and stretch
U\setminus U^{\prime}
to the entire
U
. It is possible to define this so that
\psi_{t}
restricts to a diffeomorphism
\partial\bar{M}\to\partial\bar{M}
for
t<1
, and so we can extend
\psi_{t}
to a smooth map
M\to M
so that
\psi_{t}(N)\subset N
, and so that for
t<1
,
\psi_{t}
is a diffeomorphism that restricts to a diffeomorphism on
N
.

Then thinking of
C
as a cone over
\partial C
, it suffices to construct a nullhomotopy
F_{t}:\partial C\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
of
F|_{\partial C}
. Simply define
F_{t}(x)=\psi_{t\sharp}(F(x))
. One need to check that
\psi_{1\sharp}(F(x))
does not depend on
x
. If we had been dealing with cycles of positive dimension, then collapsing it to points would zero, so this would be true. This issue is more subtle for 0-cycles:
\psi_{t\sharp}(F(x))
consists of a sum of points, each of which is the center of a generalized ball
B
, with coefficient equal to the sum of the coefficients of the points in
F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B
. (Ignore any points of
F(x)
on
\partial\bar{M}
.) Nevertheless, the construction of the extension from
[
20
]
shows that for any
x,y\in\partial C
and
B\in\mathcal{U}_{C}
,
F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B
can be obtained from
F(y)\mathbin{\llcorner}B
by adding the boundaries of some 1-chains supported in
B
, so the sum of the coefficients of the points in
F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B
is the same as that for
F(y)\mathbin{\llcorner}B
.

Now we proceed to construct the piecewise smooth structure. The polyhedral complex structure on
\tilde{X}
will be its barycentric subdivision.
F|_{\tilde{X}^{0}}
is trivially piecewise smooth: the model currents are
Z_{v}=F(v)
and the charts are
\varphi_{v}(v,-)=\id
. Let us assume by induction that
F|_{X^{d}}
is piecewise smooth with respect to the barycentric subdivison
\Sigma
of
X^{d}
, for
d\geq 0
. Each simplex
\sigma
in
\Sigma
has a chart
\varphi_{\sigma}
and model chain
Z_{\sigma}
. The barycentric subdivision of
C
is the cone over that of
\partial C
. At the apex
v
of the cone we assign
Z_{v}=F(v)
and
\varphi_{v}(v,-)=\id
. For each simplex
\sigma
of
\partial C
, let
C\sigma
denote its cone parametrized as a quotient of
I\times\sigma
. We define
Z_{C\sigma}=Z_{\sigma}
and
\varphi_{C\sigma}(t,x,-)=\psi_{t}\circ\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)
. It can be verified that these definitions satisfy
eq.

3.1
.

It remains to verify the compatibility relations. For each simplex
\sigma
in
\Sigma
, the faces of
C\sigma
are
\sigma
itself and the cones over each face of
\sigma
. (If
\sigma
is a vertex, then
v
is also a face of
C\sigma
.) Define the collapse maps
\varphi_{\sigma(C\sigma)}(x,y)=y
so that
\varphi_{\sigma(C\sigma)}(x,-)_{\sharp}(Z_{C\sigma})=\id(Z_{\sigma})=Z_{\sigma}
and for any
x\in\sigma
,
\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)\circ\varphi_{\sigma(C\sigma)}(x,-)=\psi_{0}\circ\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)=\varphi_{C\sigma}((0,x),-)
. For each face
\tau
of
\sigma
, define
\varphi_{(C\tau)(C\sigma)}((t,x),y)=\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,y)
, so that
\varphi_{(C\tau)(C\sigma)}((t,x),-)_{\sharp}(Z_{C\sigma})=\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,-)_{\sharp}(Z_{\sigma})=Z_{\tau}=Z_{C\tau}
, and for any
(t,x)\in C\tau
and
y\in M
,

\varphi_{C\tau}((t,x),\varphi_{C\tau,C\sigma}((t,x),y))=\psi_{t}(\varphi_{\tau}(x,\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,y)))\\
=\psi_{t}\circ\varphi_{\tau}(x,-)\circ\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,-)(y)=\psi_{t}\circ\varphi_{\sigma}(x,-)(y)=\varphi_{C\sigma}((t,x),y).

Define
\varphi_{v,C\sigma}(v,-)=\psi_{1}
, Observe that
\sigma
is adjacent to exactly one vertex
u
of
\tilde{X}
, and
Z_{C\sigma}=Z_{\sigma}=\dotsb=Z_{u}=F(u)
. Hence
(\varphi_{v(C\sigma)})_{\sharp}(Z_{C\sigma})=\psi_{1\sharp}(Z_{\sigma})=\psi_{1\sharp}(F(u))=F_{1}(u)=Z_{v}
and

\varphi_{v}(v,-)\circ\varphi_{v(C\sigma)}(v,-)(y)=\psi_{1}(y)=\psi_{1}(\varphi_{\sigma}(u,y))=\varphi_{C\sigma}((1,u),y)=\varphi_{C\sigma}(v,y).

∎

The
k\geq 1
case follows a similar proof but needs a further modification for the following reason. The maps
\psi_{t}
in the
k=0
case, when restricted to each generalized ball in the
\delta
-admissible neighbourhoods, stretch an annular subregion of the generalized ball onto the entire generalized ball. We chose this annular region to be away from the support of the cycles
F(x)
involved, which is possible when
\dim F(x)=0
. However, when
\dim F(x)\geq 1
, we must deal with the situation where
F(x)
has some mass in the annular region. Thus we control the amount of the stretching in the asnnular region using
\delta
-admissible collections that are doubling.

{restatable*}

lemmaLemDeltaAdmissibleDoubling

Let
X
be a finite cubical complex, each of whose cells
C
of positive dimension is associated with a
\delta
-admissible family
\mathcal{U}_{C}
. Then for some constant
c=c(\dim X,\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}_{C})
, there is another family of
c\delta
-admissible collections
\mathcal{V}_{C}
that is indexed by the cells of
X
of positive dimension and is also doubling, such that for each cell
C
,
\#\mathcal{V}_{C}\leq c
, and every
U\in\mathcal{U}_{C}
is contained inside some
V\in\mathcal{V}_{C}
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional cubical complex that contains some subcomplex
Y
, and
k\geq 1
. Suppose that
f:X^{0}\cup Y\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is a
\delta
-localized family for some
\delta>0
, which is also piecewise smooth with respect to the barycentric subdivision of
Y
. Suppose further that the corresponding family
\{\mathcal{U}_{C}\}
of
\delta
-admissible collections is also doubling. Then
f
extends to a map
F:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that satisfies the following properties for some constant
c=c(\dim X,\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}_{C})
:

1.

F
is
\delta
-localized with the same family
\{\mathcal{U}_{C}\}_{C}
.

2.

F
is piecewise smooth with respect to the barycentric subdivision of
X
.

3.

\chi_{F}(2\delta)\leq c\chi_{f}(2\delta)
.

4.

For all
x
and
y
that lie in the same cell of
X
,

\mathbf{M}(F(x),F(y))<c\chi_{f}(2\delta).

(3.3)

Proof.

We will prove this by induction on
\dim X
. When
\dim X=0
there is nothing to prove. Suppose the lemma is true for
\dim X=d
, and now suppose that
\dim X=d+1
. By applying the lemma to
X^{d}
and
X^{d}\cap Y
, we have an extension of
f
to a piecewise smooth map
F:X^{d}\cup Y\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that is
\delta
-localized with the family
\{\mathcal{U}_{C}\}
such that
\mathbf{M}(F(x),F(y))\leq c\chi_{f}(2\delta)
for any
x,y\in X^{d}\cup Y
that lie in the same cell of
X
.

Let
C
be a
(d+1)
-cell in
X
that is not contained in
Y
. List the generalized balls inside every
\mathcal{U}_{C^{\prime}}
, where
C^{\prime}
is
d
-face of
C
, as
B^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,B^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}
. Note that
q^{\prime}\leq 2(d+1)\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}_{C}\leq c
. Then for all
x,y\in\partial C
,
F(x)-F(y)
is supported within
\bigcup_{i}B^{\prime}_{i}
. Write
\mathcal{U}_{C}=\{B_{1},\dotsc,B_{q}\}
. Now define
F
over
C
using a nullhomotopy
F_{t}
of
F|_{\partial C}
: for each
B^{\prime}_{i}
, one can define a radial deformation
\psi_{t}:\bigcup_{i}2B^{\prime}_{i}\to\bigcup_{i}2B^{\prime}_{i}
, so that
\psi_{0}
is the identity, and
\psi_{1}
collapses each
B^{\prime}_{i}
to its center and stretches
2B^{\prime}_{i}\setminus B^{\prime}_{i}
to cover the whole
2B^{\prime}_{i}
. This is well-defined because the family
\{\mathcal{U}_{C}\}
is doubling, so the generalized balls
2B^{\prime}_{i}
are disjoint from one another. Moreover, we may choose this family so that every
\psi_{t}
is 2-Lipschitz. Define
F_{t}(x)=(\psi_{t})_{\sharp}(F(x))
. This is a nullhomotopy because as
x
varies within
\partial C
,
F(x)
stays the same outside of
\bigcup_{i}(2B^{\prime}_{i}\setminus B^{\prime}_{i})
.

After extending
F
over
C
, it is still
\delta
-localized with the same family
\{\mathcal{U}_{C}\}
because, as the family is doubling, we have
\bigcup_{i}2B^{\prime}\subset\bigcup_{j}B_{j}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

\chi_{F|_{C}}(2\delta)\leq(1+q^{\prime})\chi_{F|_{X^{d}\cup Y}}(2\delta)\leq c\chi_{F|_{X^{d}\cup Y}}(2\delta)
.

Proof.

Let
B
be any ball of radius
2\delta
in
M
, and consider any
x\in C
. Let
y
be any point on
\partial C
that lies a common radius of
C
with
x
. Then
F(x)
differs from
F(y)
only within
\bigcup_{i}2B^{\prime}_{i}
. Thus
\mathbf{M}(F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B)\leq\mathbf{M}(F(y)\mathbin{\llcorner}B)+\sum_{i}\mathbf{M}(F(y)\mathbin{\llcorner}2B^{\prime}_{i})\leq(1+q^{\prime})\chi_{F|_{X^{d}\cup Y}}(2\delta)
. The lemma follows.
∎

Therefore, we may extend
F
to the whole of
X
, after which we have

\chi_{F}(2\delta)\leq\max_{C}\chi_{F|_{C}}(2\delta)\leq 2(d+1)(\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}_{C})\chi_{F|_{X^{d}\cup Y}}(2\delta)\leq c\chi_{F|_{X^{d}\cup Y}}(2\delta)\leq c\chi_{f}(2\delta),

where the last inequality follows from the induction hypothesis.

And for any
x,y\in C
, since
F(x)
differs from
F(y)
only within
\bigcup_{i}2B^{\prime}_{i}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F(x),F(y))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}(\mathbf{M}(F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}2B^{\prime}_{i})+\mathbf{M}(F(y)\mathbin{\llcorner}2B^{\prime}_{i}))

\displaystyle\leq 2q^{\prime}\chi_{F|_{C}}(2\delta)

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:ExtendPiecewiseSmooth_MassConcProfileRecursion}})

\displaystyle\leq c\chi_{F|_{X^{d}\cup Y}}(2\delta)

\displaystyle(\text{induction hypothesis})

\displaystyle\leq c\chi_{f}(2\delta).

Now we must show that
F
is piecewise smooth with respect to the barycentric subdvision of
X
. This is entirely analogous to the inductive construction of the piecewise smooth structure from the proof of the
k=0
case of
theorem

3.9
.
∎

Given an open set
U\subset M
, let
\partial_{r}U
denote the
r
-neighbourhood of
\partial U
.

{restatable*}

propositionPropDiscreteDeltaLocExtension

For any
\delta>0
and
k\geq 1
, there exist constants
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(n,\bar{M},\delta)
,
c=c(n,\bar{M},\delta)
, and
0<r(M,\delta)\leq\delta
so that the following holds. Suppose that a map
f:X^{0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
from the vertices of an
n
-dimensional cubical complex
X
is
\varepsilon
-fine for some
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
. Then for some a refinement
\tilde{X}
of
X
,
f
can be extended to a
\delta
-localized map
F:\tilde{X}^{0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that satisfies following properties:

1.

For any cell
C
of
X
, any vertex
v
of
C
, and any
x\in C\cap\tilde{X}^{0}
,
\mathcal{F}(f(v),F(x))\leq c\varepsilon
.

2.

\max_{x\in\tilde{X}^{0}}\mathbf{M}(F(x))\leq\max_{x\in X^{0}}\mathbf{M}(f(x))+c\varepsilon
.

3.

For each cell
C
of positive dimension in
X
and each
x\in C\cap\tilde{X}^{0}
,
F(x)
is, roughly speaking, “patched together” from restrictions of
F(y)
for
y\in\partial C\cap\tilde{X}^{0}
. More precisely, there exists a cover
U_{1},\dotsc,U_{N}
of
M
by open sets of diameter
<\delta
, depending on
C
, so that each
x\in C\cap\tilde{X}^{0}
corresponds to some
y_{1},\dotsc,y_{N}\in\partial C\cap\tilde{X}^{0}
, such that

\mathbf{M}\left(F(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{N}F(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}U_{i}\right)\leq c\varepsilon.

(3.4)

Moreover, for any sequence of parameters
0<\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{N}<1
, the
U_{i}
’s can be chosen so that for any
x\in C\cap\tilde{X}^{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2N^{2}}{\delta}(\mathbf{M}(F(x))+c\varepsilon)

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial_{\lambda_{i}r/4}U_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{i}N(\mathbf{M}(F(x))+c\varepsilon).

(3.6)

4.

For each cell
C
of
X
, the
\delta
-admissible collection associated with
C
has at most
c_{4}(n,\dim M)
elements.

Corollary 3.12
.

For any
\delta>0
,
\mu>0
, and
k\geq 1
, there exist constants
\varepsilon_{0}(n,\bar{M},\delta)
and
c(n,\bar{M},\delta,\mu)
so that the following holds. Suppose that a map
f:X^{0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
from the vertices of an
n
-dimensional cubical complex
X
is
\varepsilon
-fine. Then the
\delta
-localized extension
F:\tilde{X}^{0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
of
f
that is obtained from
section

3.1
satisfies the following property:

•

\chi_{F}(r)\leq c_{3}(r/\delta)\chi_{f}(r)
for all
r>0
, where
c_{3}(r/\delta)=c_{3}(k,r/\delta)

Proof.

F
is as given from
section

3.1
. We will prove that for any
r>0
,
\chi_{F_{j}}(r)\leq c_{3}(r/\delta)\chi_{f}(r)
. Let
B
be any generalized ball of radius
r
. Consider any vertex
z
of
C(q_{j})
, and suppose that
F_{j}(z)=G(x,t)
. Since the
U_{i}
’s come from a cover of
M
by generalized balls,
B
can intersect at most
(r/\delta)^{\dim M}
of the open sets
U_{i}
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(z)\mathbin{\llcorner}B)\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B)+\sum_{U_{i}\cap B\neq\emptyset}\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{i})

\displaystyle+{}

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\tau(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}B)+\sum_{U_{i}\cap B\neq\emptyset}\mathbf{M}(\tau(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}B)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{}c_{3}(r/\delta)(\chi_{F_{j-1}}(r)+c_{2}\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{}c_{3}(r/\delta)(\chi_{f}(r)+\varepsilon).

∎

Proof of
theorem

3.9
when
k\geq 1
.

We will prove the case where
Y=\emptyset
as the alternative follows similarly. Let us reduce to the case where
f
has no concentration of mass. Since
X
is compact and
f
is continuous in the inductive limit topology, for all
x\in X
we have
\mathbf{M}(f(x))<\mu
for some constant
\mu

[
5
, p. 262]
. Thus we can use
[
20
, Theorem 2.3]
to homotope
f
to a map
f^{*}
such that
\mathcal{F}(f(x),f^{*}(x))<\varepsilon
for every
x\in X
and some
\varepsilon>0
that can be made arbitrarily small. From the construction of
f^{*}
, we know that
f^{*}
has no concentration of mass. We also know from the construction that there exists some constant
\mu^{\prime}>0
such that
f
,
f^{*}
, and the homotopy between them consist of only cycles of mass less than
\mu^{\prime}
. Thus
f^{*}
and the homotopy are also continuous in the inductive limit topology. Thus we may rename
f^{*}
to
f
and proceed with the proof, assuming that
f
has no concentration of mass.

Next, we will construct a sequence of piecewise smooth approximations of
f
that “converge” to
f
, show that each approximation is homotopic to a better one, and finally chain all of these homotopies together into a single homotopy from the worst approximation to
f
.

Let
\mu=\max_{x\in X}\mathbf{M}(f(x))
. Choose some decreasing sequence
1=\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}>\dotsb\to 0
, and let
\eta_{i}=\min\{\delta_{i},\varepsilon_{0}(n,M,\delta_{i},\mu)\}
, where
\varepsilon_{0}
is defined in
corollary

3.12
. Choose an increasing sequence of integers
0<q_{1}<q_{2}<\dotsb
so that
f:X(q_{i})\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is
\eta_{i}
-fine.

f:X(q_{i})\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is
\eta_{i}
-fine so
section

3.1
gives an extension of
f|_{X(q_{i})^{0}}
to a
\delta_{i}
-localized map
f_{i}:\tilde{X}_{i}^{0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
, where
\tilde{X}_{i}
is a refinement of
X(q_{i})
.
Section

3.1
allows us to assume that the family
\{\mathcal{U}_{C}\}
of
\delta
-admissible collections associated to
f_{i}
is doubling, at the expense of making
f_{i}

c\delta_{i}
-localized. Furthermore, due to
section

3.1
(4),
\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}_{C}\leq c
.

Thus
proposition

3.10
extends
f_{i}
to a
c\delta_{i}
-localized map
F_{i}:\tilde{X}_{i}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that is piecewise smooth with respect to the barycentric subdivision of
\tilde{X}_{i}
. Now let us show that each
F_{i}
is homotopic to
F_{i+1}
. via some homotopy
G_{i}:\tilde{X}_{i,i+1}\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
, where
\tilde{X}_{i,i+1}
denotes the common refinement of
\tilde{X}_{i}
and
\tilde{X}_{i+1}
. Define
G_{i}(x,0)=F_{i}(x)
and
G_{i}(x,1)=F_{i+1}(x)
. We wish to extend
G_{i}
over
\tilde{X}_{i,i+1}\times I
using
proposition

3.10
, so we have to build a family of admissible collections for
G_{i}
out of those for
F_{i}
and
F_{i+1}
.

For each cell
C
of
\tilde{X}_{i,i+1}
and
j=i,i+1
, let
\mathcal{U}^{j}_{C}
denote the
c\delta_{j}
-admissible collection associated to the cell of lowest dimension in
\tilde{X}_{j}
that contains
C
. Now, for each cell
E=C\times C^{\prime}
in
\tilde{X}_{i,i+1}\times I
, define

\mathcal{V}_{E}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{U}^{i}_{C}&C^{\prime}=\{0\}\\
\mathcal{U}^{i+1}_{C}&C^{\prime}=\{1\}\\
\mathcal{U}^{i}_{C}\cup\mathcal{U}^{i+1}_{C}&C^{\prime}=[0,1].\end{cases}

Observe that even though each
\mathcal{V}_{E}
may not be
\delta
-admissible (its members may intersect), the proof of
section

3.1
applies to the family
\{\mathcal{V}_{E}\}_{E}
. From before we have
\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}^{i}_{C}\leq c
and
\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}^{i+1}_{C}\leq c
, so we can obtain a family
\{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{E}\}_{E}
of
c\delta_{i}
-admissible collections that is doubling. Thus it can be verified that
G_{i}
is
c\delta_{i}
-localized, and hence
G_{i}
can be extended into a homotopy from
F_{i}
and
F_{i+1}
by
proposition

3.10
.

It remains to “chain” the homotopies
G_{i}
together to form a homotopy from
F_{1}
to
f
. Define a map
G:X\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
as follows: define
G
over
X\times\{0\}
using
f
, and for each positive integer
i
, define
G
over
X\times[\frac{1}{i},\frac{1}{i+1}]
using
G_{i}
(using
F_{i}
at
X\times\{\frac{1}{i}\}
and so on). Thus
G
is continuous except possibly at points in
X\times\{0\}
. To prove that it is also continuous there, it suffices to prove the following claim:

Claim
.

For any
\lambda>0
, there exists some
i
such that for any cell
C
of
X(q_{i})
,
x_{0},x\in C
, and
t\in[0,\frac{1}{i}]
,
\mathcal{F}(G(x,t),G(x_{0},0))=\mathcal{F}(G(x,t),f(x_{0}))<\lambda
.

Suppose that
i
is a large integer to be chosen later. Let
t\in[\frac{1}{j},\frac{1}{j+1}]
for some
j\geq i
. Let
C^{\prime}
be the cell of lowest dimension in
X(q_{j})
that contains
x
, so
C^{\prime}\subset C
. Let
C^{\prime\prime}
be the cell of lowest dimension in
\tilde{X}_{j}
that contains
x
, so
C^{\prime\prime}\subset C^{\prime}
. Let
v^{\prime}
(resp.
v^{\prime\prime}
) be a vertex of
C^{\prime}
(resp.
C^{\prime\prime}
). Then by the triangle inequality we have

\mathcal{F}(G(x,t),f(x_{0}))\leq\mathcal{F}(G(x,t),G(x,\tfrac{1}{j}))+\mathcal{F}(\underbrace{G(x,\tfrac{1}{j})}_{F_{j}(x)},F_{j}(v^{\prime\prime}))+\mathcal{F}(F_{j}(v^{\prime\prime}),f(v^{\prime}))+\mathcal{F}(f(v^{\prime}),f(x_{0})).

Since
G
was extended over
X\times[\frac{1}{j},\frac{1}{j+1}]
using
proposition

3.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(G(x,t),G(x,\tfrac{1}{j}))

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbf{M}(G(x,t),G(x,\tfrac{1}{j}))

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:ExtendPiecewiseSmooth}})

\displaystyle\leq c\chi_{G_{j}|_{X\times\{0,1\}}}(2c\delta_{j})

\displaystyle\leq c(\chi_{F_{j}}(2c\delta_{j})+\chi_{F_{j+1}}(2c\delta_{j}))

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:ExtendPiecewiseSmooth}})

\displaystyle\leq c(\chi_{f_{j}}(2c\delta_{j})+\chi_{f_{j+1}}(2c\delta_{j}))

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:DiscreteDeltaLocExtensionIsFine}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}(2c)c\chi_{f}(\delta_{j})

\displaystyle\leq c\chi_{f}(\delta_{j}).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(F_{j}(x),F_{j}(v^{\prime\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(x),F_{j}(v^{\prime\prime}))

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:ExtendPiecewiseSmooth}})

\displaystyle\leq c\chi_{f_{j}}(2c\delta_{j})

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:DiscreteDeltaLocExtensionIsFine}})

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}(2c)c\chi_{f}(\delta_{j})

\displaystyle\leq c\chi_{f}(\delta_{j}).

Meanwhile,
section

3.1
(1) implies that
\mathcal{F}(F_{j}(v^{\prime\prime}),f(v))\leq c\eta_{j}\leq c\delta_{j}
, and the fact that
f:X(q_{i})\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is
\eta_{i}
-fine implies that
\mathcal{F}(f(v^{\prime}),f(x_{0}))\leq\eta_{i}\leq\delta_{i}
.

To conclude, the claim follows from our assumption that
f
has no concentration of mass, which guarantees that
\mathcal{F}(G(x,t),f(x_{0}))
can be made arbitrarily small with a sufficiently large choice of
i
.
∎

4
Gluings

Throughout this section, let
(M,N)
be a collar pair.

Definition 4.1

(Gluing)
.

Given a compact Riemannian manifold
X
and a continuous map
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{d}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that vanishes over
\partial X
, a
gluing
of
f
is a sequence of continuous homomorphisms
\Phi^{k}:\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)\to\mathcal{I}_{k+d}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
for
k\geq 0
such that
\partial\circ\Phi^{k+1}=\Phi^{k}\circ\partial
and for any
x\in X
,
\Phi^{0}(x)=f(x)
.

In other words, we may write
\Phi:\mathcal{I}_{*}^{\mathrm{}}(X)\to\mathcal{I}_{*+d}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
and call it a
continuous chain homomorphism of degree
d
.

Definition 4.2
.

We say that a singular chain
T
in
X
is a
C^{k}
chain
for some
k\leq\infty
if for each singular simplex
\sigma:\Delta^{m}\to X
in the chain,
\sigma
is
C^{k}
when restricted to the interior of every subsimplex of
\Delta^{n}
.

We say that
T
intersects a submanifold of
X
transversally if the same is true of each of its singular simplices.

{restatable*}

lemmaLemCInfinityChainsDense

Let
X
be a compact Riemannian manifold, and consider any finite collection of its submanifolds. The
C^{\infty}

k
-chains in
X
that intersect each of those submanifolds transversally form a dense subspace of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
. In fact, any
T\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
is the limit in the flat topology of a sequence of
C^{\infty}

k
-chains
T_{i}
