that intersect each of those submanifolds transversally, such that
\mathbf{M}(T_{i})\to\mathbf{M}(T)
and
\mathbf{M}(\partial T_{i})\to\mathbf{M}(\partial T)
.

Theorem 4.3
.

If
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{d}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
vanishes over
\partial X
and has a piecewise smooth structure
(\{\varphi_{\sigma}\},\{Z_{\sigma}\},\{\varphi_{\tau\sigma}\})
, then
f
has a gluing
\Phi
, called the
standard gluing
for this piecewise smooth structure, such that for all
C^{\infty}

k
-chains
T
that intersects every cell of
X
transversally,

\Phi(T)=\sum_{\dim\sigma=\dim X}\varphi_{\sigma\sharp}((T\mathbin{\llcorner}\sigma)\times Z_{\sigma}).

(4.1)

Proof.

For convenience, let us say that
T\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
is
in general position
if it is a
C^{\infty}

k
-chain that intersects every open cell of
X
transversally. For such
T=\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}
, we first show that
\partial\Phi(T)=\Phi(\partial T)
. For any top-dimensional cell
\tau
, the fact that
T
is in general position implies that
\partial(T\mathbin{\llcorner}\tau)=\partial T\mathbin{\llcorner}\tau+T\cap\partial\tau
. To be precise, by
T\cap\partial\tau
we mean the
(k-1)
-chain
\sum_{j}\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}|_{\sigma_{i}^{-1}(\itr\tau_{j})}
, where the
\tau_{j}
are the faces of
\tau
with the boundary orientation,
\sigma^{-1}(\itr\tau_{j})
is triangulated and given the preimage orientation, and
\sigma_{i}|_{\sigma_{i}^{-1}(\itr\tau_{j})}
is treated as a Lipschitz
(k-1)
-chain. Thus it can be verified that, if we write
n=\dim X
,

\partial\Phi(T)=\sum_{\dim\sigma=n}\partial\varphi_{\sigma\sharp}((T\mathbin{\llcorner}\sigma)\times Z_{\sigma})=\sum_{\dim\sigma=n}\varphi_{\sigma\sharp}(\partial(T\mathbin{\llcorner}\sigma)\times Z_{\sigma})\\
=\sum_{\dim\sigma=n}\varphi_{\sigma\sharp}((\partial T\mathbin{\llcorner}\sigma+T\cap\partial\sigma)\times Z_{\sigma})=\Phi(\partial T)+\underbrace{\sum_{\dim\sigma=n}\varphi_{\sigma\sharp}((T\cap\partial\sigma)\times Z_{\sigma})}_{(\star)}.

Let us show that
(\star)
vanishes. Each
(n-1)
-cell
\tau
of
X
that is not contained in
\partial X
is a face of two
n
-cells,
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
. Pick any orientation on
\tau
; by
eq.

3.2
we have
\varphi_{\sigma}|_{\tau\times M}=\varphi_{\tau}\circ\psi
where
\psi(x,y)=(x,\varphi_{\tau\sigma}(x,y))
, so

\varphi_{\sigma\sharp}((T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\sigma})=\varphi_{\tau\sharp}\circ\psi_{\sharp}((T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\sigma})=\varphi_{\tau\sharp}((T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\tau}).

(4.2)

The second equality holds because of the following lemma:

Lemma 4.4
.

\psi_{\sharp}((T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\sigma})=(T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\tau}
.

Proof.

This is true essentially because Cartesian products of currents are characterized by projections. To be precise, let
\tilde{T}\in T\cap\tau
and
\tilde{Z}\in Z_{\tau}
, which implies that
\tilde{T}\times\tilde{Z}\in(T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\tau}
. Then consider any smooth differential forms
\omega\in\Omega^{i}(\tau)
and
\theta\in\Omega^{d+k-1-i}(M)
, and let
\pr_{1}
and
\pr_{2}
be the projections of
\tau\times M
onto its first and second factor respectively. Then

\psi_{\sharp}(\tilde{T}\times\tilde{Z})(\pr_{1}^{*}\omega\wedge\pr_{2}^{*}\theta)=(\tilde{T}\times\tilde{Z})\psi^{*}(\pr_{1}^{*}\omega\wedge\pr_{2}^{*}\theta)=(\tilde{T}\times\tilde{Z})((\underbrace{\pr_{1}\circ\psi}_{\id})^{*}\omega\wedge(\underbrace{\pr_{2}\circ\psi}_{\varphi_{\tau\sigma}})^{*}\theta)\\
=(\tilde{T}\times\tilde{Z})(\omega\wedge\varphi_{\tau\sigma}^{*}\theta)=\begin{cases}0&i\neq k-1,\\
\tilde{T}(\omega)\varphi_{\tau\sigma\sharp}(\tilde{Z})(\theta)&i=k-1.\end{cases}

The uniqueness condition in the definition of the Cartesian product of currents
[
10
, 4.1.8]
then implies that
\psi_{\sharp}(\tilde{T}\times\tilde{Z})=\tilde{T}\times\varphi_{\tau\sigma\sharp}(\tilde{Z})
. Hence,
\psi_{\sharp}((T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\sigma})=[\psi_{\sharp}(\tilde{T}\times\tilde{Z})]=[\tilde{T}\times\varphi_{\tau\sigma\sharp}(\tilde{Z})]=[\tilde{T}]\times\varphi_{\tau\sigma\sharp}[\tilde{Z}]=(T\cap\tau)\times Z_{\tau}
.
∎

Now observe that
\tau
appears in
\partial\sigma
and
\partial\sigma^{\prime}
but with opposite orientations. Thus
(\star)
may be expanded into terms of the form in
eq.

4.2
, and those terms involving
\tau
will cancel each other out. The remaining terms correspond to
(n-1)
-cells
\tau\subset\partial X
, but since
f
vanishes over
\partial X
, we have
Z_{\tau}=0
, so those terms must also vanish. Therefore, we have now proven that
\partial\Phi(T)=\Phi(\partial T)
.

It remains to analyze the continuity of
\Phi^{k}
. By the characterization of continuity in the inductive limit topology in
section

2.2
, we see that a sufficient condition for a map
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)\to\mathcal{I}_{k+d}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
to be continuous is that it is Lipschitz in both the flat metric and
\mathbf{M}
, in the sense that the map increases mass by at most some constant factor.

It follows from
eq.

4.1
that
\mathbf{M}(\Phi(T))\leq C\mathbf{M}(T)
when
T
is in general position, where
C=\max_{\sigma}\Lip(\varphi_{\sigma})\mathbf{M}(Z_{\sigma})
. Let us also prove that
\Phi^{k}
, defined over mod
p
integral currents in general position, is
2C
-Lipschitz with respect to the flat metric. Let
S,T\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
be in general position. Then there exist rectifiable chains
Q
and
R
such that
S-T=\partial Q+R
and
\mathbf{M}(Q)+\mathbf{M}(R)<2\mathcal{F}(S,T)
. By the deformation theorem (projecting
Q
), we may assume that
Q
and
R
are in general position. Thus
\Phi
is defined on
Q
and
R
, and
\Phi(S)-\Phi(T)=\Phi(\partial Q)+\Phi(R)=\partial\Phi(Q)+\Phi(R)
. Thus
\mathcal{F}(\Phi(S),\Phi(T))\leq 2C\mathcal{F}(S,T)
.

Thus
\Phi^{k}
is continuous over the chains in general position, which form a dense subspace of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
by
section

4
. Moreover,
\Phi^{k}
is uniformly continuous with respect to the flat metric. Thus it extends uniquely to a continuous map
\Phi^{k}:\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)\to F_{k}(M,N)
. Let us show that the chains in the image have finite mass: Indeed, for any
T\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
,
section

4
gives us a Cauchy sequence
T_{i}
of
C^{\infty}

k
-chains in generic position so that
\mathcal{F}(T,T_{i})\to 0
,
\mathbf{M}(T_{i})\to\mathbf{M}(T)
, and
\mathbf{M}(\partial T_{i})\to\mathbf{M}(\partial T)
. Thus we may assume that
\mathbf{M}(T),\mathbf{M}(\partial T),\mathbf{M}(T_{i}),\mathbf{M}(\partial T_{i})<\mu
for some
\mu>0
. Thus
\Phi^{k}(T_{i})
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;\mathcal{F})
, which by the Compactness Theorem for integral currents mod
p

[
10
, p. 432]
implies that
\Phi^{k}(T)
also has mass at most
C\mu
.
∎

Example 4.5
.

Consider a fiber bundle
\xi:E\to B
, where
E
and
B
are closed Riemannian manifolds.
Example

3.3
shows that the preimage map
\xi^{-1}(-):B\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(E)
is piecewise smooth. The definition of
(\xi^{-1}(-))_{\sharp}
shows that it sends the fundamental cycle of
B
to that of
E
. Thus
(\xi^{-1}(-))_{*}
sends the fundamental class of
B
to that of
E
.

Given a map
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that has a gluing
\Phi
,
\Phi
may not be the unique gluing of
f
. However, we will show that the induced homomorphism of homology groups which we denote by
\Phi_{*}:H_{\bullet}(X,\partial X)\to H_{\bullet+k}(M,N)
does not depend on the choice of gluing.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
has a gluing
\Phi
. Then there exists some
\varepsilon>0
, depending on
f
and
\Phi
, such that for any piecewise smooth approximation
g
of
f
such that
\mathcal{F}(f(x),g(x))<\varepsilon
for all
x\in X
, the homomorphism on homology groups induced by
\Phi
is precisely
\Gamma_{*}
, where
\Gamma
is the standard gluing of
g
.

Proof.

Both
\Phi
and
\Gamma
restrict to homomorphisms
C^{\text{cell}}_{\bullet}(X,\partial X)\to\mathcal{I}_{\bullet+k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
. By subdivision, we may assume that for any cell
\sigma
of positive dimension in
X
, we have
\mathbf{M}(\Phi(\sigma)),\mathbf{M}(\Gamma(\sigma))<\varepsilon
. We construct a chain homotopy
\Omega_{i}:C^{\text{cell}}_{i}(X)\to\mathcal{I}_{i+k+1}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
between these two homomorphisms inductively by using the isoperimetric inequality, following
[
17
, p. 1153]
. For each vertex
v
of
X
, since
g
is a piecewise smooth approximation of
f
, we have
\mathcal{F}(f(v),g(v))<\varepsilon
. By the isoperimetric inequality we may assume that
f(v)-g(v)
can be filled by a
(k+1)
-chain of mass at most
\varepsilon
, which we denote by
\Omega_{0}(v)
. This defines
\Omega_{0}
. Now suppose
\Omega_{d}
has been defined for some
d\geq 0
. Then for any
(d+1)
-cell
\sigma
in
X
, define
\Omega_{d+1}(\sigma)
to be a chain filling
\Phi(\sigma)-\Gamma(\sigma)-\Omega_{d}(\partial\sigma)
using the isoperimetric inequality. The mass of this filling can be made arbitrarily small by shrinking
\varepsilon
. Continue inductively.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

If piecewise smooth maps
f,g:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
are homotopic, then their standard gluings
\Phi
and
\Gamma
are chain homotopic. That is, there is a sequence of homomorphisms
\Omega_{i}:\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\mathrm{}}(X)\to\mathcal{I}_{i+k+1}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
such that
\Phi^{i}-\Gamma^{i}=\partial\circ\Omega_{i+1}+\Omega_{i}\circ\partial
.

As a consequence,
\Phi_{*}=\Gamma_{*}
.

Proof.

Using a common refinement, we may assume that
f
and
g
are piecewise smooth respect to the same cubical structure. Denote the homotopy between
f
and
g
by
H:X\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
. By
theorem

3.9
, we may assume that
H
is piecewise smooth with respect to some refinement
\widetilde{X\times I}
of
X\times I
. By
theorem

4.3
,
H
has a gluing
\Psi:\mathcal{I}_{*}^{\mathrm{}}(X\times I)\to\mathcal{I}_{*+k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
. Observe that by the definition of
\Psi
in
eq.

4.1
,
\Phi(T)=\Psi(T\times\{0\})
. Similarly,
\Gamma(T)=\Psi(T\times\{1\})
.

Set
\Omega(T)=\Psi(T\times I)
. Then

(\partial\circ\Omega_{i+1}+\Omega_{i}\circ\partial)(T)=\partial\Psi^{i+1}(T\times I)+\Psi^{i}(\partial T\times I)=\Psi^{i}(\partial(T\times I))\pm\Psi^{i}(\partial T\times I)\\
=\Psi^{i}(T\times\{1\}-T\times\{0\})=\Psi^{i}(T\times\{1\})-\Psi^{i}(T\times\{0\})=\Gamma(T)-\Phi(T).

∎

Corollary 4.8
.

If
f
and
g
are homotopic maps with gluings
\Phi
and
\Psi
, then
\Phi_{*}=\Psi_{*}
.

Thus the induced map on homology groups does not depend on the choice of gluing
\Phi
of
f
. We denote this induced map on homology groups by
f_{*}
.

{restatable*}

propositionPropPiecewiseSmoothGluing

When
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
has a gluing
\Phi
, then the following laws hold:

Composition law.

Suppose that
g:L\to\mathcal{Z}_{c}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
also has a gluing
\Psi
. Then
\Phi\circ\Psi
is a gluing for
\Phi\circ g
. More precisely, the gluing consists of chain homomorphisms
\Phi^{l+c}\circ\Psi^{l}:\mathcal{I}_{l}^{\mathrm{}}(L)\to\mathcal{I}_{l+c+d}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
.

We also have
(\Phi\circ g)_{*}=f_{*}\circ g_{*}
.

Addition law.

Suppose that
g:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
also has a gluing
\Psi
. Then
(f+g)
has a gluing
\Phi+\Psi
and
(f+g)_{*}=f_{*}+g_{*}
.

1.

Suppose that
g:X^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
also has a gluing
\Psi
. Then the induced map
f\vee g:X\vee X^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
has a gluing
T\mapsto\Phi(T\mathbin{\llcorner}X)+\Psi(T\mathbin{\llcorner}X^{\prime})
. Moreover,
(f\vee g)_{*}=f_{*}\vee g_{*}
.

2.

For any
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{d^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime})
, define the maps
Z\times f:N\to\mathcal{Z}_{d^{\prime}+d}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime}\times M)
and
Z\wedge f:N\to\mathcal{Z}_{d^{\prime}+d}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime}\wedge M)
by
(Z\times f)(x)=Z\times f(x)
and
(Z\wedge f)(x)=Z\wedge f(x)
. Similarly define
f\times Z
and
f\wedge Z
. Then

\displaystyle(Z\times f)_{\sharp}(A)

\displaystyle=Z\times f_{\sharp}(A)

\displaystyle(f\times Z)_{\sharp}(A)

\displaystyle=f_{\sharp}(A)\times Z

\displaystyle(Z\wedge f)_{\sharp}(A)

\displaystyle=Z\wedge f_{\sharp}(A)

\displaystyle(f\wedge Z)_{\sharp}(A)

\displaystyle=f_{\sharp}(A)\wedge Z.

3.

Consider the map
F=SX\xrightarrow{Sf}S\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(SM,SN)
. Then
F_{*}
and
f_{*}
commute with the suspension isomorphisms in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\bullet}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\bullet+k}(M,N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\bullet+1}(SX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\bullet+k+1}(SM,SN)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}}

4.1
The Almgren Isomorphism Theorem and Brown representability for the inductive limit topology

Let
X
be a pointed compact Riemannian
d
-manifold. Let
[X,\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)]
denote the abelian group of homotopy classes of continuous pointed maps
X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that send the point to 0 and which also vanish over
\partial X
, and group operation is induced by the addition of cycles. Now we describe the
gluing homomorphism

[X,\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)]\to H_{k+d}(M,N)
as follows. For each element
\alpha\in[X,\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)]
, choose some representative
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that has a gluing, which gives us an element
f_{*}[X]\in\hat{H}_{k+d}(M,N)
. This element exists as a result of
theorems

3.9
and
4.3
. Moreover this correspondence is an additive homomorphism because of the addition law from
section

4
.

When
X=\mathbb{S}^{i}
, the gluing homomorphism
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N))\to\hat{H}_{i+k}(M,N)
is compatible with the usual group structure of homotopy groups because of the wedge sum law from
section

4
.

Theorem 4.9
.

(The Almgren Isomorphism Theorem for the inductive limit topology)

For any
i,k\geq 0
, the gluing homomorphism
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,\partial M)\to\hat{H}_{k+i}(M,\partial M)
is an isomorphism.

Lemma 4.10
.

The map
\xi:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M)\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
satisfies a “relatively piecewise smooth homotopy lifting property”: for any continuous map
\tilde{f}:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
and a piecewise smooth homotopy
F:X\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
such that
F(-,0)=\xi\circ\tilde{f}
, there exists a continuous map
\tilde{F}:X\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
such that
F=\xi\circ\tilde{F}
.

Consequently,
\xi
is a quasifibration with fiber
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(N)
, and induces a long exact sequence

\dotsb\to\pi_{i+1}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(N))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N))\to\pi_{i-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(N))\to\dotsb

Proof.

Let
F^{\prime}(x,t)=F(x,t)\mathbin{\llcorner}\overline{M\setminus N}
, which is also relatively piecewise smooth. Thus
\partial F^{\prime}
, defined as the map
(x,t)\mapsto\partial F^{\prime}(x,t)=\partial F(x,t)\mathbin{\llcorner}\overline{M\setminus N}+F(x,t)\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\overline{M\setminus N}
, is also piecewise smooth, and has a standard gluing
\Phi
by
theorem

4.3
. Then define

\tilde{F}(x,t)=F^{\prime}(x,t)-\Phi(\{x\}\times[0,t])+F(x,0)-F^{\prime}(x,0).

\tilde{F}
is continuous in the inductive limit topology. Note that by the definition of a gluing,

\displaystyle\partial(\Phi(\{x\}\times[0,t]))

\displaystyle=\Phi(\partial(\{x\}\times[0,t]))

\displaystyle=\Phi((x,t)-(x,0))

\displaystyle=\partial F^{\prime}(x,t)-\partial F^{\prime}(x,0)

\displaystyle\implies\partial\tilde{F}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\partial F(x,0)=\partial(\xi(\tilde{f}(x)))=0,

thus
\tilde{F}
is a map
X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
. And we also have
\xi\circ\tilde{F}=F
, because relative cycles in
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
are defined up to chains supported in
N
, and the only summand of
\tilde{F}(x,t)
that is not supported in
N
is
F^{\prime}(x,t)
, so
\xi(\tilde{F}(x,t))=F^{\prime}(x,t)+F(x,t)\mathbin{\llcorner}N=F(x,t)
.

Now, we wish to show that the map
\xi_{*}:\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M),\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(N))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N))
is an isomorphism. It suffices to base both homotopy groups at 0. First we show that it is surjective: for any homotopy class in
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N))
, choose a piecewise smooth representative
F:(\mathbb{D}^{i},\partial\mathbb{D}^{i})\to(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N),0)
, which is always possible due to
theorem

3.9
. (This representative can be chosen so that it passes through 0.) Since
\mathbb{D}^{i}=C\mathbb{S}^{i-1}
, we may view
F
as a nullhomotopy of a constant map
\mathbb{S}^{i-1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that sends
\mathbb{S}^{i-1}
to 0. This constant map clearly lifts to a constant map
\mathbb{S}^{i-1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
. Thus by the piecewise smooth homotopy lifting property proven earlier,
F
lifts to a homotopy
\tilde{F}:\mathbb{S}^{i-1}\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
such that
\xi(\tilde{F}(x,1))=0
for all
x
. That is,
\tilde{F}(x,1)\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(N)
for all
x
, which give surjectivity.

To prove injectivity, suppose that
\xi_{*}(\alpha)=0
. Using
theorem

3.9
, choose a piecewise smooth representative
\tilde{f}:(\mathbb{D}^{i},\partial\mathbb{D}^{i})\to(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M),\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(N))
of
\alpha
. Thus
\xi\circ\tilde{f}
is nullhomotopic via a map
F:(\mathbb{D}^{i},\partial\mathbb{D}^{i})\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
that sends
\partial\mathbb{D}^{i}\times I
to 0. Since we can homotope
\xi\circ\tilde{f}
to a piecewse smooth map, we may apply
theorem

3.9
to homotope relative to its boundary until it is piecewise smooth. Then
\tilde{f}
lifts
F|_{(\mathbb{D}^{i},\partial\mathbb{D}^{i})\times\{0\}}
, so by the piecewise smooth homotopy lifting property,
F
can be lifted to a nullhomotopy of
\tilde{f}
. Thus
\alpha=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.11

(Excision)
.

The map
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\overline{M\setminus N},\partial\overline{M\setminus N})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Consider the homomorphism
\phi:\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\overline{M\setminus N},\partial\overline{M\setminus N}))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N))
. We first show that it is surjective: any element in the codomain has a piecewise smooth representative
f:\mathbb{S}^{i}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
by
theorem

3.9
, and if we take its piecewise smooth structure and restrict every model relative cycle to
\overline{M\setminus N}
, then we get a piecewise smooth map
\mathbb{S}^{i}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\overline{M\setminus N},\partial\overline{M\setminus N})
that is sent to
[f]
by
\phi
.

The injectivity proof is similar. Suppose that a piecewise smooth map
g:\mathbb{S}^{i}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\overline{M\setminus N},\partial\overline{M\setminus N})
is sent to the zero class by
\phi
. Then
g
extends to a map
G:\mathbb{D}^{i+1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
. By
theorem

3.9
, we can choose
G
to be piecewise smooth, and agreeing with the piecewise smooth structure of
g
on
\partial\mathbb{D}^{i+1}
. Then as before, we restrict every model relative cycle to
\overline{M\setminus N}
, and obtain a map
\mathbb{D}^{i+1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\overline{M\setminus N},\partial\overline{M\setminus N})
. Thus
g
is nullhomotopic.
∎

Theorem 4.12
.

Consider any
i,k,n\geq 0
. Then the gluing homomorphism
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))\to\hat{H}_{k+i}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is an isomorphism. Moreover, if
n\geq 1
then the gluing homomorphism
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n}))\to\hat{H}_{k+i}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
is also an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let us begin by handling various edge cases. When
n=0
, all of the groups in the theorem statement vanish unless
k=0
, in which case
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{0})=\{ax-ay~|~a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\}
with the discrete topology where
\mathbb{S}^{0}=\{x,y\}
, and
\hat{H}_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{0})=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, so the theorem holds.

When
i=0
there are three cases:

•

If
k>n
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})=\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})=\{0\}
so the theorem holds.

•

If
k=n
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
are both
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with the discrete topology, so the theorem also holds.

•

If
k<n
, then it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
are both connected. For any
T\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, the piecewise smooth approximation theorem gives a path connecting
T
to a polyhedral cycle. Find an open ball in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
disjoint from that polyhedral cycle and dilate that ball until its complement is squashed into a point. Similarly for
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
.

Now we prove the theorem by inducting on
n
; the base case of
n=0
has already been handled. Suppose that the theorem holds for some
n\geq 0
(and all
i,k\geq 0
); we will prove it for
n+1
. The case where
i=0
has been handled already. Consider any
k\geq 0
and
i\geq 1
. Identify
\mathbb{S}^{n}
with
\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
, and
\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
with the lower hemisphere of
\mathbb{S}^{n+1}
. Consider the following diagram, which may not commute
a priori.
the isomorphisms in the upper row come from the long exact sequences in the labeled lemmas. The map labeled
\partial
comes from a long exact sequence of reduced homology groups. The curved arrow arises from the suspension isomorphism. The columns are the gluing homomorphisms, among which the rightmost one is an isomorphism by the induction hypothesis. Let
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
be any choice of elements of the groups below them that correspond to each other via the isomorphisms.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\alpha}{\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1},\mathbb{D}^{n+1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\beta}{\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{S}^{n}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\gamma}{\pi_{i-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}_{k+i}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}_{k+i}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{S}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{H}_{k+i-1}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
Lem.
4.10
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
Lem.
4.11
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
Lem.
4.10
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
suspension
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Thus the groups
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1}))
,
\hat{H}_{k+i}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1})
,
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{S}^{n}))
, and
\hat{H}_{k+i}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{S}^{n})
are abstractly isomorphic to one another, but we need to prove that the corresponding gluing homomorphisms are isomorphisms. To do that, we prove that the outer “rectangle” and the inner square of the diagram commute.

Suppose that
\gamma
is represented by a piecewise smooth map
f:\mathbb{S}^{i-1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. Consider the map
F:C\mathbb{S}^{i-1}\xrightarrow{Cf}C\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(C\mathbb{S}^{n})=\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
where
C\mathbb{S}^{n}
is identified with
\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. The proof of
lemma

4.10
shows that
\beta
is represented by the map
F^{\prime}:(\mathbb{D}^{i},\mathbb{S}^{i-1})\to(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{S}^{n}),0)
induced by
F
.

The excision map sends
\beta=[F^{\prime}]\in\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{S}^{n}))
to
[F^{\prime}]\in\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1},\mathbb{D}^{n+1}))
. Thus
\alpha
is in fact represented by the map
G:\mathbb{S}^{i}=S\mathbb{S}^{i-1}\xrightarrow{Sf}S\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(S\mathbb{S}^{n})=\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+1})
. It can be verified that
F
,
F^{\prime}
, and
G
are all piecewise smooth. Thus the gluing homomorphism sends
\alpha
to
G_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i}]
and
\gamma
to
f_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]
.
Section

4
(
3
) implies that
G_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i}]
and
f_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]
are related by the suspension isomorphism, so the outer rectangle of the diagram commutes.

Now observe that the gluing homomorphism sends
\beta
to
F^{\prime}_{*}[\mathbb{D}^{i}]
. By definition,
\partial(F^{\prime}_{*}[\mathbb{D}^{i}])
is computed by taking an absolute chain that represents
F^{\prime}_{*}[\mathbb{D}^{i}]
and returning the homology class of its boundary. Let
\Phi
be a gluing for
F
. Then if we identify
C\mathbb{S}^{i-1}
with
\mathbb{D}^{i}
, then
F^{\prime}
has a gluing
\Phi^{\prime}
which is simply
\Phi
composed with the map
\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1})\to\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{S}^{n})
, because this map commutes with the boundary map. Therefore
\partial(F^{\prime}_{*}[\mathbb{D}^{i}])=\partial(\Phi^{\prime}_{*}[\mathbb{D}^{i}])
is the class, in
\hat{H}_{k+i-1}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, of
\partial\Phi[\mathbb{D}^{i}]=\Phi\partial[\mathbb{D}^{i}]=\Phi[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]
. Let
j:\mathbb{S}^{n}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
be the inclusion of the boundary. The class of
\Phi[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]
in
\hat{H}_{k+i-1}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is
\Phi_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]=F_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]=F_{*}j_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]
which by the composition law in
section

4
is
(F\circ j)_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]=f_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{i-1}]
. Thus the inner square of the diagram commutes.
∎

Proof of
theorem

4.9
.

Find a triangulation of
M
. Our proof will use the relative cellular homology of
M
, which is the homology of the relative cellular chain complex
\hat{H}_{*}(M^{d},M^{d-1}\cup(\partial M)^{d})
. There exists a sequence
\mathcal{N}_{d}
of compact tubular neighbourhoods in
M
of
M^{d}
, with increasing radii, such that each
\mathcal{N}_{d}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{d-1}
is a disjoint union of “thickenings” of the
d
-cells of
M
. Similarly, let
\mathcal{N}^{\partial}_{d}
be the tubular neighbourhood of the
d
-skeleton of
\partial M
with the same radius as
\mathcal{N}_{d}
. Observe that
Y_{d}=\mathcal{N}_{0}\cup\dotsb\cup\mathcal{N}_{d}
deformation retracts onto
M^{d}
,
Y^{\partial}_{d}=\mathcal{N}^{\partial}_{0}\cup\dotsb\cup\mathcal{N}^{\partial}_{d}
deformation retracts onto
(\partial M)^{d}
, and that

\hat{H}_{j}(Y_{d},Y_{d-1}\cup Y^{\partial}_{d})\cong\hat{H}_{j}(M^{d},M^{d-1}\cup(\partial M)^{d}).

(4.3)

Note that
(Y_{d},Y_{d-1}\cup Y^{\partial}_{d})
is a collar pair. Let us first prove that the gluing homomorphism,

\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(Y_{d},Y_{d-1}\cup Y^{\partial}_{d}))\to\hat{H}_{k+i}(Y_{d},Y_{d-1}\cup Y^{\partial}_{d}),

(4.4)

is an isomorphism. By
lemma

4.11
, the domain is isomorphic to
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\coprod_{l}e^{d}_{l},\coprod_{l}\partial e^{d}_{l}))
, where the
e^{d}_{l}
’s are the
d
-cells of
M
that are not contained in
\partial M
. The codomain is isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{l}\hat{H}_{k+i}(e^{d}_{l},\partial e^{d}_{l})
. Thus we can apply
theorem

4.12
to show that
eq.

4.4
is an isomorphism. After this, it is a matter of diagram chasing to prove the theorem.
∎

Theorem 4.13

(Brown Representability for Spaces of Cycles)
.

For each cell complex
X
, there is an isomorphism
[X,\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})]\xrightarrow{\cong}\hat{H}^{n-k}(X,\partial X)
, where the homotopy class of a map
f
that has a gluing corresponds to the cohomology class that evaluates on a homology class
\alpha\in\hat{H}_{n-k}(X,\partial X)
to
f_{*}(\alpha)\in\hat{H}_{n}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

There is also an analogous isomorphism
[X,\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})]\xrightarrow{\cong}\hat{H}^{n-k}(X)
, whose definition is the same except with
H_{n}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
in the place of
H_{n}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
.

Proof.

We will prove the theorem for
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
as the other case follows similarly.
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is weakly homotopy equivalent to the Eilenberg-Maclane space
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n-k)
, due to
theorem

4.12
. Hence the Brown Representability Theorem and standard arguments from obstruction theory imply that there is an isomorphism
[X,\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})]\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{n-k}(X)
that sends
[f]
to
f^{*}\iota_{n-k}
. We fix
\iota_{n-k}
by first fixing an isomorphism
\pi_{n-k}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))\cong\hat{H}_{n}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where the first isomorphism comes from
theorem

4.12
and the second from identifying the fundamental class associated to the standard orientation with 1. This fixes a generator of
\pi_{n-k}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))
corresponding to 1, which corresponds to a generator of
H_{n-k}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))
by the Hurewicz theorem. The dual of this homology generator is
\iota_{n-k}
.

Now consider some
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and homology class
\alpha\in\hat{H}_{n-k}(X)
. By the preceding choice of
\iota_{n-k}
, to evaluate
f^{*}\iota_{n-k}
on
\alpha
, we have to push
\alpha
forward using
f
to a homology class in
H_{n-k}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))
, find its preimage in
\pi_{n-k}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))
over the Hurewicz isomorphism, and then apply the gluing homomorphism to get a homology class in
\hat{H}_{n}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

By obstruction theory, we may assume that we have homotoped
f
until it vanishes on
X^{n-k-1}
. By the relative piecewise smooth appoximation theorem,
f
is also piecewise smooth with respect to some refinement
\tilde{X}
of
X
, so it has a gluing
\Phi
. Each homology class
\alpha\in\hat{H}_{n-k}(X)
can be represented by a cellular cycle
A
. Since
f
vanishes over the boundary of each summand cell
e^{n-k}
of
A
,
f
defines an element of
\beta\in\pi_{n-k}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}))
which is precisely the sum of the images of
e^{n-k}
with their respective multiplicities in
A
.
\beta
precisely corresponds to the pushforward of
\alpha
along
f
under the Hurewicz isomorphism. Then applying the gluing homomorphism we get
[\Phi(A)]=f_{*}(\alpha)
.
∎

4.2
Gluings as Brown representatives

Since gluings give maps between spaces of cycles, it is natural to ask what cohomology classes they represent.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
X
be a compact Riemannian manifold, and let
\alpha_{i1},\dotsc,\alpha_{id_{i}}
be a basis for
H_{i}(X,\partial X)
. Then for any
k\geq 0
,
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X,\partial X)\sim_{w}\prod_{i>k}\prod_{j=1}^{d_{i}}K_{i-k,j}
where each
K_{mj}
is a copy of
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)
.

In addition, let
\iota_{mj}\in H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X,\partial X))
correspond to the fundamental cohomology class of
K_{mj}
. Suppose that a class
\gamma\in H^{i}(X,\partial X)
has a piecewise smooth Brown representative
f:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{l}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{i+l},\partial\mathbb{D}^{i+l})
. Then if
\Phi
is the standard gluing of
f
, then
\Phi^{k}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X,\partial X)\to\mathcal{Z}_{l+k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{i+l},\partial\mathbb{D}^{i+l})
is a Brown representative for
\gamma(\alpha_{i1})\iota_{i-k,1}+\dotsb+\gamma(\alpha_{id_{i}})\iota_{i-k,d_{i}}
.

The real content of this proposition is that
\Phi^{k}
is a Brown representative for a cohomology class that only contains fundamental cohomology classes as summands, and not any other classes, such as cross products or Steenrod powers of some classes in lower degrees. In this sense, gluings by themselves cannot represent “interesting” cohomology classes.

Proof.

For our purposes it is enough to prove this for
X=L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
as this is the only case we need for the proof of
theorem

1.3
. The general case will follow similarly, just with more cumbersome notation.

In this case,
theorem

4.9
and the Thom Isomorphism Theorem imply that
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X,\partial X)\sim_{w}\prod_{j=n+1-k}^{p(n+1)-1-k}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},j)
. Thus
\Phi^{k}
is a Brown representative of a class
\phi
which is the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear combination of cross products of cup products of mod
p
cohomology operations applied to
\iota_{j}
’s for
j\leq i-k
.

Our main task is to show that
\phi
is simply a multiple of
\iota_{i-k}
(after which the precise multiplicative factor may be computed by a simple evaluation), and that there are no more complicated terms. To prove this, we “restrict
f
to
\prod_{j=n+1-k}^{q}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},j)
” where
q=i-k-1
and show that the result is a Brown representative for the zero class. Consider a triangulation of the polyhedral structure of
X
that comes from the piecewise smooth structure of
f
, and let
\mathcal{N}_{i-1}
denote a tubular neighbourhood of
\overline{X^{i-1}\setminus\partial X}
in
X
. Then consider the inclusion

\lambda:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathcal{N}_{i-1},\mathcal{N}_{i-1}\cap\partial X)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X,\partial X).

Theorem

4.9
implies that
\pi_{j}(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathcal{N}_{i-1},\mathcal{N}_{i-1}\cap\partial X))\cong H_{k+j}(X^{i-1},(\partial X)^{i-1})
. Thus
\lambda
induces a surjection on homotopy groups of degree at most
q
. By considering
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathcal{N}_{i-1},\mathcal{N}_{i-1}\cap\partial X)
as weakly homotopy equivalent to a product of Eilenberg-Maclane spaces, one for each generator of a homotopy group, we see that the
\lambda
also induces surjections on homology groups of degree at most
q
. Thus
\lambda
induces injections on cohomology groups of degree at most
q
, and it suffices to check that
\Phi^{k}\circ\lambda
is nullhomotopic.

By the deformation retract of
\mathcal{N}_{i-1}
onto
X^{i-1}
,
\lambda
is homotopic to a map
\lambda^{\prime}
such that every
\lambda^{\prime}(T)
is supported in
X^{i-1}
. However, if the
(i-1)
-cells of
X
are denoted by
e^{i-1}_{r}
, then the support of every
\Phi^{k}(\lambda^{\prime}(T))
is inside a
(i-1+l)
-dimensional set (because
\Phi
is a standard gluing), which cannot cover the entirety of
\mathbb{D}^{i+l}
. This allows us to nullhomotope
\Phi^{k}\circ\lambda^{\prime}
, and also
\Phi^{k}\circ\lambda
.
∎

5
Brown Representatives of some Cohomology Classes and Operations

5.1
Representing the cohomology generators of projective and lens spaces

Let
n\geq 1
. When
p=2
, let
\omega_{k}
denote the generator of
H^{k}(\mathbb{RP}^{n})
for all
k\leq n
. When
p>2
and
L
is an
n
-dimensional lens space for
n\leq\infty
, let
\omega_{k}
denote the generator of
H^{k}(L)
for all
k\leq n
, as chosen in
section

B.1
. We will represent points of lens spaces as sets of
p
points, which are the fibers of the covering map
\mathbb{S}^{n}\to L
. Let
\vspan_{\mathbb{C}}
denote the complex span.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
.

1.

When
p=2
, the map
\mathbb{RP}^{n}\to\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
that is defined by
\ell\mapsto\ell^{\perp}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
(see
example

3.4
) is a Brown representative for
\omega_{1}
.

2.

When
p>2
, the map
\mathbb{CP}^{n}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2n}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{2n+2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2n+2})
that is defined by
\ell\mapsto\ell^{\perp_{\mathbb{C}}}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{D}^{2n+2}
(see
example

3.5
) is a Brown representative for the generator of
H^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})
that evaluates to 1 on the 2-cell.

3.

When
p>2
, the map
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2n-2}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{2n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2n})
that is defined by
\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p}\}\mapsto\vspan_{\mathbb{C}}\{x_{1}\}^{\perp_{\mathbb{C}}}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{D}^{2n}
is a Brown representative for
\omega_{2}
.

4.

When
p>2
, the map
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2n-1}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{2n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2n})
that is defined in
example

3.6
is a Brown representative for
\omega_{1}
.

Proof.

To prove (
1
), it suffices to evaluate the cohomology class represented by the map
\ell\mapsto\ell^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
on the homology class represented by
\mathbb{RP}^{1}\subset\mathbb{RP}^{n}
, where
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
consists of the lines through the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}^{n-1}
. Observe that a generic point in
\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
lies
\ell^{\perp}
for exactly one
\ell\in\mathbb{RP}^{1}
. For this reason, the disks
\ell^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
glues into the relative fundamental class of
\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. (
2
) follows similarly.

(
3
) follows from the fact that the map
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}
sending
\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p}\}
to
\vspan_{\mathbb{C}}\{x_{1}\}
induces an isomorphism on
H^{2}
. This is a well-known fact that follows from the Serre spectral sequence of the fiber bundle
S^{1}\to\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}
.

To prove (
4
), let
f
denote the map in
example

3.6
. Note that the 1-skeleton of
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}
is
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, which we may parametrize as an arc
\{(e^{i\theta},0,\dotsc,0)~|~\theta\in[0,2\pi/p]\}\subset\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}
(see
[
22
, p. 144]
). Then
f_{*}[\mathbb{S}^{1}]
is precisely the fundamental cycle of
\mathbb{D}^{2n}
, because a generic point in
\mathbb{C}^{d-1}
(with norm less than 1) lies in exactly one of the mod
p
cycles
f(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Intuitively, the cycles
f(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
rotate about
(\mathbb{C}\times\{0\}^{d-1})^{\perp_{\mathbb{C}}}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{C}^{d-1}
and sweep out
\mathbb{D}^{2n}
exactly once.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Consider the general lens space
L=\mathbb{L}_{p}(\ell_{1},\dotsc,\ell_{n})
, where each
\ell_{j}
is a nonzero element of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let
\ell_{j}^{-1}
denote the multiplicative inverse of
\ell_{j}
. Then the map
L\to\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}
defined by

(r_{1}e^{i\theta_{1}},\dotsc,r_{n}e^{i\theta_{n}})\mapsto(r_{1}e^{i\ell_{1}^{-1}\theta_{1}},\dotsc,r_{n}e^{i\ell_{n}^{-1}\theta_{n}}),

composed with the maps from
lemma

5.1
(
3
) and (
4
) give Brown representatives
\omega_{2}
and
\omega_{1}
respectively.

Proof.

By
[
22
, p. 310]
, the map induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}(-)
, and since the Bockstein homomorphisms
H^{1}(-)\to H^{2}(-)
are isomorphisms for
L
and
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{2n-1}
, it is also an isomorphism on
H^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
X
be a closed manifold and
X\times\mathbb{D}^{q}
be a trivial disk bundle over
X
. If some
\alpha\in H^{k-n}(X)
has a Brown representative
a:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
, then the the class in the Thom space
(X\times\mathbb{D}^{n})/(X\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
that corresponds to
\alpha
via the Thom isomorphism has the following Brown representative:

\displaystyle f:X\times\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}((\mathbb{D}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{q}),\partial(\mathbb{D}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{q}))

\displaystyle f(x,v)=a(x)\times\{v\}.

Proof.

This follows from
theorem

4.13
, and the fact that evaluating this
f
on a homology class involves gluing it over the product of a cycle in
X
and a copy of
\mathbb{D}^{n}
.
∎

5.2
The cohomology pushforward and the Künneth formula

Proposition 5.4
.

Consider a fiber bundle
M\to E\xrightarrow{\pi}B
where
M
is a closed Riemannian
m
-manifold, and
E
, and
B
are compact Riemannian manifolds of dimensions
\dim E=e
and
\dim B=b
respectively such that
\partial E=\pi^{-1}(\partial B)
. Suppose that
\partial B
is the disjoint union of
\partial_{-}B
and
\partial_{+}B
, either of which may be empty. Let
\partial_{\pm}E=\pi^{-1}(\partial_{\pm}B)
. Then the pushforward map on
n^{\text{th}}
cohomology, where
0\leq n\leq e

\pi_{*}=H^{n}(E,\partial_{+}E)\xrightarrow{\text{Poincar\'{e} duality}}H_{e-n}(E,\partial_{-}E)\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}}H_{e-n}(B,\partial_{-}B)\xrightarrow{\text{Poincar\'{e} duality}}H^{n-m}(B,\partial_{+}B),

sends a relative cohomology class represented by a piecewise smooth map
f:(E,\partial_{+}E)\to(\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k}),0)
to the class represented by

\pi_{*}f:=(B,\partial_{+}B)\xrightarrow{\pi^{-1}(-)}(\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(E),\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\partial_{+}E))\xrightarrow{f_{\sharp}}(\mathcal{Z}_{k+m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k}),0).

(5.1)

Proof.

Let us first establish the adjointness property: for any piecewise smooth map
g:B\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{b-(n-m)})
,

\displaystyle(\pi_{*}f\wedge g)_{*}[B]

\displaystyle=\big(x\mapsto f_{\sharp}(\pi^{-1}(x))\wedge g(x)\big)_{*}[B]

(wedge sum law from
section

4
)

\displaystyle=\big(x\mapsto(f\wedge(g\circ\pi))_{\sharp}(\pi^{-1}(x))\big)_{*}[B]

\displaystyle=((f\wedge(g\circ\pi))_{\sharp}\circ\pi^{-1})_{*}[B]

(composition law from
section

4
)

\displaystyle=(f\wedge(g\circ\pi))_{*}\circ\pi^{-1}_{*}[B]

(
example

4.5
)

\displaystyle=(f\wedge(g\circ\pi))_{*}[E].

The fact that
\pi_{*}
represents the cohomology pushforward can be proven from this adjointness relation, by noting that the wedge product here represents cup product, relating the cup product to the cap product and thus Poincaré duality, and then performing algebraic manipulations.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
X
be a closed manifold and
Y
be a manifold with boundary, and let
f:X\times Y\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
be a piecewise smooth map that represents
\gamma=\bigoplus_{i+j=n-k}\alpha_{i}\otimes\beta_{j}
, where
\alpha_{i}\in H^{i}(X)
and
\beta_{j}\in H^{j}(Y)
. Then for any closed
d
-dimensional submanifold
A\subset X
,
\alpha_{d}([A])\beta_{n-k-d}\in H^{n-k-d}(Y)
is represented by the map

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Z}_{k+d}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{\sharp}(A\times\{y\}).

Proof.

Observe that the above map is
\pi_{*}f|_{A\times Y}
, where
\pi:A\times Y\to Y
is the projection onto the second factor. Let the classes
\alpha_{i}
pull back to
\alpha^{\prime}\in H_{i}(A)
along the inclusion
A\hookrightarrow X
. Then
f|_{A\times Y}
represents
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\otimes\beta_{n-k-i}
, so
proposition

5.4
implies that
\pi_{*}f|_{A\times Y}
represents the cohomology pushforward of
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\otimes\beta_{n-k-i}
. That is, take the Poincaré dual to
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\overline{\alpha_{i}^{\prime}}\otimes\bar{\beta}_{n-k-i}
(bars indicate Poincaré duals), push forward via
\pi_{*}
to get
\overline{\alpha_{d}^{\prime}}\bar{\beta}_{n-k-d}=\alpha_{d}^{\prime}[A]\bar{\beta}_{n-k-d}=\alpha_{d}[A]\bar{\beta}_{n-k-d}
, then take the Poincaré dual again to get
\alpha_{d}[A]\beta_{n-k-d}
.
∎

5.3
Brown representatives for the cross product and cup product

Let
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
be collar pairs. In this section we will show that the Cartesian product of cycles gives a Brown representative for the cross product. However, to formalize this we need to decide on the topology on products of the form
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\times\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
. A product of this form has two natural topologies: the product topology of the two inductive limit topologies, and the inductive limit topology with respect to the family of sets
A\times B
, where
A
is mass-bounded in
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
and
B
is mass-bounded in
\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
. We call the former the
product topology
and the latter the
inductive limit topology
. The product topology is finer than the inductive limit topology
[
8
, 2.6]
.

Let us show that these topologies are weakly homotopy equivalent. The following remark will be helpful:

Remark 5.6
.

On the space
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\times\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})^{\mu^{\prime}}
for any
\mu,\mu^{\prime}>0
, the topolgoies induced as a subspace of the product topology and as a subspace of the inductive limit topology agree with the topology as a subspace of the product of the flat topologies.

Lemma 5.7
.

The product and inductive limit topologies on
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\times\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weakly homotopy equivalent.

Proof.

We wish to prove that the relevant homomorphisms between homotopy groups, going from the product topology to the inductive limit topology, are isomorphisms. First we show surjectivity: for any map
f:\mathbb{S}^{i}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\times\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
that is continuous in the inductive limit topology, its image is compact so by adapting
[
5
, (1.9)]
we see that its image must lie in
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\times\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})^{\mu^{\prime}}
for some
\mu,\mu^{\prime}>0
. By
remark

5.6
,
f
is also continuous in the product topology. A similar argument shows injectivity.
∎

Henceforth, we will give the inductive limit topology to finite products of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{j}\mathcal{Z}_{k_{i}}^{\mathrm{}}(M_{i},N_{i})
. By
lemma

5.7
, we can compute their cohomology using the Künneth formula.

Lemma 5.8
.

The map
c:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\times\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\to\mathcal{Z}_{k+k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M\times M^{\prime},M\times N^{\prime}\cup N\times M^{\prime})
where
c(S,T)=S\times T
is continuous.

Proof.

We will adapt the sufficient condition for continuity in
section

2.2
. Suppose that for some
S_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
and
T_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
for
i=1,2
, and
\mu,\mu^{\prime}>0
we have
\mathbf{M}(S_{i})<\mu
and
\mathbf{M}(T_{i})<\mu^{\prime}
. Note that
\mathbf{M}(S_{i}\times T_{i})<\mu\mu^{\prime}
. The triangle inequality implies that

\mathcal{F}(S_{0}\times T_{0},S_{1}\times T_{1})\leq\mathcal{F}(S_{0}(T_{0}-T_{1}))+\mathcal{F}((S_{0}-S_{1})T_{1})\leq\mu\mathcal{F}(T_{0},T_{1})+\mu^{\prime}\mathcal{F}(S_{0},S_{1}).

This implies that
c:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\times\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})^{\mu^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k+k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M\times M^{\prime},M\times N^{\prime}\cup N\times M^{\prime})^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}
is continuous in the flat topology in the codomain, and the product topology of the flat topologies in the domain, for all
\mu,\mu^{\prime}>0
. The lemma statement follows.
∎

Let
\wedge
denote the smash product, and let
(-)^{\wedge k}
denote the
k
-fold smash power.

Proposition 5.9
.

The map
f:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})^{m}\to\mathcal{Z}_{mk}^{\mathrm{}}((\mathbb{D}^{n})^{m},\partial(\mathbb{D}^{n})^{m})
is a Brown representative for the
m
-fold cross product. In other words,
f^{*}(\iota_{m(n-k)})=\iota_{n-k}^{\otimes m}
.

Likewise, the map
g:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{m}\to\mathcal{Z}_{mk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{mn})
defined by
g(T_{1},\dotsc,T_{m})=T_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge T_{m}
is also a Brown representative for the
m
-fold cross product.

Proof.

We will prove this for
f
, as the proof is similar for
g
. The continuity of
f
follows from
lemma

5.8
.
A priori
,
f^{*}(\iota_{m(n-k)})
is a class in degree
m(n-k)
, so by the Künneth formula it must be the sum of classes of the form
\gamma_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes\gamma_{m}
. Let us first show that each
\gamma_{i}
must have positive degree: if not, suppose without loss of generality that
\gamma_{1}
has degree 0. Then we may evaluate
f^{*}(\iota_{m(n-k)})
on a cycle
A_{1}\times\dotsb\times A_{m}
where
A_{i}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
, but
A_{1}
is a linear combination of cycles, which we may assume is the zero cycle. Clearly
f(A_{1}\times\dotsb\times A_{m})=0
by the formula for
f
.

Thus each
\gamma_{i}
has degree at least
n-k
, which forces
f^{*}(\iota_{m(n-k)})
to be
a\iota_{n-k}^{\otimes m}
for some
a\neq 0
. To compute
a
, consider the canonical map
h:\mathbb{S}^{n-k}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})
defined by
h(x)=(\{x\}\times\mathbb{D}^{k})\cap\mathbb{D}^{n}
. It can be checked using
theorem

4.13
that
h^{*}(\iota_{n-k})
is a generator
\alpha\in H^{n-k}(\mathbb{S}^{n-k})
. In fact, if
h^{m}
denotes the induced map
(\mathbb{S}^{n-k})^{m}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{m}
, then it can be verified using
theorem

4.13
that
f\circ h^{m}
is a Brown representative for
\alpha^{\otimes m}
. Thus
a=1
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

The diagonal map
\Delta:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
, defined by
T\mapsto(T,T)
, is continuous.

Proof.

For any
T\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
and
\mu>\mathbf{M}(T)
,
(T,T)\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
. Clearly the diagonal map
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
is continuous in the flat metric. By
remark

5.6
the diagonal map
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is continuous with respect to the flat topology in the domain and the inductive limit topology in the codomain. Thus by
section

2.2
, the diagonal map
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is continuous.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

The following maps are Brown representatives for the
p
-fold cup power.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}((\mathbb{D}^{n})^{p},\partial(\mathbb{D}^{n})^{p})

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{m}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Z}_{mk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{mn})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto T^{p}

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto T^{\wedge p}

Proof.

This follows from
lemma

5.10
and the definition of the cup product in terms of the cross product and the diagonal map
[
22
, p. 279]
.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

Suppose that for
i=1,\dotsb,q
,
f_{i}:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k_{i}}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n_{i}},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n_{i}})
is a Brown representative for
\alpha_{i}\in H^{n_{i}-k_{i}}(X)
. Then the class
\alpha_{1}\smile\dotsb\smile\alpha_{q}
has a Brown representative which is the following composite map:

X\xrightarrow{\Delta}X^{q}\xrightarrow{f_{1}\times\dotsb\times f_{q}}\prod_{i}\mathcal{Z}_{k_{i}}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{n_{i}},\partial\mathbb{D}^{n_{i}})\xrightarrow{c}\mathcal{Z}_{k_{1}+\dotsb+k_{q}}^{\mathrm{}}(\textstyle\prod_{i}\mathbb{D}^{n_{i}},\partial(\prod_{i}\mathbb{D}^{n_{i}})),

where
\Delta(x)=(x,\dotsc,x)
and
c(T_{1},\dotsc,T_{q})=T_{1}\times\dotsb\times T_{q}
.

Remark 5.13
.

Corollary

5.12
can be combined with
lemmas

5.1
and
5.3
to obtain Brown representatives of every cohomology class in any real or complex projective space and any lens space, by using the cup product structure on their cohomology rings.

6
The Cyclic Product Map and the Bockstein Homomorphisms

Let us define the map
\mathit{cyc}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
from
theorem

1.3
formally. First let us define it over
polyhedral cycles
, i.e. the cycles formed from linear combinations of the faces of a fine cubical structure on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Consider the diagonal
\Delta=\{(x,\dots,x)~|~x\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\}\in\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}
and let
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
denote the open
\epsilon
-neighbourhood of
\Delta
.

We will need the following “equivariant isoperimetric inequality”:

Theorem 6.1
.

For any
0\leq k\leq p(n+1)-3
, let
R\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1})
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant cycle that has an invariant filling and a filling
Q
of mass
\varepsilon=\mathbf{M}(Q)\leq 1
. Then for any
m>4pn
, there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant chain
S
in
\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(S)\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-2pn}{m}}

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(R-\partial S)\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-2pn}{m}}+\mathbf{M}(R\mathbin{\llcorner}\Delta_{2pn\varepsilon^{1/m}}).

Proof.

Let
F
be the uncontrolled invariant filling. Since
F-Q
is a
(k+1)
-cycle and
k+1<p(n+1)-1
, it can be filled by a
(k+2)
-chain
G
. Using the coarea inequality, choose some
r\in[2pn\varepsilon^{1/m},(2pn+1)\varepsilon^{1/m}]
such that
\mathbf{M}(Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\Delta_{r})\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-1}{m}}
.

Take the standard
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant cell structure on
\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)(n+1)-1}
, find the product cell structure on
\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)(n+1)-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. Let
X_{d}
be the preimage over
f
of the
d
-skeleton of
\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)(n+1)-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. Given any sequence of distances
r_{n+1},\dotsc,r_{p(n+1)-1}
, we can define a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant filtration

\emptyset\subset Y_{n+1}\subset Y_{n+2}\subset\dotsb\subset Y_{p(n+1)-1}=\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}_{r},\qquad\text{where }Y_{i}=\overline{\bigcup_{j=n+1}^{i}N_{r_{j}}(X_{j})\setminus\Delta_{r}}.

(6.1)

We will modify
Q
in each successively higher elements of the filtration. More precisely, we will inductively construct, for each
n+1\leq d\leq p(n+1)-1
, a distance
r_{d}
and a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant
(k+1)
-chain
Q_{d}
such that:

•

r_{d}\in[(2pn-i)\varepsilon^{1/m},(2pn-d+1)\varepsilon^{1/m}]
.

•

Q_{d}
is a relative filling of
R\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d}
. That is,
Q_{d}
is supported in
Y_{d}
and
\partial Q_{d}-R\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d}
is suported in
\partial Y_{d}
.

•

Q_{d}
is relatively homologous to
F\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d}
. That is, there is some chain
G_{d}
such that
\partial G_{d}+Q_{d}-F\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d}
is supported in
\partial Y_{d}
.

•

We have the bounds

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(Q_{d})\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-d}{m}}

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial N_{r_{d}}(X_{d}))\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-1}{m}}

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(Q_{d}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial N_{r_{d}}(X_{d}))\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-d}{m}},

(6.4)

and, for
d>n+1
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}(\partial N_{r_{d-1}}(X_{d-1})\cap\partial N_{r_{d}}(X_{d})))\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-d}{m}}.

(6.5)

Observe that if all of the
r_{d}
’s are chosen from the stipulated intervals, then each
Y_{i}\setminus Y_{i-1}
is the disjoint union of
p
components, each homeomorphic to a disk. For the base case of
d=n+1
: use the coarea inequality to choose
r_{n+1}\in[(2pn-n-1)\varepsilon^{1/m},(2pn-n)\varepsilon^{1/m}]
to satisfy
eq.

6.3
. Then
Y_{n+1}
is homeomorphic to
p
disks
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{p}
, and the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
action maps each disk homeomorphically to another. Thus we can simply replace each
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{i}
with the image of
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{1}
to get the required
Q_{1}
. Thus
eqs.

6.2
and
6.4
are also satisfied. We can also define
G_{n+1}
by replacing each
G\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{i}
with an image of
G\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{1}
.

For the induction step, assume that the induction hypothesis holds for
d-1
. Use the simultaneous coarea inequality to slice
Q
,
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial N_{r_{d-1}}(X_{d-1})
, and
Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial N_{r_{d-1}}(X_{d-1})
, so we choose
r_{d}\in[(2pn-d)\varepsilon^{1/m},(2pn-d+1)\varepsilon^{1/m}]
to satisfy the bounds
eqs.

6.3
and
6.5
and the following:

\mathbf{M}(Q\mathbin{\llcorner}(\partial N_{r_{d-1}}(X_{d-1})\cap\partial N_{r_{d}}(X_{d})))\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-2}{m}}.

(6.6)

Observe that
\overline{Y_{d}\setminus Y_{d-1}}
is
p
disjoint disks
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{p}
.
D_{1}
and
Y_{d-1}
have disjoint interiors but share part of their boundary at
B=\partial Y_{d-1}\cap\partial D_{1}=\partial Y_{d-1}\cap N_{r_{d}}(e^{d})
for some
d
-cell
e^{d}
of
X_{d}
. Thus
B
is homeomorphic, and nearly 1-bilipschitz, to
S^{n+1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-n-2}\times\mathbb{D}^{p(n+1)-1-d}(r_{d}))
.

Let us assume that
B
and
\partial B
intersect
Q
,
Q_{d-1}
, and
R
transversally. Then
\partial(Q\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{1})=R\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{1}+Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial D_{1}
and
\partial Q_{d-1}=R\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d-1}+Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial Y_{d-1}
. It can be shown that
(Q-Q_{d-1})\mathbin{\llcorner}B
is a relative cycle, because

\displaystyle\partial(Q\mathbin{\llcorner}B)-\partial(Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}B)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle R\mathbin{\llcorner}B+Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B-R\mathbin{\llcorner}B-Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B-Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B,

and
\partial B\subset\partial Y_{d}
. Let us find a small relative filling of this relative cycle. Observe that
\partial B\subset\partial N_{r_{d-1}}(X_{d-1})\cap\partial N_{r_{d}}(X_{d})
, so by
eqs.

6.5
and
6.6
,
\mathbf{M}(Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B-Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B)\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-d}{m}}
. Moreover,
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B-Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B
is a null-homologous cycle because both
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B
and
Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B
are homologous to
F\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B
by the induction hypothesis. Hence, by the isoperimetric inequality (this holds because
\frac{m-d}{k}\geq 1
and so we can apply the deformation theorem with a grid size less than
r_{d}
)),
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B-Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B
can be filled by a chain of mass at most
C\varepsilon^{\frac{k(m-d)}{(k-1)m}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-d}{m}}
.

Adding that filling to
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}B-Q_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}B
, we get a cycle of mass at most
C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-d}{m}}
by
eq.

6.3
and
eq.

6.4
from the
d-1
case. Since this cycle lies in
D_{1}
which is nearly 1-bilipschitz to
S^{n+1}(\mathbb{D}^{d-n-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{p(n+1)-1-d}(r_{d}))
, it can be coned off by a chain in
D_{1}
of mass at most
C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-d}{m}}
. Add this to
Q_{d-1}
and the images of
Q\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{1}
to get
Q_{d}
. One can verify that
Q_{d}
is a relative filling of
R\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d}
in
Y_{d}
, and that
eqs.

6.2
and
6.4
hold.

It remains to check that
Q_{d}
is relatively homologous to
F\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d}
. This is true because
Q_{d}-F\mathbin{\llcorner}Y_{d}
can be filled by
G_{d}
which is the sum of
G_{d-1}+G\mathbin{\llcorner}D_{1}
with the cone of
G_{d-1}\mathbin{\llcorner}B+G\mathbin{\llcorner}B
inside
D_{1}
.

Therefore we now have a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant chain
Q_{p(n+1)-1}
in
\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}_{r}
such that
\partial Q_{p(n+1)-1}-R
is supported in
\Delta_{r}
, and by
eq.

6.4
,
\mathbf{M}(\partial Q_{p(n+1)-1}-R)\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{m-p(n+1)+1}{m}}+\mathbf{M}(R\mathbin{\llcorner}\Delta_{r})
.
∎

To formally define
\mathit{cyc}
, we must specify the homeomorphism
h:\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}\setminus\Delta\to L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
from
eq.

1.3
, and estimate its Jacobian. It is covered by a smooth embedding
f:\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}\setminus\Delta\to\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)(n+1)-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
that is defined using a real analogue of the Discrete Fourier Transform Matrix. Let
\theta=2\pi/p
, and consider the
real discrete Fourier transform matrix

F
defined as follows. When
p=2
, define
F=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. When
p>2
, define

F=\begin{bmatrix}u&v_{1}&w_{1}&v_{2}&w_{2}&\cdots&v_{\frac{p-1}{2}}&w_{\frac{p-1}{2}}\end{bmatrix}^{T},

whose rows are, for
j=1,\dotsc,p-1
,

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{p}}(1,\dotsc,1)

(6.7)

\displaystyle v_{j}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{p}}(1,\cos(j\theta),\cos(2j\theta),\dotsc,\cos((p-1)j\theta))

\displaystyle w_{j}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{p}}(0,\sin(j\theta),\sin(2j\theta),\dotsc,\sin((p-1)j\theta)).

Let
F^{\perp}
denote
F
with the first row removed, and
F^{\perp}_{m}=F^{\perp}\otimes I_{m}
, and
F_{m}=F\otimes I_{m}
, where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product. Then
f
is defined by the formula

f(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})=\left(\frac{F^{\perp}_{n+1}x}{\lVert F^{\perp}_{n+1}x\rVert},\frac{x_{1}+\dotsb+x_{p}}{p}\right).

(6.8)

Write
\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}
, and let
f_{\varepsilon}=f|_{\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}_{\varepsilon}}
. In particular, the image of
f_{\varepsilon}
is
\mathbb{S}^{(n+1)(p-1)-1}\times B_{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{2}}}(0)
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\tilde{\Delta}=\{(x,\dotsc,x)~|~x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\}
. Consider any
v\in\Delta_{\varepsilon}
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. Then if the domain of
Df_{v}:\mathbb{R}^{p(n+1)}\to\mathbb{R}^{p(n+1)}
is decomposed as
\mathbb{R}^{p(n+1)}=\tilde{\Delta}\oplus\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp}
and the codomain is given some permutation of coordinates, then
Df_{v}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}I_{n+1}\oplus\frac{1}{\varepsilon}I_{(p-1)(n+1)}
.

Proof.

f
extends onto a composite map

\mathbb{R}^{p(n+1)}\setminus\tilde{\Delta}\xrightarrow{F_{n+1}}\mathbb{R}^{p(n+1)}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{0\}^{(p-1)(n+1)}\xrightarrow{(x,y)\mapsto\left(\frac{y}{\lVert y\rVert},\frac{x}{\sqrt{p}}\right)}\mathbb{S}^{(n+1)(p-1)-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Observe that
F_{n+1}
is an isometry that maps
\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}
to the set
\{(x,y)~|~x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},y\in\mathbb{R}^{(p-1)(n+1)},\lVert x\rVert=\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{2}},\lVert y\rVert=\varepsilon\}
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. The lemma statement follows.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
A,B
be vector subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
(A+B)^{\perp}=A^{\perp}\cap B^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Observe that
(A+B)^{\perp}\subset A^{\perp}
and
(A+B)^{\perp}\subset B^{\perp}
, so
(A+B)^{\perp}\subset A^{\perp}\cap B^{\perp}
. For the reverse inclusion, let
v\in A^{\perp}\cap B^{\perp}
. Then for all
u+w\in A+B
, where
u\in A
and
w\in B
,
v\perp u
and
v\perp w
so
v\perp w+u
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\Pi_{1},\dotsc,\Pi_{p}
be subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Then the rank of the orthogonal projection
\pi:\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\to\tilde{\Delta}
is at least
\frac{1}{p}(\dim\Pi_{1}+\dotsb+\dim\Pi_{p})
. Equality is attained when
\Pi_{1}=\dotsb=\Pi_{p}
.

Proof.

Let
d=\dim\Pi_{1}+\dotsb+\dim\Pi_{p}
.

By
lemma

6.3
,

\displaystyle\rank\pi

\displaystyle=d-\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=d-p(n+1)+\dim(\tilde{\Delta}+\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}^{\perp}))

\displaystyle=d-p(n+1)+\underbrace{\dim\tilde{\Delta}}_{n+1}+\underbrace{\dim(\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}^{\perp})}_{p(n+1)-d)}-\dim(\tilde{\Delta}\cap\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}^{\perp}))

\displaystyle=n+1-\dim(\tilde{\Delta}\cap\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}^{\perp})).

Thus it remains to prove that
\dim(\tilde{\Delta}\cap\Pi_{1}^{\perp}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}^{\perp}))\leq n+1-d/p
. By the rank-nullity theorem, this is equivalent to proving that the dimension of the image of the orthogonal projection
\tilde{\Delta}\to\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}
is at least
d/p
. To show this, note that the Pigeonhole Principle implies that some
\Pi_{i}
must have dimension at least
d/p
. Consider the composite projection
\tilde{\Delta}\to\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\to\Pi_{i}
.
The image of this composite projection is surjective because for each vector
v\in\Pi_{i}
,
(v,\dotsc,v)
projects to
v

When
\Pi_{1}=\dotsb=\Pi_{p}
, by applying a linear isomorphism to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we may assume that
\Pi_{i}=\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1-m}
for some fixed
m
. Then the orthogonal projection
\tilde{\Delta}\to\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}
fixes
(e_{i},\dotsc,e_{i})
when
i\leq m
and kills it otherwise. Thus the rank of this projection is exactly
m
.
∎

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
R\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
be a polyhedral cycle, where
k\geq 1
. Then for sufficiently small
r
, there exists a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\Delta_{r}))\leq C\mathbf{M}(R)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\Delta_{r}))\leq Cr\mathbf{M}(R).

Proof.

Let the grid size be
\lambda
. Let
k
-cells of
R
as
e^{k}_{1},\dotsc,e^{k}_{q}
. Let
e^{k}_{i}
appear with multiplicity
a_{i}
in
R
. (Note that
\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\leq p
.) Thus
\spt R^{p}=(\spt R)^{p}
is a
pk
-dimensional cubical complex whose top-dimensional cells are of the form
e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}
.

For each
0\leq d\leq k
, let
K_{d}=4^{k-d+1}
and let
V_{d}
(resp.
\frac{1}{2}V_{d}
) be the
K_{d}\varepsilon
-neighbourhood (resp.
\frac{1}{2}K_{d}\varepsilon
-neighbourhood), in the
\ell^{\infty}
metric, of the
d
-skeleton of
R
. Note that for all
1\leq d\leq k
,
K_{d-1}=4K_{d}
.

let
e^{d}_{1},\dotsc,e^{d}_{q_{d}}
denote the
d
-cells of
R
, for
0\leq d\leq k
. Let
a_{i}
denote the multiplicity of
e^{k}_{i}
in
R
. Observe that for all
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})\in\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}
, any
x_{j}
and
x_{k}
must be distance at most
2\varepsilon
apart. Then

Lemma 6.6
.

For each
1\leq d\leq k
and for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
,
\overline{V_{d}\setminus\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}}
is a disjoint union of cuboids, each cuboid containing
e^{d}_{i}\setminus\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}
for a unique
i
. The cuboid
E^{d}_{i}
consists of points at
\ell^{\infty}
distance at most
K_{d}\varepsilon
from
e^{d}_{i}
, and whose orthogonal projection onto
e^{d}_{i}
lies in
\overline{e^{d}_{i}\setminus\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}}
.

Furthermore, the cuboids are further than distance
2\varepsilon
away from each other.

Proof.

First we show that those “cuboid” sets defined above are actually cuboids. Without loss of generality, and by the invariance of the
\ell^{\infty}
metric under coordinate permutations and translations, we may consider
e^{d}_{1}
and assume that it is
[0,\delta]^{d}\times\{0\}^{n-d}
. Then the “cuboid sets” are
[\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon,\delta-\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon]^{d}\times[-K_{d}\varepsilon,K_{d}\varepsilon]^{n-d}
. These are indeed cuboids.

Next we show that the union of the cuboids is indeed
\overline{V_{d}\setminus\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}}
. To prove the inclusion, first note that the union of the cuboids is certainly contained in
V_{d}
. Each cuboid is also disjoint from the interior of
\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}
. To see this, again use the above translation and change of coordinates; then since each point in the cuboid orthogonally projects onto
e^{d}_{1}
to give a point in
[\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon,\delta-\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon]^{d}\times\{0\}^{n-d}
, its first
d
coordinates must be in the interval
[\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon,\delta-\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon]
, which means that the point itself must be at
\ell^{\infty}
distance at least
\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon
from
\partial e^{d}_{1}
.

To prove the reverse inclusion, consider any
x\in\overline{V_{d}\setminus\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}}
. Then
x
must lie within
\ell^{\infty}
distance
K_{d}\varepsilon
from some
e^{d}_{i}
. Do the same coordinate transformations as before to get
e^{d}_{i}=[0,\delta]^{d}\times\{0\}^{n-d}
, so that
x\in[-K_{d}\varepsilon,\delta+K_{d}\varepsilon]^{d}\times\{-K_{d}\varepsilon,K_{d}\varepsilon\}^{n-d}
. Since
x
must be further than
\ell^{\infty}
distance
\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon=2K_{d}\varepsilon
from every face of
e^{d}_{i}
, for each
1\leq j\leq d
we must have that
x
lies outside

[-2K_{d}\varepsilon,\delta+2K_{d}\varepsilon]^{j-1}\times([-2K_{d}\varepsilon,2K_{d}\varepsilon]\cup[\delta-2K_{d}\varepsilon,\delta+2K_{d}\varepsilon])\\
\times[-2K_{d}\varepsilon,\delta+2K_{d}\varepsilon]^{d-j}\times[-2K_{d}\varepsilon,2K_{d}\varepsilon]^{n-d}.

Therefore the
j^{\text{th}}
coordinate of
x
must lie in
[\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon,\delta-\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon]
. Since this is true for each
j
, we have that the projection of
x
onto
e^{d}_{i}
lies outside
V_{d-1}
.

Finally, we show that the cuboids are at distance at least
2\varepsilon
away from each other. For that we need the following lemma about cubic lattices:

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider the standard cubic lattice of edge length
\delta
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Suppose that the cube
c=[0,\delta]^{r}\times\{0\}^{n-r}
intersects but does not contain another cube
c^{\prime}
. Then either for some
i\leq r
the
i^{\text{th}}
factor of
c^{\prime}
is
[-\delta,0]
or
[\delta,2\delta]
, or for some
i>r
the
i^{\text{th}}
factor of
c^{\prime}
is
[-\delta,0]
or
[0,\delta]
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise for the sake of contradiction. Then
c^{\prime}=A_{i}\times\dotsb\times A_{r}\times\{(x_{r+1},\dotsc,x_{n})\}
, where since
c
intersects
c^{\prime}
, each
A_{i}
is either
[0,\delta]
or a point that has to be either 0 or
\delta
, and each
x_{i}=0
. That implies that
c^{\prime}\subset c
, giving a contradiction.
∎

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that, without loss of generality, the cuboids associated with distinct
d
-cells
e^{d}_{1}
and
e^{d}_{2}
contain points
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
respectively such that
\lVert x_{1}-x_{2}\rVert\leq 2\varepsilon
. Then the triangle inequality implies that
e^{d}_{1}
and
e^{d}_{2}
are within
\ell^{\infty}
distance
(2K_{d}+2)\varepsilon
apart, which for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
implies that
e^{d}_{1}
and
e^{d}_{2}
must touch.
Lemma

6.7
implies that, without loss of generality via a reflection, some factor of
e^{d}_{2}
is
[-\delta,0]
. Thus that coordinate of
E^{d}_{2}
must be at most
-\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon=-2\cdot K_{d}\varepsilon
, while that coordinate of
E^{d}_{1}
must be at least
-K_{d}\varepsilon
. Hence the distance between
E^{d}_{1}
and
E^{d}_{2}
is at least
K_{d}\varepsilon>2\varepsilon
, giving a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

For sufficiently small
\varepsilon
,

(\spt R)^{p}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}\subset\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q_{d}\\
0\leq d\leq k\\
e^{d}_{l}\subset e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}\end{subarray}}(E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}.

Proof.

For any
x=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})\in(\spt R)^{p}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}
, choose the largest
d
such that some
x_{i}
lies in
V_{d}\setminus V_{d-1}
. This implies that every
x_{j}
also lies in
V_{d}
. In addition,
x_{i}
must lie in some
E^{d}_{l}
by
lemma

6.6
, and
x_{i}
is further than distance
K_{d-1}\varepsilon
away from the
(d-1)
-skeleton of
R
. This implies that every
x_{j}
must be further than distance
\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon
away from the
(d-1)
-skeleton of
R
, because
\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon=2\cdot 4^{k-d+1}\varepsilon>2\varepsilon
. Thus
x_{j}
lies in
V_{d}\setminus\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}
. Together with
lemma

6.6
and the fact that
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
must be within distance
2\varepsilon
of each other, this implies that
x_{j}\in E^{d}_{l}
. Moreover,
x_{j}
must lie in some
e^{k}_{i_{j}}
. Consequently,
x\in(E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}
.

It remains to show that
e^{d}_{l}\subset e^{k}_{i_{j}}
for all
j
. Assume for the sake of contradiction that this is false for some
e^{k}_{i_{j}}
. Nevertheless,
e^{k}_{i_{j}}
has a point
x_{j}
that is also contained in
E^{d}_{l}
, so it is within distance
K_{d}\varepsilon
of a point in
\overline{e^{d}_{l}\setminus\frac{1}{2}V_{d-1}}
. For sufficiently small
\varepsilon
this implies that
e^{k}_{i_{j}}
intersects but does not contain
e^{d}_{l}
. Perform coordinate transformations until
e^{k}_{i_{j}}=[0,\lambda]^{k}\times\{0\}^{n-k}
. By
lemma

6.7
and without loss of generality due to a reflection,
e^{d}_{l}
has some factor that is
[-\lambda,0]
. However, that would imply that that coordinate of
E^{d}_{2}
would be at most
-\frac{1}{2}K_{d-1}\varepsilon=-2\cdot K_{d}\varepsilon
, so actually
e^{k}_{i_{j}}
and
E^{d}_{l}
have to be further than distance
K_{d}\varepsilon
apart, giving a contradiction.
∎

Let
\Pi_{j}
be the tangent space of
e^{k}_{i_{j}}
. (Assume that the cells are nearly flat so we can identify all the tangent spaces.)

Lemma 6.9
.

For any
1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q
and
v
in the interior of
e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}
,

J_{v}f|_{e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}}=\frac{(\sqrt{p})^{pk-\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})}}{\varepsilon^{\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})-1}}\leq\frac{p^{pk/2}}{\varepsilon^{\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})-1}}.

Proof.

From
lemma

6.2
, only the stretching in the direction of
\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp}
matters, where
\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal complement to
\tilde{\Delta}
at
v
. The result follows.
∎

R^{p}
has a finite number of tangent
pk
-dimensional planes at each point. Each tangent
pk
-plane is (affinely) spanned by a product of
k
-cells
e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}
. For each
v\in D
, let
S_{v}
denote the sphere of radius
\varepsilon
around
v
in the orthogonal complement to
\tilde{\Delta}
at
p
.

Lemma 6.10
.

For each
1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q
, suppose that
e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}
contains some
e^{d}_{l}
for some and
0\leq d\leq k
. Then

\mathbf{M}((E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon})\leq C\lambda^{d}\varepsilon^{pk-d-1}

Proof.

The cells share at least one vertex, and assuming that the cells are small, and by applying a rotation of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we may assume that
e_{1}\in e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}
where
e_{1}=(1,0,\dotsc,0)
. By applying a diffeomorphism with bilipschitz constant close to 1, we may assume that the cells are affine subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that are orthogonal to
e_{1}
and that
\Delta
can be identified with the part of its span
\tilde{\Delta}
near
(e_{1},\dotsc,e_{1})
, whose linear part is spanned by the vectors
d_{i}=(e_{i},\dotsc,e_{i})
for
2\leq i\leq n+1
. By a further rotation of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we may assume that the edges of the cubical structure are parallel to the standard basis vectors.

(E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}
is a
pk
-dimensional cuboid whose edges in the direction of
(E^{d}_{l})^{p}
have length
\lambda
, and whose other edges have length
(k-d)\varepsilon
. whose affine span is the
pk
-plane
\Pi
.

We will apply the coarea formula for the orthogonal projection
\pi:(E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}\to\Delta
. Thus we may study the differential of the projection,
D_{v}\pi
, for
v=(v_{1},\dotsc,v_{p})\in e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}
:
\ker D_{v}\pi=\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp}
. Thus the fibers of
\pi
are the intersections of the domain with the planes
\{v+\ker D_{v}\pi\}
. In particular, this implies that
\dim(\im\pi)=pk-\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})
.

Let the tangent spaces of
e^{d}_{l}
be
W
. When
D_{v}\pi
is restricted to
W^{p}
,
lemma

6.4
implies that its image has dimension
d
.

Since
\pi
is 1-Lipschitz,
\vol(\pi((e^{d}_{l})^{p}))\leq C\lambda^{d}
. Since every point in
(E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}
is distance at most
CK_{d}\varepsilon
from
(e^{d}_{l})^{p}
, the image of
\pi
is an affine set of dimension
pk-\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})
that is within distance
(k-d)\varepsilon
from a flat
d
-dimensional disk of volume at most
C\lambda^{d}
, so
\vol(\im\pi)\leq C\lambda^{d}\varepsilon^{pk-d-\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})}
. Now, since each fiber of
\pi
is part of a sphere of radius
\varepsilon
and dimension
\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})-1
, the volume of the fiber is at most
C\varepsilon^{\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})-1}
. Therefore the coarea inequality implies that

\mathbf{M}((E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon})\leq C\lambda^{d}\varepsilon^{pk-d-1}.

∎

Then by the area formula,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\spt R^{p}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}}\theta_{R^{p}}(v)Jf|_{\spt R^{p}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}}\,dv

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:CartPowerNearDiag_BlockDecomp}})

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q_{d}\\
0\leq d\leq k\\
e^{d}_{l}\subset e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}\end{subarray}}\int_{(E^{d}_{l})^{p}\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}}J_{v}f|_{e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}}\,dv

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:CycProdMapJacobian}})

\displaystyle\leq p^{p+k/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q_{d}\\
0\leq d\leq k\\
e^{d}_{l}\subset e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbf{M}((E^{d}_{l})\cap e^{k}_{i_{1}}\times\dotsb\times e^{k}_{i_{p}}\cap\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})-1}},

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:MassCubeSliceNearDiagonal}})

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q_{d}\\
0\leq d\leq k\\
e^{d}_{l}\subset e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{d}\varepsilon^{pk-d-\dim(\Pi_{1}\times\dotsb\times\Pi_{p}\cap\tilde{\Delta}^{\perp})}

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:PlaneProdProjDiagRank}})

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q_{d}\\
0\leq d\leq k\\
e^{d}_{l}\subset e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{d}\varepsilon^{k-d}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\leq q_{d}\\
0\leq d\leq k\\
e^{d}_{l}\subset e^{k}_{i_{1}}\cap\dotsb\cap e^{k}_{i_{p}}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{k}.

Each summand corresponds to
p

k
-cells and a
d
-cell in their intersection, so those
k
-cells must all share some vertex. Thus the number of summands is bounded by the number of vertices in
R
times the number of
k
-cells around a vertex, times the number of lower-dimensional cells in a
k
-cell. This is all bounded by a constant
C(n)
times the number of
k
-cells in
R
, which is at most
\mathbf{M}(R)/\lambda^{k}
. Altogether, this implies that
\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon}))\leq C\mathbf{M}(R)
.

\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\Delta_{r}))
can be bounded using the coarea inequality by integrating
\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\Delta_{s}))
for
0\leq s\leq r
.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

For each mod
p
polyhedral cycle
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, let
R
be one of its representatives mod
p
. Then the following hold:

1.

The sequence of integral chains
S_{1},S_{2},\dotsc
, where
S_{i}=f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}_{1/i})
, converges in the flat topology to an integral chain
S
in
\mathbb{S}^{(n+1)(p-1)-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
that is
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(S)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbf{M}(R)^{p}}{r_{0}^{pk}}+Cr_{0}\mathbf{M}(R)

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\partial S)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbf{M}(R)

2.

The image of
S
under the covering map
