\mathbb{S}^{(n+1)(p-1)-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}\to L\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
is
pT
for some flat chain
T
in
L\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
.

3.

[T]
is a mod
p
relative cycle in
\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
.

Proof.

This result is immediate for 0-cycles as they are formal sums of points. Henceforth we assume that
k\geq 1
. First let us establish that each
S_{i}
is an integral current mod
p
. By the Closure Theorem
[
10
, p. 432]
, it suffices to show that
\mathbf{M}(S_{i})
and
\mathbf{M}(\partial S_{i})
are finite. The former is true because
R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}_{1/i}
has finite mass and over its support,
f
has Jacobian bounded by some power of
i
by
lemma

6.9
. The latter is true because
\partial\Delta_{1/i}
intersects
R^{p}
transversally so that the intersection has finite mass, and the Jacobian of
f
has a uniform bound over the intersection.

The sequence
S_{i}
is Cauchy in the mass metric, and thus also in the flat metric: Fix any integer
k
, and consider any
i>j>k
. Then
theorem

6.5
implies that
\mathbf{M}(S_{i}-S_{j})\leq C\mathbf{M}(R)/k
. To apply the Compactness Theorem for integral currents mod
p

[
10
, p. 432]
to the Cauchy sequence
S_{i}
, we need uniform bounds on
\mathbf{M}(S_{i})
and
\mathbf{M}(\partial S_{i})
. Let
r_{0}
be the threshold for “sufficiently small” in
theorem

6.5
. We may drop initial terms of the sequence until every
1/i<r_{0}
. Then
theorem

6.5
and
lemma

6.2
imply that

\mathbf{M}(S_{i})\leq\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}_{r_{0}}))+\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\Delta_{r_{0}}))\leq\frac{\mathbf{M}(R)^{p}}{r_{0}^{pk}}+Cr_{0}\mathbf{M}(R).

Additionally,
theorem

6.5
implies that
\mathbf{M}(\partial S_{i})=\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(R^{p}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\Delta_{1/i}))\leq C\mathbf{M}(R)
. Thus the Compactness Theorem guarantees that
S_{i}
converges in the flat topology to an integral chain in
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1};\mathcal{F})
.

It can be verified that the
S_{i}
’s are restrictions of
S
. Since each of the
S_{i}
’s are
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant,
S
is also
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant. As a result, the image of
S
under the covering map is
pT
for some flat chain
T
in
L\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. It can be verified that
T
is a mod
p
relative cycle.
∎

Let
\mathit{cyc}
denote the map from
proposition

6.11
that maps polyhedral cycles to
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
to
\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
. Let
\mathbb{D}^{m}_{r}
denote a closed
m
-disk of radius
r
.

Proposition 6.12
.

\mathit{cyc}
is continuous over the space of polyhedral cycles. It also extends to a continuous map
\mathit{cyc}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
.

Proof.

For any
0<\varepsilon<1
and
\mu>0
, let
\delta=\varepsilon^{5pn}/(\mu+1)^{6pn}<1
. Consider any polyhedral cycles
R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
within flat distance
\delta
of each other, such that
\mathbf{M}(R_{1}),\mathbf{M}(R_{2})<\mu
. Without loss of generality, assume that they are within the same grid size. By the isoperimetric inequality,
R_{1}-R_{2}
is filled by a chain
Q
of mass at most
C(\delta+\delta^{\frac{k+1}{k}})\leq C\delta
.

Expanding
R_{1}^{p}=(R_{2}+\partial Q)^{p}
using the binomial theorem gives a sum of monomials that, other than
R_{2}^{p}
and
(\partial Q)^{p}
, can be groups of
p
terms so that each group consists of different cyclic permutations of the same term. Consider any such group, whose sum is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant cycle
G
. Each term in the group must have at least one factor of
\partial Q
, so one of the terms must be
\partial Q\times A_{2}\times\dotsb\times A_{p}=\partial(Q\times A_{2}\times\dotsb\times A_{p})
, where each
A_{i}
is either
\partial Q
or
R_{2}
. Thus
G
can be filled by
\sum_{\text{cyc}}Q\times A_{2}\times\dotsb\times A_{p}
, a chain of mass at most
C\delta\mu^{p-1}
. Similarly,
(\partial Q)^{p}
can be filled by
Q\times(\partial Q^{p-1}
which is a chain of mass at most
C\delta\mu^{p-1}
. Summing over all such groups
G
and
(\partial Q)^{p}
, we obtain that
R_{1}^{p}-R_{2}^{p}
is an invariant cycle with a filling by a chain of mass at most
C\delta\mu^{p-1}
.

By
theorem

6.1
for
m=5pn
, there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-invariant chain
S
in
\mathbb{S}^{p(n+1)-1}
of mass at most
C(\delta\mu^{p-1})^{3/5}
such that
\mathbf{M}(R_{1}^{p}-R_{2}^{p}-\partial S)\leq C(\delta\mu^{p-1})^{3/5}+\mathbf{M}((R_{1}^{p}-R_{2}^{p})\mathbin{\llcorner}\Delta_{r})
, where
r=2pn(\delta\mu^{p-1})^{1/5pn}
. Pushing all these chains by
f_{\sharp}
,
lemma

6.2
implies that that the Jacobian of
f|_{\spt S}
is at most
1/r^{k+1}\leq C(\delta\mu^{p-1})^{2/5}
. Thus
\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}(S))\leq C\delta\mu^{p-1}\leq C\varepsilon^{5pn}(\frac{\mu}{\mu+1})^{p-1}/(\mu+1)^{6pn-p+1}\leq C\varepsilon
. Similarly, if we let
\tilde{T}_{i}
be the lift of
\mathit{cyc}(R_{i})
over the universal cover, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\tilde{T}_{1}-\tilde{T}_{2}-\partial S)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon+\mathbf{M}(f_{\sharp}((R_{1}^{p}-R_{2}^{p})\mathbin{\llcorner}\Delta_{r}))

\displaystyle(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:CartPowerMassNearDiagonal}})

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon+Cr\mu

\displaystyle=C\varepsilon+C(\delta\mu^{p-1})^{1/5pn}\mu

\displaystyle=C\varepsilon+C\varepsilon\left(\frac{\mu^{p-1}}{(\mu+1)^{6pn}}\right)^{1/5pn}\mu

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon+C\varepsilon(\mu+1)^{\frac{p-1+5pn-6pn}{5pn}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon.

Therefore,
\mathcal{F}(\mathit{cyc}(R_{1}),\mathit{cyc}(R_{2}))\leq C\varepsilon/p
.

So far we have established that
\mathit{cyc}
is uniformly continuous in the flat metric as a map from the polyhedral cycles in
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n};\mathcal{F})^{<\mu}=\{T\in\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n};\mathcal{F}):\mathbf{M}(T)<\mu\}
to
\mathcal{I}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1};\mathcal{F})
. Moreover, the closure of the image is compact because of
eq.

6.9
and the Compactness Theorem for integral currents mod
p

[
10
, p. 432]
. Thus
\mathit{cyc}
extends to a continuous map
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n};\mathcal{F})^{<\mu}\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1};\mathcal{F})
. The extensions must be consistent for every value of
\mu
, giving a continuous map
\mathit{cyc}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n};\mathcal{F})\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1};\mathcal{F})
. The proposition then follows.
∎

6.1
Geometric representations of the Bockstein homomorphisms

Now we are in a position to prove
theorem

1.2
. Observe that the map
b
in
eq.

1.2
is equal to
\mathit{cyc}:\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
composed with the map that projects every relative 0-cycle in
L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
onto the second factor
\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. Combined with
proposition

6.12
, this implies that
b
is continuous.

Proof of
theorem

1.2
.

Let us first handle the case where
p>2
. Since
H^{n+1}(K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n),\mathbb{Z}_{p})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, it suffices to prove that
b^{*}(\iota_{n+1})
evaluates to the same value as
\beta(\iota_{n})
on some generator of
H_{n+1}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1});\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Let us first prove this theorem for
n=1
. Let
X
be the mapping cone of the map
\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
defined by
w\mapsto w^{p}
. Define the map
g:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
as follows. Parametrize the points in
X
by
(t,w)
where
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}
and
t\in I
, such that all points
(0,w)
are identified and each point
(1,w)
is identified with
(1,\zeta w)
, where
\zeta=e^{2\pi i/p}
. Define

g(t,w)=\{we^{2\pi itk/p}~|~{-(p-1)/2}\leq k\leq(p-1)/2\}.

(6.10)

This is well-defined because
g(0,w)=0
for all
w
, and
g(1,w)
is
w
times the roots of unity, so
g(1,\zeta w)=g(1,w)
.

g
represents the cohomology class in
g^{*}(\iota_{1})\in H^{1}(X)
, and it evaluates on the 1-skeleton of
X
, denoted by
X^{1}
, by the Almgren isomorphism: gluing
g|_{X^{1}}
into exactly the fundamental homology class of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, i.e.
1\in H_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. (Orientation is preserved.) Let us compute
\beta g^{*}(\iota_{1})\in H^{2}(X)
. Thus we consider the map of short exact sequences among cellular cochains,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

We need to go from the top-right group to the bottom-left group by the snake lemma.
g^{*}(\iota_{1})
is represented by a cochain
\psi
in the top-right group that evaluates to 1 on the 1-cell
e^{1}\subset X
. That cochain is the image of another cochain
\varphi\in C^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}})
that evaluates to
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}
on
e^{1}
. Then
d\varphi
evaluates on the 2-cell
e^{2}
of
X
to
\varphi(pe^{1})=p\varphi(e^{1})=p\in\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}
. Dividing by
p
gives a cochain that evaluates to
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
on
e^{2}
. Therefore
\beta g^{*}(\iota_{1})
has to evaluate to 1 on
e^{2}
.

Let us now evaluate
g^{*}b^{*}(\iota_{2})
on
e^{2}
. It is the same as evaluating
\iota_{2}
on the homology class
[b\circ g]\in H_{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{2}))
represented by the family of cycles
b\circ g:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
. To evaluate the fundamental cohomology class
\iota_{2}
on
[b\circ g]
, observe that over the 1-skeleton of
X
, which is
\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
b\circ g
consists of points that are a uniform distance away from
\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
. This is because each point in
b\circ g(X^{1})
is the barycentre of
p
points
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p}
that are not all the same, and at least one of those points, which we may assume to be
x_{1}
without loss of generality, is at least a distance of
2\pi/p
(along
\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
) away from the others. The barycenter is equal to
\frac{p-1}{p}y+\frac{1}{p}x_{1}
, where
y=\frac{1}{p-1}(x_{2}+\dotsb+x_{p})
. Moreover, the arc of points on
\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
at that distance away from
x
, has a convex hull
K
that contains
y
. Since
\frac{p-1}{p}K+\frac{1}{p}x
is a uniform distance from
\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
, the same is true of the barycenter.

Therefore we can contract
b\circ g(X^{1})
to the origin, and get an equivalent map that is constant over
X^{1}
, which descends to a map
\tilde{b}:X/X^{1}\sim S\mathbb{S}^{1}\sim\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
. Thus we may see
\tilde{b}
as an element
[\tilde{b}]\in\pi_{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{2}))
which is sent to
[b\circ g]
by the Hurewicz homomorphism. For each
w\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
\tilde{b}(-,w):[0,1]\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
for fixed
w
and
t
going from 0 to 1 traces out
m
paths from
w
to the origin. Thus gluing
\tilde{b}
according to the Almgren isomorphism means sweeping each of those paths into a copy of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
in the opposite orientation, eventually giving us
(-m)
times of the fundamental homology class of
(\mathbb{D}^{2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2})
. Thus we want to compute
-m
modulo
p
.

m
is the number of points in
\mathit{cyc}(\text{sum of $p$ distinct points})
, which is the number of orbits of size
p
in
(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{p}
under the cyclic shift action, which is
(p^{p}-p)/p=p^{p-1}-1\equiv-1\pmod{p}
. Therefore the homology class
[b\circ g]
is the image under the Hurewicz homomorphism of
[\tilde{b}]
which corresponds to
-m\equiv 1\in H_{2}(\mathbb{D}^{2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2})
via the Almgren isomorphism. By the definition of fundamental cohomology class
[
27
, p. 3]
,
\iota_{2}([b\circ g])=[\tilde{b}]=1
.

Therefore
g^{*}b^{*}(\iota_{2})=\beta g^{*}(\iota_{1})=g^{*}\beta(\iota_{1})\neq 0
. This implies that
g^{*}
is not the zero map on
H^{2}
, which means it must be an isomorphism on
H^{2}
. Thus
\beta(\iota_{1})=b^{*}(\iota_{2})
. Therefore
b
represents
\beta
. This proves the theorem for
n=1
.

For
n\geq 2
, define the map
G:S^{n-1}X\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(S^{n-1}\mathbb{S}^{1})\sim\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
by
G(s,x)=\{s\}\times g(x)
.
G
represents the cohomology class
G^{*}(\iota_{n})\in H^{n}(X)
, and it evaluates on the
n
-skeleton
(S^{n-1}X)^{n}\sim S^{n-1}\mathbb{S}^{1}
by the Almgren isomorphism: gluing
G|_{(S^{n-1}X)^{n}}
gives exactly the fundamental homology class of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, i.e.
1\in H_{n}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. A similar snake lemma argument as before shows that
\beta G^{*}(\iota_{n})
evaluates to 1 on the
(n+1)
-cell
e^{n+1}
of
S^{n-1}X
.

Same as before, we can evaluate
G^{*}b^{*}(\iota_{n+1})
by evaluating
\iota_{n+1}
on the homology class
[b\circ G]\in H_{n+1}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1}))
represented by the family of cycles
b\circ G:S^{n-1}X\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{rel}}(\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
. Like before, we can contract
(b\circ G)|_{(S^{n-1}X)^{n}}
to
I^{n-1}\times\{0\}\subset S^{n-1}\mathbb{D}^{2}\sim\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
(the origins of every slice) and then push the stack of origins to the side, into
\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
. Then the same arguments as before show that
\iota_{n+1}([b\circ G])=1
. Therefore
G^{*}b^{*}(\iota_{n+1})=\beta G^{*}(\iota_{n})=G^{*}\beta(\iota_{n})\neq 0
. This implies that
G^{*}
is not the zero map on
H^{n+1}
, which means it must be an isomorphism on
H^{n+1}
. Thus
\beta(\iota_{n})=b^{*}(\iota_{n+1})
. Therefore
b
represents
\beta
.

For
p=2
, the proof is largely the same with a few modifications. Observe that the mapping cone
X
is now
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
, and replace
eq.

6.10
with the following formula:

g(t,w)=\{we^{-i\pi t/2},we^{i\pi t/2}\}.

The rest of the proof follows similarly.
∎

7
Geometric Representations of the Steenrod Powers

Our goal for this section is to formalize and prove
theorem

1.3
. It can be rigorously stated as follows:

Theorem 7.1
.

For each prime
p
,
i\geq 0
, and
0\leq k<n
. Let
m=n-k
. Then
P^{i}:H^{m}(-)\to H^{m+2i(p-1)}(-)
and
\beta\circ P^{i}:H^{m}(-)\to H^{m+2i(p-1)+1}(-)
have Brown representatives
\Phi_{pk+m+2i(p-1)}\circ\mathit{cyc}
and
\Phi_{pk+m+2i(p-1)+1}\circ\mathit{cyc}
respectively, where each
\Phi_{q}
is a gluing of some piecewise smooth Brown representative
g_{q}
for a generator of
H^{q}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
.

At a high level, our proof can be thought of as a combination of gluing with the construction of the Steenrod powers using equivariant cohomology.

7.1
The equivariant cohomology characterization of the Steenrod powers

Let us give a brief overview of the equivariant cohomology construction of the Steenrod powers. We will use a minor modification of this construction as presented in
[
22
]
. For a topological space
X
, its
p
-fold smash product
X^{\wedge p}
also admits an action of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
by cyclic permutation. Let us construct a
homotopy quotient
, a homotopy-theoretic analogue of
X^{\wedge p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with “nicer” properties. Let
d
be either
\infty
or an odd integer, and consider the free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
action on the contractible infinite-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{\infty}
corresponding to the covering space
\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{L}_{p}^{d}
. This gives a free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
action on
\mathbb{S}^{d}\times X^{\wedge p}
by acting on each factor simultaneously. The quotient by this action is denoted by
\mathbb{S}^{d}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}
. When
d=\infty
, it is called the homotopy quotient of
X^{\wedge p}
. The cohomology groups
H^{*}(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p})
are the
equivariant cohomology groups
of
X^{\wedge p}
with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
action.

The connection between the Steenrod powers and equivariant cohomology can be appreciated by adapting an analogy offered by Steenrod: for each
\alpha\in H^{n}(X)
, the cup power
\alpha^{p}
can be expressed as the pullback of the
p
-fold cross product
\alpha^{\otimes p}=\alpha\otimes\dotsb\otimes\alpha\in H^{pn}(X^{p})
over the diagonal map
X\hookrightarrow X^{p}
that sends
x
to
(x,\dotsc,x)
. We may think of the cross product
H^{n}(X)\to H^{pn}(X^{p})
as an “external operation” as its output does not stay within
H^{*}(X)
. In contrast, the cup square
H^{n}(X)\to H^{pn}(X)
is an “internal operation.” Thus in this case, the internal operation is defined by first applying the external operation, then pulling back over a diagonal map.

In the same vein, the Steenrod powers
P^{i}:H^{n}(X)\to H^{n+2i(p-1)}(X)
are internal operations that are defined by first applying an external operation,
H^{n}(X)\to H^{pn}(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p})
, and then pulling back over a “diagonal map”
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{p}
. The diagonal map is defined by sending
(t,x)
to
(\tilde{t},x,\dotsc,x)
, where
\tilde{t}
is any preimage of
t
under the covering map
\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}
.

Theorem 7.2

(
[
3
, pp. 152–155]
,
[
22
, p. 504]
,
[
32
, p. 112]
)
.

For each
n\geq 1
, there is a unique family of operations
\Gamma:H^{n}(X)\to H^{pn}(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p})
for each cell complex
X
that satisfies the following properties:

1.

Let
i
be an inclusion of a fiber in the fiber bundle

X^{\wedge p}\to\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}\to\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty},

(7.1)

which is induced by the projection onto the first factor. Then
\Gamma(\alpha)
pulls back over
X^{p}\to X^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{i}\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}
to
\alpha^{\otimes p}\in H^{pn}(X^{p})
.

2.

Naturality: For every continuous map
f:X\to Y
and
\alpha\in H^{n}(Y)
, let
\Gamma(f)
denote the induced map
\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}\to\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}Y^{\wedge p}
. Then
\Gamma(f)^{*}\Gamma(\alpha)=\Gamma(f^{*}\alpha)
.

3.

\Gamma(\alpha\smile\beta)=\Gamma(\alpha)\smile\Gamma(\beta)
.

Furthermore, the Steenrod powers are determined by the classes
\Gamma(\alpha)
as follows. Choose generators
\omega_{k}\in H^{k}(\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty})
that are dual to the
k
-cells in
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}
. Then
\Gamma(\alpha)
pulls back along the diagonal inclusion
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}
to
\sum_{k}\omega_{pn-n-k}\otimes\theta_{k}(\alpha)
.

•

When
p=2
,
\mathit{Sq}^{i}(\alpha)=\theta_{i}(\alpha)
.

•

When
p=2m+1
is odd,

\displaystyle P^{i}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i+mn(n+1)/2}(m!)^{n}\theta_{2i(p-1)}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\beta P^{i}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i+mn(n+1)/2+1}(m!)^{n}\theta_{2i(p-1)+1}(\alpha),

and all other
\theta_{k}(\alpha)
vanish.

7.2
An overview of the proof of
theorem

1.3

From
theorem

7.2

\mathit{Sq}^{i}(\iota_{m})
is related to
\Gamma(\iota_{m})
via the Künneth formula. In particular,
\sum_{i}\omega_{n-i}\otimes\mathit{Sq}^{i}(\iota_{m})
has Brown representative

\displaystyle\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{2k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{2n})

\displaystyle(t,T)\mapsto F(t,T,T)=\bigcup_{x\in\{t\}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}T^{\wedge 2}}f(x).

We proved a result that expresses the Künneth formula geometrically in terms of Brown representatives. It implies that
\mathit{Sq}^{i}(\iota_{m})
has the following Brown representative. Let
H
denote the upper hemisphere of
\mathbb{S}^{n-i}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}_{2k+n-i}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{2n})

(7.2)

\displaystyle T\mapsto F(H\times\{(T,T)\})=\bigcup_{x\in H\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}T^{\wedge 2}}f(x).

We proved that the map in
eq.

7.2
factors through
\mathit{cyc}
. To prove this, observe that
H\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}T^{\wedge 2}\subset\mathbb{S}^{n-i}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge 2}
. It is useful to view
\mathbb{S}^{n-i}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge 2}
as a “fiber bundle” over
(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge 2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
induced by projection onto the second factor.

7.3
Brown representatives of the images of
\Gamma

By the naturality property in
theorem

7.2
and Brown representability,
\Gamma
is determined by
\Gamma(\iota_{n})
for all
n
. First we will find Brown representatives for
\Gamma(\alpha)
, for the generators
\alpha
of
H^{n}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. These representatives are maps
f_{n}:\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pn})
, and we will construct them so that they are piecewise smooth when restricted to the subspace
\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge p}
, where
\bar{n}=2pn+1
. This subspace is a closed manifold so we can consider its space of cycles, and
f_{n}
restricted to this manifold will have a gluing. From this gluing we can derive a Brown representative not exactly for
\Gamma(\iota_{n})\in H^{pn}(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p})
, but for its image under the restriction homomorphism

H^{pn}(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{pn}(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}),

(7.3)

for
X=\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})
. For general spaces
X
, the fact that this homomorphism is an isomorphism can be deduced from the Serre spectral sequences of the fiber bundle in
eq.

7.1
and the analogous bundle for
\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}
. Denote the images of classes
\Gamma(\alpha)
under this isomorphism by
\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\alpha)
.
\Gamma^{\bar{n}}
is also natural in the sense that maps
f:X\to Y
give rise to maps
\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(f):\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p}\to\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}Y^{\wedge p}
.

Henceforth, by
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
we will mean a cell complex whose
n
-skeleton is
\mathbb{S}^{n}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

For any
m\geq 1
, let
a:\mathbb{S}^{m}\hookrightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)
denote the inclusion of the
m
-skeleton, which represents a generator of
H^{m}(\mathbb{S}^{m})
, that we denote by
\alpha
. Then for some
\varepsilon>0
,
\Gamma(\alpha)
is represented by a continuous map
f_{m}:\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pm})
such that:

•

f_{m}|_{\mathbb{S}^{\bar{m}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}}
is piecewise smooth.

•

f_{m}(t,x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})=0
whenever
t
lies in the
(p-1)m-1
-skeleton
(\mathbb{S}^{\infty})^{(p-1)m-1}
and
(x_{1},\dotsb,x_{p})
is within distance
\varepsilon
of the image of the diagonal
\Delta\subset(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{p}
in
(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}
.

In addition, for topological spaces
X
and
Y
, consider the map

R:\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X\times Y)^{\wedge p}\to(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{\wedge p})\times(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}Y^{\wedge p}),

defined by
R(t,((x_{i},y_{i}))_{i})=((t,(x_{i})_{i}),(t,(y_{i})_{i}))
. Then for any
m,n\geq 1
,
f_{m}
,
f_{n}
and
f_{m+n}
are related through the following diagram that commutes up to homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m}\times\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+n})^{\wedge p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p})\times(\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pm})\times\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pn})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(m+n)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m+n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m}\times f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

(7.4)

Proof.

Let
\Delta\subset(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}
denote the diagonal, and consider the
(pm-1)
-dimensional cell complex
\tilde{\Delta}=\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Delta
. Let
a:\mathbb{S}^{m}\hookrightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)
be the inclusion of the
m
-skeleton, which generates
H^{m}(\mathbb{S}^{m})
. Then
\Gamma(a)
actually restricts to an embedding on
\tilde{\Delta}
. Observe that
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times\{*\}\cup\tilde{\Delta}
can be formed from
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times\{*\}
by attaching cells of dimension at most
pm-1
, which does not change the
pm
-dimensional cohomology group. Thus
\Gamma(\iota_{m})
still pulls back to zero over the inclusion
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times\{*\}\cup\tilde{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)^{\wedge p}
.

In other words, we can represent
\Gamma(\iota_{m})
by a map
h:\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)^{\wedge p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pm})
that becomes nullhomotopic when restricted to
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times\{*\}\cup\tilde{\Delta}
. By the homotopy extension property, we may assume that
h
actually vanishes over
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times\{*\}\cup\tilde{\Delta}
. In fact, we can assume that
h
vanishes over the union of
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\infty}\times\{*\}
and the closed
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of the diagonal, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
.

By naturality,
\Gamma(\alpha)=\Gamma(a^{*}\iota_{m})=\Gamma(a)^{*}\Gamma(\iota_{m})
. Thus by Brown representability, we can represent
\Gamma(\alpha)
by
f_{m}=h\circ\Gamma(a)
.

Let us now prove that the diagram (
7.4
) commutes. From our construction of
f_{m}
and
f_{n}
, we know that
(f_{m})^{*}(\iota_{pm})=\Gamma(\alpha)
and
(f_{n})^{*}(\iota_{pn})=\Gamma(\beta)
, where
\alpha:\mathbb{S}^{m}\hookrightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m)
and
\beta:\mathbb{S}^{n}\hookrightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
are the respective inclusions of the
m
-skeleton and
n
-skeleton.

The maps in the left column compose into a map
\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m}\times\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\wedge p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pm})\times\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pn})
that is the pair of maps
(f_{m}\circ\Gamma(\pr_{1}),f_{n}\circ\Gamma(\pr_{2}))
. Therefore the composite of the left column and bottom row represents the cup product

\displaystyle\Gamma(\pr_{1})^{*}(f_{m})^{*}(\iota_{pm})\smile\Gamma(\pr_{2})^{*}(f_{n})^{*}(\iota_{pn})

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\pr_{1})^{*}\Gamma(\alpha)\smile\Gamma(\pr_{2})^{*}\Gamma(\beta)

(by naturality)

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\pr_{1}^{*}(\alpha))\smile\Gamma(\pr_{2}^{*}(\beta))

(by (3))

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\pr_{1}^{*}(\alpha)\smile\pr_{2}^{*}(\beta))

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\alpha\times\beta).

On the other hand, the composite of the top row and the right column represents
\Gamma(q)^{*}(f_{m+n})^{*}(\iota_{p(m+n)})=\Gamma(q)^{*}\Gamma(\gamma)
, where
q
is the quotient map of the smash product, and
\gamma:\mathbb{S}^{m+n}\hookrightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},m+n)
is the inclusion of the
(m+n)
-skeleton. By naturality,
\Gamma(q)^{*}\Gamma(\gamma)=\Gamma(q^{*}(\gamma))
, but
\gamma\circ q
represents
\alpha\times\beta
. The lemma statement follows by Brown representability.
∎

Let
\Phi_{m}
denote a gluing for
f_{m}|_{\mathbb{S}^{\bar{m}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}}
, which exists by
theorem

4.3
.

Proposition 7.4
.

For any
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
,
\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\iota_{n})\in H^{pn}(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p})
is represented by a map

F_{k}^{n}:\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}\to\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}((\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p})\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p})\xrightarrow{\Phi_{n+k}^{pk}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)}),

(7.5)

where the first map is induced by the Cartesian product in the second variable and the second map is
(t,A)\mapsto\{t\}\times A
.

Furthermore, for any cell complex
X
and cohomology class
\alpha\in H^{n}(X)
that is represented by a map
a:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})
,
\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\alpha)=\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(a)(F_{k}^{n})^{*}(\iota_{pn})
.

Proof.

In order to prove the proposition, the naturality of
\Gamma^{\bar{n}}
implies that it suffices to prove that when
X
is a cellular model for
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
with
\mathbb{S}^{n}
as its
n
-skeleton, then
\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\iota_{n})=\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\iota_{n})^{*}(F_{k}^{n})^{*}(\iota_{pn})
. For any cell complex
X
and
\alpha\in H^{n}(X)
, define the operation
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{n}}(\alpha)=\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\alpha)^{*}(F_{k}^{n})^{*}(\iota_{pn})
where Brown representability lets us view
\alpha
as a map
X\to K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
.

We verify that
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{n}}
satisfies properties (1)–(3) in
theorem

7.2
. The naturality property (2) follows directly from the definition and Brown representability. (1): We prove that
j^{*}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{n}}(\iota_{n})=\iota_{n}^{\otimes p}
, where
j:K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)^{\wedge p}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)^{\wedge p}
is an inclusion of the fiber. Consider another inclusion of a fiber,
i:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}\to\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}
. By the definition of
F_{k}^{n}
, the composite map

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{i}\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{F_{k}^{n}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{(A_{1},\dotsc,A_{p})\mapsto\{*\}\times A_{1}\times\dotsb\times A_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p})\xrightarrow{\Phi_{n+k}^{pk}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{(-)^{\wedge p}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}((\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p})\xrightarrow{(i_{n+k})_{\sharp}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p})\xrightarrow{\Phi_{n+k}^{pk}}\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)}),

where
(-)^{\wedge p}
denotes the
p
-fold smash product. Observe that the composition law in
section

4
implies that
f_{n+k}\circ i_{n+k}
has a gluing, namely
\Phi_{n+k}^{pk}\circ(i_{n+k})_{\sharp}
.

Property (1) of
\Gamma
implies that
i_{n+k}^{*}(f_{n+k})^{*}(\iota_{p(n+k)})=i_{n+k}^{*}\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\otimes p}
(where
\alpha:\mathbb{S}^{n+k}\hookrightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n+k)
). Since
f_{n+k}\circ i_{n+k}
represents
\alpha^{\otimes p}
, we can homotope it until it is the inclusion of the smash product,
(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{h}\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)})
. Note that the identity homomorphism is a gluing of
h
. Therefore, by
lemma

4.7
,
\Phi_{n+k}^{pk}\circ(i_{n+k})_{\sharp}
is homotopic to the identity map.

To summarize,
F_{k}^{n}\circ i
is homotopic to the
p
-fold smash product map
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)})
, which represents the
p
-fold cross product by
proposition

5.9
. That is,
F_{k}^{n}\circ i
represents
\iota_{n}^{\otimes p}
.

Now consider the corresponding commutative diagram for
X=K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
, induced by a map
\iota_{n}:K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})
that vanishes at the basepoint:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{n}^{\times p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\iota_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{k}^{n}}

Since the top row is a weak homotopy equivalence,
j^{*}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{n}}(\iota_{n})=j^{*}\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\iota_{n})^{*}(F_{k}^{n})^{*}(\iota_{pn})=(\iota_{n}^{\times p})^{*}i^{*}(F_{k}^{n})^{*}(\iota_{pn})=\iota_{n}^{\otimes p}
, which demonstrates (1).

(3): Let us prove that the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+j})\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k}))^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{j+k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+n+j+k})^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+j})^{p})\times(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{pj}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(m+j)})\times\mathcal{Z}_{pk}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+k)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{p(j+k)}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(m+n+j+k)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\chi^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j+k}^{m+n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j}^{m}\times F_{k}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

(7.6)

Given
x\in\mathbb{S}^{b}
and
y\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
, let
x\wedge y
denote the image of
(x,y)
under the smash product map
\mathbb{S}^{b}\times\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{S}^{b+d}
. Given
X\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{b})
and
Y\in\mathcal{Z}_{c}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
, let
X\wedge Y\in\mathcal{Z}_{a+c}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{b+d})
denote the image of
X\times Y
under the smash product map. Starting with
(t,((X_{i},Y_{i}))_{i})
in the top left corner, if we go clockwise then we get

\displaystyle F_{j+k}^{m+n}\circ(\id\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\chi^{p})(t,((X_{i},Y_{i}))_{i})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle F_{j+k}^{m+n}(t,(X_{i}\wedge Y_{i})_{i})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{m+n+j+k}^{p(j+k)}(\{t\}\times X_{1}\wedge Y_{1}\times\dotsb\times X_{p}\wedge Y_{p}).

(7.7)

Consider the map
\psi:\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+j})\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k}))^{p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{p(j+k)}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+j}\times\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{p})
defined by
\psi(t,((X_{i},Y_{i}))_{i})=\{t\}\times X_{1}\times Y_{1}\times\dotsb\times X_{p}\times Y_{p}
. Then the clockwise direction is equal to
\Phi_{m+n+j+k}^{p(j+k)}\circ\varphi_{\sharp}\circ\psi
, where
\varphi
comes from
eq.

7.4
. By the composition law in
section

4
,
f_{m+n+j+k}\circ\varphi
has a gluing, namely
\Phi_{m+n+j+k}\circ\varphi_{\sharp}
.

On the other hand, going counterclockwise gives us

\displaystyle\chi\circ(F_{j}^{m}\times F_{k}^{n})\circ R(t,((X_{i},Y_{i}))_{i})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\chi(F_{j}^{m}(t,(X_{i})_{i}),F_{k}^{n}(t,(Y_{i})_{i}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{m+j}^{pj}(\{t\}\times X_{1}\times\dotsb\times X_{p})\wedge\Phi_{n+k}^{pk}(\{t\}\times Y_{1}\times\dotsb\times Y_{p}).

(7.8)

The composition law in
section

4
implies that
\chi\circ(f_{m+j}\times f_{n+k})\circ R
has a gluing, namely
\chi\circ(\Phi_{m+j}\times\Phi_{n+k})\circ R_{\sharp}
.

However,
proposition

7.3
implies that the diagram in
eq.

7.4
commutes up to homotopy. By
lemma

4.7
, this means that
\Phi_{m+n+j+k}\circ\varphi_{\sharp}
is homotopic to
\chi\circ(\Phi_{m+j}\times\Phi_{n+k})\circ R_{\sharp}
. Therefore the diagram in
eq.

7.6
commutes up to homotopy.

Now suppose that two cohomology classes of
X
are represented by maps
\alpha:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+j})
and
\beta:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})
. The following diagram also commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(X\times X)^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+j})\times\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k}))^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{p})\times(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}X^{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{m+j})^{p})\times(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+k})^{p})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\Delta^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\alpha\times\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\bar{n}}(\alpha)\times\Gamma(\beta)}

Together, the last two diagrams imply that
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{n}}(\alpha\smile\beta)=\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{n}}(\alpha)\smile\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{n}}(\beta)
. This proves (3).
∎

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
\iota_{n}
denote a generator of
H^{n}(\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m}))
. Then for all integers
k
such that
1\leq 2k\leq n
,
P^{k}(\iota_{n})=\mathit{cyc}^{*}(\alpha_{2k(p-1)})
and
\beta P^{k}(\iota_{n})=\mathit{cyc}^{*}(\alpha_{2k(p-1)+1})
for some nonzero classes
\alpha_{j}\in H^{n+j}(\mathcal{Z}_{pm}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n+m}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1},L_{n+m}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1}))
where
L_{n+m}
is the lens space from
eq.

1.3
.

In fact,
\alpha_{j}
has a Brown representative
\Psi_{j}
which is a gluing of a map
h_{j}:L_{n+m}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{pn-n-j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)})
that vanishes over all
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})
for which some
x_{i}=*
.

Proof.

Consider the maps
f_{n}
and
F_{k}^{n}
from
propositions

7.3
and
7.4
.

Let
j\geq 1
. By
proposition

7.4
, the class
\theta_{j}(\iota_{n})
from
theorem

7.2
can be derived by applying the Künneth formula to the following composite map,

\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\bar{n}}\times\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{F_{m}^{n}}\mathcal{Z}_{pm}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)}).

(7.9)

Lemma 7.6
.

\theta_{j}
has a Brown representative which is the map
\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})\to\mathcal{Z}_{p(m+n)-n-j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)})
defined by
T\mapsto F_{m}^{n}(\tilde{C}\times\{(T,\dotsc,T)\})
where
\tilde{C}
is a lift, over the covering space
\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\to\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\bar{n}}
, of a cellular
(pn-n-j)
-cycle
C
in
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\bar{n}}
that represents the appropriate generator in
H_{pn-n-j}(\mathbb{L}_{p}^{\bar{n}})
.

Proof.

Note that
F_{m}^{n}(\tilde{C}\times\{(T,\dotsc,T)\})=\Phi_{n+m}(\tilde{C}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}T^{\wedge p})
. Now find a map
K\to\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})
where
K
is a high skeleton of
K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},n)
, and extend it to a map
Y\to\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})
where
Y
is a tubular neighbourhood of
K
embedded in a Euclidean space of large dimension. Homotope this map
Y\to\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})
until it is piecewise smooth using
theorem

3.9
. Now apply
corollary

5.5
.
∎

Since
j\geq 1
, we have
pn-n-j\leq(p-1)(n+m)-1
, that is,
\tilde{C}\subset(\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}})^{(p-1)(n+m)-1}
. Together with
proposition

7.3
, this implies that for all
x\in\Delta
, we have
\Phi_{n+m}(\tilde{C}\times\{x\})=0
. We will show that this map
\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})\to\mathcal{Z}_{p(n+m)-n-j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)})
factors into some composite map

\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})\xrightarrow{\mathit{cyc}}\mathcal{Z}_{pm}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n+m}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1},L_{n+m}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1})\to\mathcal{Z}_{p(n+m)-n-j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)}).

We are looking at the map

\tilde{C}\times\{(T,\dotsc,T)\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})^{\wedge p}\xrightarrow{F_{m}^{n}}\mathcal{Z}_{pm}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)}).

(7.10)

Recall the homeomorphism
h:(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m+1)-1}\setminus\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to L_{n+m}\times\itr\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1}
from
eq.

1.3
. Applying
corollary

5.5
to the above map gives

\displaystyle F_{m}^{n}\left(\tilde{C}\times\left\{(T,\dotsc,T)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{n+m}(\tilde{C}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}T^{p})

\displaystyle\text{\footnotesize(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:ExternalOperation_Sphere})}={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{n+m}(\tilde{C}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(T^{p}\setminus\Delta)),

and observing using
eq.

1.4
that
T^{p}\setminus\Delta=\gamma^{-1}(h^{-1}(\mathit{cyc}(T)))
, where
\gamma:\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m+1)-1}\setminus\Delta\to(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m+1)-1}\setminus\Delta)/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is the covering map, and thus
T^{p}\setminus\Delta
is the sum of translates
a\cdot D
of a fundamental domain
D
by all
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{n+m}\left(\tilde{C}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(a\cdot D)\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{n+m}\left(\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(a\cdot\tilde{C})\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}D\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Phi_{n+m}(\mathbb{S}^{pn-n-j}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}D).

Therefore for
1\leq j\leq(p-1)n
,
\theta_{j}(\iota_{n})
has a Brown representative which is the composite map

\mathcal{Z}_{m}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{n+m})\xrightarrow{\mathit{cyc}}\mathcal{Z}_{pm}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n+m}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1},L_{n+m}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1})\xrightarrow{\Psi_{j}}\mathcal{Z}_{p(n+m)-n-j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)}),

where
\Psi_{j}
is a gluing of the desired map
h_{j}:L_{n+m}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+m+1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{pn-n-j}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{p(n+m)})
defined by
h_{j}(x)=\Phi_{n+m}(\mathbb{S}^{pn-n-j}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{y\})
, where
y
is any one of the
p
points in
\gamma^{-1}(h^{-1}(x))
.
h_{j}
does not depend on the choice of
y
because of the equivalence relation involved in
\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
, and because the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
action on
\mathbb{S}^{\bar{n}}
is orientation-preserving when
p
is odd and the orientation is irrelevant when
p=2
.

Finally, for all
x=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})
such that some
x_{i}=*
,
f_{n+m}(-,x)=0
by
proposition

7.3
, so
h_{j}(x)=0
.
∎

Note that we could also have defines
h_{j}
in terms of the map
\Lambda^{n+m}_{pn-n-j}
from the following proposition.

Proposition 7.7
.

Consider the map
f_{m}:\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pm})
from
proposition

7.3
. Then for each
1\leq k\leq(p-1)m-1
there is a map
\Lambda^{m}_{k}:(\mathbb{S}^{pm}/\hat{\Delta})/\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pm})
, where
\mathbb{S}^{pm}=(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}
, defined by
\Lambda^{m}_{k}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})=\Phi_{m}(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{p})\})
.

Furthermore, given that
(\mathbb{S}^{pm}/\hat{\Delta})/\mathbb{Z}_{p}\sim\Th(L\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1})
where
L
is a lens space by
lemma

B.1
,
\Lambda^{m}_{k}
represents the image of
\omega_{(p-1)m-1-k}\in H^{(p-1)m-1-k}(L)
under the Thom isomorphism.

Proof.

Let us check that
\Lambda_{k}^{m}
is well-defined and continuous. Let
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
denote the open tubular neighbourhood of radius
\varepsilon
around the diagonal
\Delta\subset(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}
. Write
(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}_{\varepsilon}=(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon}
. We can define a fiber bundle
\xi:\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{S}^{pm}_{\varepsilon}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
by projection onto the second factor. Observe that
\Lambda_{k}^{m}
vanishes over
\Delta_{\varepsilon}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and otherwise is equal to
\xi_{*}f_{m}|_{\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{S}^{m})^{\wedge p}_{\varepsilon}}
. Thus
\Lambda_{k}^{m}
is continuous.

Consider the map
f:\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{pm}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{\infty}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{pm}\xrightarrow{f_{m}}\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{pm})
. Since
k\leq(p-1)m-1
,
f
vanishes over
\mathbb{L}_{p}^{k}\times\Delta
. Then
f
represents a cohomology class in
H^{pm}(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{pm}_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\partial\mathbb{S}^{pm}_{\varepsilon})
.

Now consider the fiber bundle
\mathbb{S}^{k}\to\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}N\xrightarrow{\pi}N/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
where
\pi
is induced by projection onto the second coordinate. (This is a fiber bundle because
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts freely on
N
.)
\pi
is also a map of pairs
\pi:(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}N,\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\partial N)\to(N/\mathbb{Z}_{p},\partial N/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

By excision,
\Lambda^{m}_{k}
represents the same relative cohomology class in
H^{pm-k}(N/\mathbb{Z}_{p},\partial N/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
as the cohomology pushforward
\pi_{*}f
as defined in
proposition

5.4
. Now we have to compute (relative) Poincaré duality in
\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}N
and
N/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and the homology pushforward
\pi_{*}
. Thus we need to compute this pushforward map.

We can exploit the nice geometry around the fixed points of the action. The proof of
lemma

B.1
shows that
N
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts freely on
\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}
with quotient
L=\mathbb{L}_{p}(1^{m},\dotsc,(\frac{p-1}{2})^{m})
. Moreover,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts trivially on the fibers, so in fact
\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}N\sim(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1}
. The action preserves the boundary so
\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\partial N\sim(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{m+1}
. Now consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{pm}((\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1},(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{m+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{pm-k}(L\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1},L\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{m+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{(p-1)m-1}(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{(p-1)m-1-k}(L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
Thom
Thom
pushforward
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pr_{2})_{*}}

To be more specific, a map
h:(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{a}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{b})
that vanishes on the boundary of its domain corresponds under the Thom isomorphism to a map
h^{\prime}:(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\to\mathcal{Z}_{a+m+1}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{S}^{b})
defined by
h^{\prime}(x)=h(x\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1})
. Therefore restricting
h^{\prime}
via the inclusion
\{*\}\times\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})
gives the fiber integral of the restriction of
h
via the inclusion
(\{*\}\times\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1},\{*\}\times\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{m+1})\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1},(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{m+1})
. This induces a map
H^{b-a}(\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\times\mathbb{D}^{m+1},\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{m+1})\to H^{b-a-(m+1)}(\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})
that is the inverse of the suspension isomorphism. Since
f
is supposed to restrict to the fundamental class over each copy of
\mathbb{S}^{pm}
, the Thom isomorphism sends it to cohomology class
\phi\in H^{(p-1)m-1}(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})
that restricts to the fundamental class over each copy of
\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}
.

Consider now the Serre spectral sequence of the fiber bundle
\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{k}
, whose
E_{2}
page looks like this:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p-1)m-1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

All differentials on this and future pages are zero because their domain or codomain is 0. Thus the
\phi
must correspond to the only class in
E_{2}=E_{\infty}
that restricts to the fundamental class on a copy of
\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}
, namely
1\in E_{2}^{0,(p-1)m-1}
.

To compute the pushforward of
\phi
along the map
(\pr_{2})_{*}:H^{(p-1)m-1}(\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1})\to H^{(p-1)m-1-k}(L)
, we evaluate the pushforward on the generator
\alpha\in H_{(p-1)m-1-k}(L)
that corresponds to
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. By the computations in the proof of
proposition

5.4
, if
\gamma\in H^{k}{L}
is the Poincaré dual of that homology generator
\alpha
, then
((\pr_{2})_{*})\phi(\alpha)=(\phi\smile\pr_{2}^{*}\gamma)[\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}]
. We have
\gamma=1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
; then we have to identify where
\pr_{2}^{*}\gamma
is on the
E_{2}
page of the above spectral sequence.

Consider the “diagonal inclusion”
\delta:\mathbb{L}_{p}^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}
defined by
[x]\mapsto[(x,x)]
. Note that
{\pr_{2}}\circ\delta
is the inclusion of the
k
-skeleton so
\delta^{*}\pr_{2}^{*}\gamma=1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Moreover,
{\pr_{1}}\circ\delta
is the identity map so
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cong H^{k}(\mathbb{L}_{p}^{k})
pulls back over
\pr_{1}
to
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cong E_{2}^{k,0}
. Thus
\pr_{2}^{*}\gamma=1\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cong E_{2}^{k,0}
.

Therefore the cup product of
1\in E_{2}^{0,(p-1)m-1}
and
1\in E_{2}^{k,0}
is
1\in E_{2}^{k,(p-1)m-1}
. Thus
((\pr_{2})_{*}\phi)(\alpha)=(\phi\smile\pr_{2}^{*}\gamma)[\mathbb{S}^{k}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{S}^{(p-1)m-1}]=1
. Thus
(\pr_{2})_{*}\phi=\omega_{(p-1)m-1-k}
.

Hence,
\pi_{*}f
is the Thom isomorphism of
\omega_{(p-1)m-1-k}
, which is the same class represented by
\Lambda_{k}^{m}
.
∎

This brings us to a proof of the formal version of
theorem

1.3
.

Proof of
theorem

7.1
.

Consider the classes
\alpha_{j}
from
theorem

7.5
and their Brown representatives
\Psi_{j}
. From
theorem

4.9
and the Thom Isomorphism Theorem we know that
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(L_{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},L_{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})\sim_{w}\prod_{j=n+1-k}^{p(n+1)-1-k}K(\mathbb{Z}_{p},j)
. Since each
\alpha_{j}
has a Brown representative that is a gluing,
proposition

4.14
implies that actually
\alpha_{j}=a\iota_{j}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
Proposition

7.7
implies that
a\neq 0
. Thus the Steenrod powers and Bockstein homomorphisms have Brown representatives as given in the theorem statement.
∎

7.4
Brown representatives for the Steenrod squares

Proof of
theorem

1.7
.

From
theorem

1.3
we know that
\mathit{Sq}^{i}
has a Brown representative which is the composition of
\mathit{cyc}
with the standard gluing of a Brown representative
g_{q}
of a class in
H^{*}(\mathbb{RP}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1},\mathbb{RP}^{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
, where
q=n+k+i
.

To write
\mathit{cyc}
down explicitly, we will use the formula from
eq.

6.8
for the homeomorphism
h
from
eq.

1.3
. Since
p=2
,
F=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. The formula for
\mathit{cyc}
in
eq.

1.4
says that to evaluate
\mathit{cyc}(V\cap\mathbb{S}^{m})
, we look at the we take unordered pairs of distinct points
x,y\in V\cap\mathbb{S}^{m}
, and look at the locus of the pairs
(\vspan\{x-y\},\frac{x+y}{2})
in
\mathbb{RP}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}
.

g_{q}
can be constructed using
lemmas

5.1
,
5.3
and
5.12
, starting from the observation that the class represented by
g_{q}
in the Thom space
(\mathbb{RP}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1})/(\mathbb{RP}^{n}\times\partial\mathbb{D}^{n+1})
corresponds, under the Thom isomorphism, to the generator of
H^{k+i-1}(\mathbb{RP}^{n})
. Thus we may choose
g_{q}
to be the map

\displaystyle g_{q}:\mathbb{RP}^{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{n+1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{n(k+i-1)}^{\mathrm{}}(\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)},\partial\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)})

\displaystyle g_{q}(\ell,v)=((\ell^{\perp})^{k+i-1}\times\{v\})\cap\mathbb{D}^{(n+1)(k+i)}.

Combining the formulas for
\mathit{cyc}
and
g_{q}
gives us the formula for
\mathit{sq}^{i}
in
eq.

1.5
.

Now suppose that
V\cap\mathbb{S}^{n}
is a planar cycle, where
V
is a
(k+1)
-dimensional affine subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be the set of lines in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
through the origin that are parallel to
V
. Then it suffices to prove that

\mathit{cyc}(V\cap\mathbb{S}^{n})=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}}\{\ell\}\times(\ell^{\perp}\cap V\cap\mathbb{D}^{n+1}).

(
\subset
): For any unordered pair
x,y\in V\cap\mathbb{S}^{m}
such that
x\neq y
, let
\ell=\vspan\{x-y\}\in\mathbb{RP}(V_{0})
. Then since
V\cap\mathbb{D}^{m+1}
is a convex set containing
x
and
y
, it also contains
\frac{x+y}{2}
. Observe that
\ell^{\perp}
is orthogonal to the chord
xy
. The Pythagorean theorem implies that
\ell^{\perp}
must bisect the chord, thus
\frac{x+y}{2}\in\ell^{\perp}
.

(
\supset
): Choose any
\ell\in\mathbb{RP}(V_{0})
and
z\in\ell^{\perp}\cap V\cap\mathbb{D}^{m+1}
, so that
\lVert z\rVert<1
. Let
\ell^{\prime}
be the line parallel to
\ell
that passes through
z
.
\ell^{\prime}
should intersect
V\cap\mathbb{S}^{m}
in two distinct points,
x
and
y
. Thus
\ell=\vspan\{x-y\}
. Since
\ell^{\prime}\perp\ell^{\perp}\ni z
, the Pythagorean theorem implies that
\lVert x-z\rVert^{2}+\lVert z\rVert^{2}=\lVert y-z\rVert^{2}+\lVert z\rVert^{2}=1
, so in fact
z=\frac{x+y}{2}
.
∎

Appendix A
Geometric Measure Theory

A.1
The Standard Definitions of Mod
p
Integral Currents and Cycles

We will show that our definitions from
section

2
are compatible with the standard definitions of the space of mod
p
integral currents.

Let
\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{ext}}(M;G)
, and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{ext}}(M,N;G)
denote respectively the extrinsically defined mass functional, flat metric, spaces of integral currents in
M
and integral relative cycles with coefficients in
G
. These are defined by fixing some Riemannian embedding
e:M\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
which is always possible due to the Nash embedding theorem. This embedding induces an embedding
\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(M)\hookrightarrow M\times\Gr_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The standard definition of a varifold is that is a Radon measure on
M\times\Gr_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. When
R
is a rectifiable current, the standard definition of the varifold
\lvert R\rvert^{\mathrm{ext}}
associated with
R
is so that for all
A\subset M\times\Gr_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\begin{gathered}\lvert R\rvert^{\mathrm{ext}}(A)=\int_{\{x\in U~:~(x,\Tan^{k}(\mathcal{H}^{k}\mathbin{\llcorner}U,x))\in A\}}\Theta^{k}(\lVert R\rVert,-)\,d\mathcal{H}^{k},\\
\text{where }U=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}~:~\Theta^{k}(\lVert R\rVert,x)\geq 1\}.\end{gathered}

(A.1)

It is clear that our definitions from
section

2
could have used the coefficient group
\mathbb{Z}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, with
\left\lvert a\right\rvert
being the usual absolute value for
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. When we use coefficient group
G
, we denote the sets and groups defined by
I_{k}(M;G)
,
I_{k}(M,N;G)
,
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;G)
, and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N;G)
.

In what follows, let
G
be
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for any prime
p
. For each Lipschitz singular chain
T=\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}
with coefficients
a_{i}\in G
, the
current parametrized by
T
is
\mathcal{C}(T)=\sum_{i}a_{i}(e\circ\sigma_{i})_{\sharp}(\Delta^{k})
, where
\Delta^{k}
is treated as a
k
-dimensional current with its standard orientation. When
G=\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{C}(T)
is rectifiable due to
[
10
, 4.1.28(3)]
, and clearly
\spt\mathcal{C}(T)\subset\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}\subset M
.
\partial\mathcal{C}(T)
must also be rectifiable because it is the current parametrized by
\partial T
, thus
\mathcal{C}(T)
is actually an integral current. This gives a map
\mathcal{C}:I_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z})\to\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z})
. When
G=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, considering congruence classes shows that we have an analogous map
\mathcal{C}:I_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

For any
T\in I_{k}(M,N;\mathbb{Z})
, define
\mathcal{C}(T)=\mathcal{C}(\tilde{T})\mathbin{\llcorner}(M\setminus N)
for any
\tilde{T}\in T
.

Lemma A.1
.

For any
T\in I_{k}(M,N;\mathbb{Z})
and
A\subset\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(M)
,
\lvert T\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}(A)=\lvert\mathcal{C}(T)\rvert^{\mathrm{ext}}(A)
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we will abbreviate “
\mathcal{H}^{k}
-almost” to “almost.” Let
\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}\in T
. Choose a standard simple
k
-vector field
\beta
on
\Delta^{k}
that is the wedge of a positively oriented orthonormal basis. By the proof of
[
10
, 4.1.28(4)]
, we have
\mathcal{C}(T)=(\mathcal{H}^{k}\mathbin{\llcorner}B)\wedge\eta
where
[
10
, 4.1.25]
implies that for almost all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\eta(y)=\sum_{i}a_{i}\sum_{x\in\sigma_{i}^{-1}(y)}\frac{(\bigwedge_{k}D\sigma_{i})(\beta(x))}{J_{k}\sigma_{i}}

and

\displaystyle B=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\eta(y)\neq 0\}.

Let us show that we can replace
B
with
\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}
. The proof of
[
10
, 4.1.28(4)]
implies that
B
is almost contained in
\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}
. In fact,
\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}
is also almost contained in
B
: since
T
is non-overlapping, for almost all
y\in\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}
there is a unique
x\in\Delta^{k}
and a unique
\sigma_{i}
such that
\sigma_{i}(x)=y
. Moreover, for almost all such
y
, the proof of
[
10
, 4.1.25]
implies that
\sigma_{i}
is differentiable at
x
and
\im D\sigma_{i}=\Tan^{m}(\mathcal{H}^{k}\mathbin{\llcorner}\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i},y)
, which by
[
10
, 3.2.19]
is a
k
-dimensional vector space for almost all such
y
. Therefore
\eta(y)=a_{i}(\bigwedge_{k}D\sigma_{i})(\beta(x))/J_{k}\sigma_{i}\neq 0
, which by
[
10
, 4.1.25]
implies that
\lVert\eta(y)\rVert=\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert
. Thus we may write
R=(\mathcal{H}^{k}\mathbin{\llcorner}\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i})\wedge\eta
.

For almost all
y\in\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}
,
y
lies in the image of some unique
\sigma_{i}
, in which case the proof of
[
10
, 4.1.28]
implies that
\Theta^{k}(\lVert\mathcal{C}(T)\rVert,y)=\lVert\eta(y)\rVert=\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert
. Therefore
eq.

A.1
simplifies to

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{C}(T)\rvert^{\mathrm{ext}}(A\setminus\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(N))

\displaystyle=\int_{\{x\in\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}\setminus N~:~(x,\Tan^{k}(\mathcal{H}^{k}\mathbin{\llcorner}\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i},x))\in A\}}\Theta^{k}(\lVert\mathcal{C}(T)\rVert,-)\,d\mathcal{H}^{k}

(
T
is non-overlapping)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int_{\{x\in\im\sigma_{i}\setminus N~:~(x,\Tan^{k}(\mathcal{H}^{k}\mathbin{\llcorner}\im\sigma_{i},x))\in A\}}\Theta^{k}(\lVert\mathcal{C}(T)\rVert,-)\,d\mathcal{H}^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\underbrace{\mathcal{H}^{k}(\{x\in\im\sigma_{i}\setminus N~:~(x,\Tan^{k}(\mathcal{H}^{k}\mathbin{\llcorner}\im\sigma_{i},x))\in A\})}_{\lvert\sigma_{i}\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}(A)}

\displaystyle=\lvert T\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}(A).

∎

Lemma A.2
.

For any
T=\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}\in I_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, let
\tilde{T}=\sum_{i}\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\sigma_{i}\in I_{k}(M;\mathbb{Z})
. Then
\mathbf{M}(T)=\mathbf{M}(\tilde{T})
,
\lvert T\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}=\lvert\tilde{T}\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}
, and
\mathcal{C}(\tilde{T})
is representative mod
p
.

Proof.

The first two claims follow directly from the respective definitions. For the third claim, recall from the proof of
lemma

A.1
that for
\mathcal{H}^{k}
-almost all
x\in\bigcup_{i}\im\sigma_{i}
,
\Theta^{k}(\lVert\mathcal{C}(\tilde{T})\rVert,x)=\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\leq p/2
for some
i
. The third claim then follows from
[
10
, p. 130]
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

For any
T\in I_{k}(M;G)
,
\mathbf{M}(T)=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(T))
.

Proof.

Let
T=\sum_{i}a_{i}\sigma_{i}
. When
G=\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathbf{M}(T)=\sum_{i}\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\mathcal{H}^{k}(\im\sigma_{i})=\sum_{i}\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\lvert\sigma_{i}\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{\emptyset}(\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(M))=\lvert T\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{\emptyset}(\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(M))\\
\text{\footnotesize(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Varifold_CompareIntExt})}=\lvert\mathcal{C}(T)\rvert^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(M))=\lvert\mathcal{C}(T)\rvert^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(T).

When
G=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, let
\tilde{T}=\sum_{i}\left\lvert a_{i}\right\rvert\sigma_{i}
. Then by
lemma

A.2
and the
G=\mathbb{Z}
case,
\mathbf{M}(T)=\mathbf{M}(\tilde{T})=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{T}))
. Therefore,
\mathcal{C}(\tilde{T})\in\mathcal{C}(T)
, and thus
\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(T))=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{T}))
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

For any
T\in I_{k}(M,N;G)
,
\mathbf{M}(T)=\lvert T\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}(\tilde{\Gr}_{k}(M))=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(T))
.

Proof.

This follows from
lemma

A.3
and the definition of
\mathbf{M}
for a relative current.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Suppose that
S,T\in I_{k}(M,N;G)
satisfy
\mathcal{F}(S,T)=0
. Then
\mathcal{C}(S)=\mathcal{C}(T)
,
\mathbf{M}(S)=\mathbf{M}(T)
, and
\lvert S\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}=\lvert T\rvert^{\mathrm{}}_{N}
.

Proof.

First we consider the case where
N=\emptyset
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, choose any
P\in I_{k+1}(M;G)
and
Q\in I_{k}(M;G)
such that
\partial P+Q=S-T
and
\mathbf{M}(P)+\mathbf{M}(Q)<\varepsilon
. Then
\partial\mathcal{C}(P)+\mathcal{C}(Q)=\mathcal{C}(S)-\mathcal{C}(T)
, and by
lemma

A.3
,
\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(P))+\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(Q))<\varepsilon
. Thus
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(S),\mathcal{C}(T))\leq\varepsilon
. By letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we see that
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(S),\mathcal{C}(T))=0
. Since
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is a metric on the space of rectifiable currents with coefficients in
G
, we conclude that
\mathcal{C}(S)=\mathcal{C}(T)
.

Finally,
lemma

A.3
implies that
\mathbf{M}(S)=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(S))=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(T))=\mathbf{M}(T)
.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

1.

\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M;G)
is bilipschitz to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{ext}}(M;G)
.

2.

When
M
is closed,
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M;G)
is bilipschitz to
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{ext}}(M;G)
.

Proof.

The embedding
e:M\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
L
-bilipschitz for some
L\geq 1
. Let
\mathcal{N}
be a tubular neighbourhood of
M
, so that the projection map onto
M
is 2-Lipschitz. (1) and (2) follow from projecting chains and fillings of chains in
\mathcal{N}
onto
M
.
∎

Lemma A.7
.

For any
T\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
,
\mathbf{M}(T)=\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}(\mathcal{C}(T))
.

Proof.

This essentially follows from the fact that
\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is lower semicontinuous with respect to
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is bilipschitz to
\mathcal{F}
by
lemma

A.6
(
1
), and we defined
\mathbf{M}
to be lower semicontinuous with respect to
\mathcal{F}
using
eq.

2.1
.
∎

\LemCInfinityChainsDense

Proof.

Choose some embedding
X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The key ingredient in this proof is the Approximation Theorem from
[
10
, 4.2.20]
, which implies that for any
T\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(X)
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
k
-chain
S
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that is a linear combination of convex polyhedra so that
S
is supported within the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of
X
, and for some
(1+\varepsilon)
-bilipschitz
C^{1}
diffeomorphism
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have
\mathbf{M}(T-f_{\sharp}(S))<\varepsilon
and
\mathbf{M}(\partial T-\partial f_{\sharp}(S))<\varepsilon
. Thus
\mathcal{F}(T,f_{\sharp}(S))<\varepsilon
. We may smoothen
f
via a convolution with a
C^{\infty}
bump function to get a
C^{\infty}
map
f^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mathcal{F}(T,f^{\prime}_{\sharp}(S))<\varepsilon
,
\left\lvert\mathbf{M}(T)-\mathbf{M}(f^{\prime}_{\sharp}(S))\right\rvert<\varepsilon
, and
\left\lvert\mathbf{M}(\partial T)-\mathbf{M}(\partial f^{\prime}_{\sharp}(S))\right\rvert<\varepsilon
. We may even assume that
f^{\prime}_{\sharp}(S)
intersects each of the given submanifolds of
X
transversally by performing a small perturbation on
f^{\prime}
. Now simply let
T_{i}=f^{\prime}_{\sharp}(S)
for
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{i}
.
∎

A.2
The inductive limit topology

\LemContinuityIndLimTop

Proof.

We will only need to prove the statements for
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{}}
, as the proof works verbatim for
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{}}
as well.

Suppose that
g
is continuous in the inductive limit topology. Then for any
\mu>0
and open set
U\subset Y
,
g^{-1}(U)
is open in the inductive limit topology which implies that
g^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
is open in the flat topology on
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
. Thus
g
is continuous in the flat topology when restricted to
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
.

Suppose that
g
is continuous in the flat topology when restricted to
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
for every
\mu>0
. Then for any open set
U\in Y
and
\mu>0
,
g^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
is open in the subspace topology of
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
. That is,
g^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}=V_{\mu}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}
for some
V_{\mu}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
which is open in the flat topology. Thus for any subset
A\subset\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
of cycles whose masses are less than some
\mu>0
, we have

g^{-1}(U)\cap A=g^{-1}(U)\cap\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\cap A=V_{\mu}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\cap A=V_{\mu}\cap A,

which is open in the subspace topology of
A
. Thus
g^{-1}(U)
is open in the inductive limit topology. Therefore
g
is continuous in the inductive limit topology.

Now we prove the statement about
f
. Assume that
f
satisfies the stated property. Then for each
\mu>0
and the corresponding
\mu^{\prime}
,
f_{\mu}
is continuous with respect to the flat topology in the domain and the inductive limit topology in the codomain, so
f_{\mu}:\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)^{\mu}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is also continuous in the flat topology in the domain and the inductive limit topology on the codomain. Then it remains to apply the first part of the lemma.
∎

A.3
\delta
-localized families of cycles

Lemma A.8

(Coarea inequality with boundary mass control)
.

Consider a family of chains
\tau_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{d_{j}}^{\mathrm{}}(M)
, for
j=1,\dotsc,k
. Let
B
be a generalized ball of radius
r
in
M
. Then for any
0<s\leq r
and parameter
0<\lambda<1
,
B
can be replaced by a concentric generalized ball
B_{\lambda}
of radius
r_{\lambda}\in[r,r+s]
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2k^{2}}{s}\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\tau\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial_{s\lambda/4}B_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda k\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}).

(A.2)

Proof.

Let
\alpha=s/r
and
L=\lceil 1/\lambda\rceil
, and partition the region
(1+\alpha)B\setminus B
into
L
“annuli”
A_{l}=(1+\frac{l}{L}\alpha)B\setminus(1+\frac{l-1}{L}\alpha)B
. Thus we have

\sum_{l}\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}A_{l})\leq\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})\implies\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\frac{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}A_{l})}{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})}\leq k,

and by the Pigeonhole Principle, for some
A=A_{l}
,

\sum_{j}\frac{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}A)}{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})}\leq\frac{k}{L}\leq\lambda k\implies\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j_{0}}\mathbin{\llcorner}A)\leq\sum_{j}\frac{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}A)\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j_{0}})}{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})}\leq\lambda k\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j_{0}})\text{ for all }j_{0}.

Let
A^{\prime}=(1+\frac{l-1/4}{L}\alpha)B\setminus(1+\frac{l-3/4}{L}\alpha)B
. Now for each
j
, we have the coarea inequality

\displaystyle\int_{r+\frac{l-3/4}{L}s}^{r+\frac{l-1/4}{L}s}\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial(\tfrac{t}{r}B))\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}A^{\prime})\leq\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}A)\leq\frac{k}{L}\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})

\displaystyle\implies\int_{r+\frac{l-3/4}{L}s}^{r+\frac{l-1/4}{L}s}\sum_{j}\frac{L\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial(\tfrac{r}{r}B))}{k\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq k.

Therefore for some
r+\frac{l-3/4}{L}s\leq r_{L}\leq r+\frac{l-1/4}{L}s
, if we choose
B_{\lambda}=\frac{r_{L}}{r}B
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\frac{L\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B_{\lambda})}{k\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2Lk}{s}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j_{0}}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B_{\lambda})\leq\sum_{j}\frac{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j}\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial B_{\lambda})\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j_{0}})}{\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2k^{2}}{s}\mathbf{M}(\tau_{j_{0}})\text{ for all }j_{0}.

(A.3)

It remains to observe that
\partial_{s\lambda/4}B_{\lambda}\subset A
.
∎

\LemDeltaAdmissibleDoubling

Proof.

Without loss of generality (a small cost in the constant
c
) we may assume that the elements of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
are generalized balls. We may also assume that
\max_{C}\#\mathcal{U}_{C}\leq c
.

We will induct on
\dim X
. When
\dim X=0
there is nothing to prove. Now assume that the lemma is true for
\dim X=d
. Now let
\dim X=d+1
, and suppose we are given the initial
\delta
-admissible family
\mathcal{U}_{C}
. Applying the induction hypothesis to
X^{d}
gives us a
c\delta
-admissible family
\mathcal{V}_{C}
indexed by cells of dimension at most
d
, which satisfies properties (1)–(3) for those cells, and such that
\#\mathcal{V}_{C}\leq c
. Now, consider a
(d+1)
-cell
C
in
X
. We wish to define
\mathcal{V}_{C}
. For every
d
-face
C^{\prime}
of
C
and
B\in\mathcal{V}_{C^{\prime}}
, add
2B
to
\mathcal{U}_{C}
to get a family
\mathcal{W}_{C}
of generalized balls whose diameters sum to less than
(c+4dc)\delta<c\delta
. Thus
\max_{C}\#\mathcal{W}_{C}\leq c+2dc\leq c
.

For each
B\in\mathcal{W}_{C}
, let
\bar{B}=2B
. Replace every
B
with
\bar{B}
to get families
\bar{\mathcal{W}}_{C}
, whose elements have diameters summing up to less than
2c\delta\leq c\delta
.

Consider the following
merging procedure
for
\bar{\mathcal{W}}_{C}
. For any
\overline{B_{1}},\overline{B_{2}}\in\bar{\mathcal{W}}_{C}
that touch, replace them with
2B
, where
B
is the smallest generalized ball containing both of them. Keep repeating within
\bar{\mathcal{W}}_{C}
until it cannot be repeated. We are left with a family
\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{C}
of generalized balls, perhaps fewer in number, whose diameters sum to less than
2^{\#\bar{\mathcal{W}}_{C}}c\delta\leq c\delta
. Now replace each generalized ball in
\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{C}
with the concentric generalized ball of half the radius. The result is the desired family
\mathcal{V}_{C}
.

After carrying out the above process for each
(d+1)
-cell
C
, we have defined the collections
\mathcal{V}_{C}
for every cell
C
of positive dimension. The family
\{\mathcal{V}_{C}\}_{C\subset X^{d+1}}
is doubling due to the radius-halving step at the end, and the elements of
\mathcal{V}_{C}
are added (with radius doubled) to
\mathcal{U}_{C^{\prime}}
when
C
is a codimension-1 face of
C^{\prime}
. Finally, each element
\mathcal{U}_{C}
is contained in some element of
\mathcal{V}_{C}
because the sets involved only grow larger or are merged into larger sets. We also have
\max_{C}\#\mathcal{V}_{C}\leq c+\max_{C}\#\mathcal{W}_{C}\leq c
.
∎

\PropDiscreteDeltaLocExtension

Proof.

This follows from the proof of
[
20
, Proposition 2.7]
. That proof inductively constructs a sequence of
c_{1}\delta
-localized maps
F_{j}:X^{j}(q_{j})^{0}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
for
0=q_{0}<q_{1}<\dotsb<q_{n}=q_{n}(n,M,\delta)
, where
F_{0}=F
and
F_{j}
extends
F_{j-1}
, and for
N=\partial M
. It can be adapted for the case where
(M,N)
is a collar pair. We will prove by induction that each
F_{j}
satisfies the properties (
2
) and (
3
) with
X^{j}
instead of
X^{j}
and
X(q_{j})
instead of
\tilde{X}
, and
\chi_{F_{j}}(r)\leq c_{3}(r/\delta)(\chi_{f}(r)+\varepsilon)
for all
r>0
. Then
F_{n}
will be our desired
F
.

This is vacuously true for
j=0
. Assume by induction that it is true for
j-1
.
F_{j}
is first defined over the vertices of
X^{j-1}(q_{j})
by
F_{j}(x)=F_{j-1}(x^{\prime})
, where
x^{\prime}
is a “closest vertex of
X^{j-1}(q_{j-1})
” to
x
, in a sense defined formally in the proof. This ensures that (
3
) holds for each cell of positive dimension in
X^{j-1}
.

For each
j
-cell
C
of
X
,
F_{j}
is then extended over the vertices of
C(q_{j})
using a discrete analogue of the method to extend a map
g:\partial C\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
over
C
by finding a nullhomotopy
\partial C\times I\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,\mathcal{F})
of
g
. More precisely, a map
G:\partial C(q_{j})^{0}\times\{0,1,\dotsc,3^{q_{j}-1}\}\to\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
is constructed so that
G(-,0)=F_{j}
and
G(-,3^{q_{j}})
is a constant map. A vertex
v
of
C
is fixed. To define
G
,
[
20
, Lemma 2.8]
is used to find a family of “small” chains
\tau:\partial C(q_{j})^{0}\to\mathcal{I}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{}}(M,N)
such that
\partial\tau(x)=f(v)-F_{j}(x)
and
\mathbf{M}(\tau(x))\leq c_{2}(n,M,\delta)\varepsilon
. Some coarea family
\{U_{1},\dotsc,U_{N}\}
for
N\leq 3^{q_{j}-1}
is found for
\tau
, which are defined from generalized balls of diameter at most
\delta
. Apply
lemma

A.8
to
\{F_{j}(x),\tau(x)\}_{x\in\partial C(q_{j})^{0}}
(and
\lambda_{i}
) to replace those generalized balls with new ones, to get some new family which we also call
\{U_{i}\}
, and which satisfies
eqs.

3.5
and
3.6
for
F_{j}(x)
and
\tau
instead of
F(x)
. For each
U_{i}
, let
y_{i}
be a vertex
y
of
\partial C(q_{j})
that minimizes
\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(y)\mathbin{\llcorner}U_{i})
. Then it follows from
[
20
, (14)]
that
G
has the following formula,

\displaystyle G(x,t)

\displaystyle=f(v)-\partial\left(\tau(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\bigcup_{i=1}^{N-t}U_{i}+\sum_{i=N-t+1}^{N}\tau(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}U_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=F_{j}(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\bigcup_{i=1}^{N-t}U_{i}+\sum_{i=N-t+1}^{N}F_{j}(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}U_{i}-\underbrace{\tau(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial\bigcup_{i=1}^{N-t}U_{i}-\sum_{i=N-t+1}^{N}\tau(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{i}}_{B}.

By
[
20
, (5)]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(B)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2N\#(\partial C(q_{j})^{0})}{r_{0}}\max\{\mathbf{M}(\tau(x)),\mathbf{M}(\tau(y_{1}),\dotsc,\mathbf{M}(\tau(y_{N}))\}

\displaystyle\leq c(n,M,\delta)\varepsilon,

which gives
eq.

3.4
.

Let us prove that
F_{j}
satisfies
eqs.

3.5
and
3.6
. Suppose that
F_{j}(z)=G(x,t)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(z)\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{l})\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{l})+\sum_{i=N-t+1}^{N}\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{l})

\displaystyle+{}

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\tau(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{l})+\sum_{i=N-t+1}^{N}\mathbf{M}(\tau(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}\partial U_{l}),

and it suffices to recall that
\mathbf{M}(\tau(x))\leq c_{2}\varepsilon
and note that when
x
is a vertex of
\partial C(q_{j})
, the
F_{j}(x)
’s and
\tau(x)
’s satisfy
eqs.

3.5
and
3.6
.

To prove that
F_{j}
satisfies (
2
), for any
F_{j}(z)=G(x,t)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(z))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{M}\left(F_{j}(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\bigcup_{i=1}^{N-1}U_{i}\right)+\sum_{i=N-t+1}^{N}\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(y_{i})\mathbin{\llcorner}U_{i})+\mathbf{M}(B)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{M}\left(F_{j}(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}\bigcup_{i=1}^{N-1}U_{i}\right)+\sum_{i=N-t+1}^{N}\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(x)\mathbin{\llcorner}U_{i})+c\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{M}(F_{j}(x))+c\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in X^{j-1}(q_{j-1})^{0}}\mathbf{M}(F_{j-1}(x))+c\varepsilon

\displaystyle(\text{induction hypothesis})

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in X^{0}}\mathbf{M}(f(x))+c\varepsilon.

After defining
F
over
\tilde{X}^{0}
as above, let us show that it satisfies (
1
). Consider any cell
C
of
X
, and vertex
v
of
C
, and any
C\cap\tilde{X}^{0}
. If
x
is also a vertex of
C
, then the property holds because
f
is
\varepsilon
-fine. Otherwise, let
C
be the cell of lowest dimension in
X
that contains
x
. Then
F(x)=G(x^{\prime},t)
is
f(v^{\prime})
minus the boundary minus a chain of mass at most
Nc_{2}\varepsilon\leq c\varepsilon
, where
v^{\prime}
is a vertex of
C
. The property holds because
\mathcal{F}(f(v),f(v^{\prime}))<\varepsilon
.
∎

A.4
The gluing chain homomorphism

\PropPiecewiseSmoothGluing

Proof.

We will prove the composition law, and the others follow from elementary geometric constructions. For any
x\in L
,
\Phi^{c}\circ\Psi^{0}(x)=\Phi^{c}(g(x))
. In addition, every
\Phi^{l+c}\circ\Psi^{l}
is continuous. And we have
\partial\circ\Phi^{l+c}\circ\Psi^{l}=\Phi^{l+c-1}\circ\partial\circ\Psi^{l}=\Phi^{l+c-1}\circ\Psi^{l-1}\circ\partial
.
∎

Appendix B
Topological Lemmas about Lens Spaces

B.1
The conventional choice of cohomology generators for lens spaces

In this paper we consider all cohomology to be singular cohomology. On the other hand, for spaces like
E\mathbb{Z}_{p}
it is convenient to define classes using cellular cohomology, which only makes sense after we have chosen a certain isomorphism
C_{n}^{cell}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}(X^{n},X^{n-1})
, from which the cellular boundary maps are defined, and which induces an isomorphism
C^{n}_{cell}(X;G)=Hom(C_{n}^{cell}(X),G)\xrightarrow{\cong}Hom(H_{n}(X^{n},X^{n-1}),G)\cong H^{n}(X^{n},X^{n-1};G)
. (The last isomorphism comes from the Universal coefficient theorem.)
